Table 1
The Primate Prefrontal Cortex: Comparative Framework, Anatomy, and Methods
1. Q: What fundamental function does this book propose for the primate prefrontal cortex, and what makes the proposal distinctive?
A: Show answer
The book proposes that the primate prefrontal (PF) cortex uses information about the current behavioural context to generate goals in accordance with current biological needs, and that it can do this on the basis of single events. The proposal is not simply that PF cortex stores information or supervises behaviour in a broad sense; it identifies a specific relationship between context, need, and goal generation. The chapter presents this as a hypothesis to be explained through anatomy, physiology, behaviour, comparative biology, and tests that could potentially disconfirm it.
Study instruction:
- Restate the proposed function in your own words, then identify the three elements it links: current context, biological need, and goal generation.
Key point:
- The proposed role is context-sensitive goal generation shaped by current biological needs.
- A useful theory must explain both how this role is implemented and why PF cortex is suited to it.
2. Q: Why do the authors adopt a comparative approach to understanding prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
A comparative approach places the primate PF cortex in the evolutionary and functional context of other mammals and primates. It asks which cortical components are shared, which appeared later in primate evolution, and what behavioural advantages those changes may confer. This prevents a theory from treating a function as uniquely dependent on a primate-specific PF subdivision when other species can perform comparable behaviour using different neural arrangements. The authors argue that evolutionary history can therefore reveal functional distinctions that studies of the human or macaque brain alone might miss.
Study instruction:
- Compare a purely within-species explanation with a comparative explanation, and note what each can reveal about homology and specialization.
Key point:
- Similar behaviour across species does not require identical cortical structures.
- Evolutionary comparisons help distinguish ancient capacities from later primate specializations.
3. Q: What are Tinbergen's four questions, and why are all four relevant to a biological theory of PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Tinbergen's framework asks how a biological system works (mechanism), how it develops during an individual's life (ontogeny), how it evolved (phylogeny), and how it contributes to survival or reproductive success (selection or adaptive value). The chapter notes that PF research has often emphasized mechanism and development while giving less attention to evolution and fitness. The authors argue that all four levels matter because the present organization of PF cortex reflects both immediate neural operations and the historical processes that produced its structure and behavioural advantages. These questions complement rather than replace one another.
Study instruction:
- Create a four-column note with one possible PF-cortex research question under mechanism, ontogeny, phylogeny, and selection.
Key point:
- Mechanism, development, evolution, and adaptive value are distinct but complementary explanatory levels.
- A complete account should not treat current neural operations as the whole biological explanation.
4. Q: How does the chapter distinguish proximate explanations from evolutionary explanations?
A: Show answer
A proximate explanation describes the mechanisms operating now, such as neural inputs, cell activity, and behavioural outputs. An evolutionary explanation asks how ancestral variation and selection produced the system and why its present properties were retained. The chapter illustrates the distinction with the heart-rate response to danger: autonomic and muscular mechanisms explain how the rate changes, while evolutionary history helps explain why the system has that organization. Applied to PF cortex, the distinction prevents researchers from confusing a description of neural activity with an account of why the cortex evolved its distinctive connections and functions.
Study instruction:
- For one PF-cortex finding, write one mechanism-level explanation and one evolutionary-level question without treating them as interchangeable.
Key point:
- Mechanism explains present operation; phylogeny and selection explain historical origin and adaptive significance.
- Both levels contribute to a biological explanation.
5. Q: What five aims organize the book's investigation of primate PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The authors aim to explain the evolution of primate PF cortex and the advantages it brings; show how its connections allow it to do what other cortical areas cannot; propose a fundamental function for PF cortex as a whole; explain human imaging activations during complex cognitive tasks; and compare their proposal with competing theories while identifying ways to test it. These aims create a chain of explanation from comparative anatomy through connectional specialization and whole-system function to human cognition and falsifiability. The chapter uses this structure to show why no single method or narrow task can settle the question.
Study instruction:
- Memorize the five aims as a sequence, then explain how the evolutionary and connectional aims support the whole-cortex proposal.
Key point:
- The book links evolution, connectivity, fundamental function, human imaging, and theory testing.
- The aims are intended to form one integrated argument rather than five unrelated topics.
6. Q: Why do the authors argue that working memory cannot by itself be the defining function of primate PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges the claim that PF cortex is fundamentally a working-memory system by noting that non-primate mammals can perform tasks requiring retention and monitoring of recent events. Rats can learn the radial-arm maze, in which they must avoid revisiting arms already visited to collect food efficiently, despite lacking the primate-specific PF subdivisions that some working-memory accounts emphasize. This does not show that PF cortex is uninvolved in primate working memory; rather, it shows that working memory-like capacities cannot be defined as abilities that only primate PF cortex makes possible. A comprehensive theory must explain both shared capacities and primate-specific neural contributions.
Study instruction:
- Describe the radial-arm maze rule and state precisely what the rat result does and does not establish about PF cortex.
Key point:
- Working-memory-like behaviour exists in species without the same primate PF subdivisions.
- The theory must explain specialization without denying capacities supported by other neural systems.
7. Q: What is the generality problem in theories of PF cortex?
A: Show answer
A theory may appear persuasive when judged only against the tasks that originally inspired it, yet fail when applied to a broader range of behaviour. The chapter argues that theories based heavily on delayed-response or delayed-alternation tasks risk mistaking one important task demand for the fundamental function of the entire PF cortex. A strong theory should account for diverse lesion effects, cell-activity patterns, and imaging results, including findings from tasks that were not used to construct the theory. This broad-coverage requirement is later called the generality test.
Study instruction:
- Choose a proposed PF function and list at least three different task families that a theory based on it should also explain.
Key point:
- A theory should generalize beyond the small set of experiments that motivated it.
- Task diversity is necessary to identify a fundamental function rather than a task-specific demand.
8. Q: Why must a theory explain how PF cortex differs from posterior parietal and other cortical areas?
A: Show answer
Many cognitive functions depend on distributed networks, so identifying PF activation or connectivity within a network does not establish what PF cortex contributes uniquely. The chapter criticizes accounts that assign similar broad functions to PF and posterior parietal cortex without explaining why lesions in the two regions can have different behavioural consequences. A theory must identify the specific information or computation contributed by PF cortex relative to its network partners. Otherwise, it describes a network-level capacity but does not explain the distinctive role of one component.
Study instruction:
- For a distributed cognitive task, distinguish the network's overall function from the specific contribution that a PF region might make.
Key point:
- Network participation is not the same as unique functional responsibility.
- A PF theory must explain meaningful differences between connected cortical areas.
9. Q: Why do the authors insist that a theory of PF cortex must be testable and potentially refutable?
A: Show answer
Broad labels such as executive function can be difficult to falsify because almost any complex behaviour can be said to involve executive control. The authors argue that a useful theory should make specific predictions about what PF cortex does and where else in the brain that function can or cannot be performed. Their proposal would be challenged if another brain region were shown to perform the same proposed function independently, or if PF cortex did not perform it under appropriate tests. Testability converts a general description into a scientific account that can be evaluated against evidence.
Study instruction:
- Write one observation that would support the proposal and one observation that would count against it.
Key point:
- A scientific theory needs possible disconfirming observations.
- Specific functional claims are more testable than unrestricted labels such as executive function.
10. Q: How does the chapter define PF cortex in relation to the frontal lobe?
A: Show answer
The authors use PF cortex to refer to a particular set of cortical regions and distinguish it from the frontal lobe as a whole. The frontal lobe also contains primary motor and premotor areas, which should not automatically be included when discussing PF function. The chapter quotes earlier criticism of loose terminology to emphasize that statements such as 'frontal lobe removed' can be misleading when only anterior portions or selected PF regions were affected. Precise anatomical language is essential because behavioural effects depend on which tissue was actually damaged.
Study instruction:
- Sketch the frontal lobe and label PF, premotor, and primary motor territories separately; explain why the distinction matters when interpreting a lesion.
Key point:
- PF cortex is not synonymous with the entire frontal lobe.
- Anatomical precision is a prerequisite for interpreting lesion and activation findings.
11. Q: What is the difference between granular, agranular, and dysgranular cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter classifies cortical types partly by the prominence of the internal granular layer, layer 4. Granular cortex has a conspicuous layer 4; agranular cortex has very few cells concentrated into that layer or nearly lacks it; dysgranular cortex has a thin, sometimes discontinuous layer 4 and is intermediate. The authors use granular versus agranular as a helpful simplification while acknowledging that cortical architecture varies along a continuum. Much of primate PF cortex is granular, but the book also includes selected agranular orbital and medial regions in its definition.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simplified cortical column for each type and annotate the relative prominence of layer 4.
Key point:
- Layer 4 prominence is the key architectural distinction used in this chapter.
- Granular and agranular are useful categories, not an exhaustive description of every transitional pattern.
12. Q: Why does the book combine several cortical area naming systems rather than use one uniform atlas?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes how von Bonin and Bailey, Brodmann, Walker, and later anatomists developed different maps and labels. Their schemes do not always align because investigators used different criteria, sampling, and interpretations of cortical borders. The authors therefore adopt an eclectic nomenclature, using the label that best serves each discussion rather than pretending that all systems are interchangeable. A Brodmann number used today may not correspond exactly to the area Brodmann originally described, so the name alone is not proof of anatomical identity.
Study instruction:
- Select two area labels used in the chapter and trace how the relevant naming conventions arose and where their equivalence is approximate.
Key point:
- Area names are conventions tied to particular maps and historical definitions.
- A shared label does not guarantee identical boundaries or functional meaning.
13. Q: Why do neuroanatomists disagree about the subdivisions and borders of PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Different investigators may detect different structural transitions, especially in regions without clear sensory or motor topographic maps. PF cortex lacks the kind of well-defined receptive-field maps that help delineate visual, auditory, and somatosensory areas. Anatomists consequently rely heavily on architectonic features, and judgments about where a border begins or ends can vary across observers and mapping schemes. The chapter also notes that different studies have recognized substantially different numbers of orbital and medial subdivisions.
Study instruction:
- Compare at least two PF maps mentioned in the chapter and identify the criteria or surface regions on which their subdivisions differ.
Key point:
- PF borders are difficult to define because there is no single decisive topographic map.
- Differences among maps partly reflect methodological and interpretive choices.
14. Q: What are architectonics, cytoarchitectonics, and myeloarchitectonics?
A: Show answer
Architectonics is the study of cortical areas through structural patterns. Cytoarchitectonics emphasizes the distribution, density, and arrangement of cell bodies across cortical layers, whereas myeloarchitectonics emphasizes patterns of myelinated fibres. These methods can reveal transitions between cortical fields, but the chapter characterizes the practice as demanding and inexact because it requires recognition of complex, high-dimensional patterns. Architectonic evidence is therefore informative but should not be mistaken for an infallible or direct readout of function.
Study instruction:
- Define each term and identify which tissue feature an investigator would examine when using it.
Key point:
- Architectonic maps infer cortical fields from structural organization.
- Structural boundaries are evidence about anatomy, not automatic proof of functional boundaries.
15. Q: How does the observer-independent method described by Schleicher and colleagues attempt to identify cortical borders?
A: Show answer
The method quantifies optical density across cortical layers rather than relying solely on a human observer's visual judgment. Cell density and staining patterns vary from superficial layer 1 toward deep layer 6; measurements taken in strips along the cortex can be analyzed statistically to detect significant changes between neighbouring fields. The chapter presents this as a promising route toward more objective and reproducible maps, while stressing that a complete observer-independent map was not yet available at the time discussed. Objectivity in border detection does not, by itself, establish what a border means functionally.
Study instruction:
- Explain the sequence from tissue measurement to statistical border detection, and list one advantage and one remaining limitation.
Key point:
- Quantified optical-density profiles can reduce reliance on subjective visual border judgments.
- More reproducible anatomy does not automatically yield a functional parcellation.
16. Q: Why might a cytoarchitectonic border fail to mark a meaningful functional subdivision?
A: Show answer
A structural difference can arise for reasons that do not correspond to a new computational role. The chapter gives the primary motor cortex as an example: medial and lateral portions differ in cytoarchitecture, including cell-body size, partly because neurons controlling the leg project farther down the spinal cord. That anatomical difference reflects projection distance and body representation rather than necessarily indicating a distinct fundamental function. The same caution applies when interpreting structural variation within PF cortex.
Study instruction:
- Use the motor-cortex example to explain why an anatomical boundary should be tested against connectivity, physiology, and behaviour.
Key point:
- Anatomical differentiation and functional specialization are related but not identical.
- A border should not be assigned a functional interpretation from cytoarchitecture alone.
17. Q: Why do the authors prefer broad descriptive regions for PF cortex in this book?
A: Show answer
Because published maps disagree about exact borders and the functional significance of subdivisions remains uncertain, the authors use general descriptive terms that are not tied to one particular atlas. Their scheme identifies medial, orbital, caudal, dorsal, and ventral PF regions and is intended to remain broadly compatible with several conventional maps. This choice reduces the risk that an argument depends on a disputed boundary or nomenclature. It does not deny that finer subdivisions may exist; it is a practical convention for comparing evidence across studies.
Study instruction:
- Locate the five descriptive regions on a macaque brain diagram and compare them with at least one detailed architectonic map.
Key point:
- Broad regional labels improve cross-study communication when fine borders are disputed.
- Descriptive groupings are a pragmatic framework, not a claim that PF cortex is anatomically uniform.
18. Q: Why is homology between human and macaque cortical areas more than a matter of using the same name?
A: Show answer
Homology means that two structures are inherited from a common ancestral structure, not merely that researchers assign them the same label or place them in similar positions. The chapter warns that applying a common name to human and macaque regions does not prove that they are homologous. Establishing homology requires comparative evidence about evolutionary relationships and relevant anatomical organization. The authors defer a fuller treatment to the book's comparative chapter because functional claims across species depend on getting this relationship right.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish homology from analogy and from simple nomenclatural correspondence; give one reason why each distinction matters in comparative neuroscience.
Key point:
- A common cortical label is not sufficient evidence of evolutionary homology.
- Cross-species functional comparisons depend on defensible anatomical correspondences.
19. Q: What is a connectional fingerprint, and what information does it provide about a cortical area?
A: Show answer
A connectional fingerprint is the overall pattern of inputs and outputs linking a cortical area with other brain regions. The chapter adapts the fingerprint metaphor to emphasize that an area's identity is captured by a combination of connection strengths and targets rather than one isolated projection. Because connections determine what information reaches an area and where its outputs can influence processing, the fingerprint constrains the functions that area can perform. It also provides a basis for comparing neighbouring or network-linked areas.
Study instruction:
- Choose one PF region and list its major inputs and outputs from the chapter's connectional discussion; explain how the pattern could constrain function.
Key point:
- The whole pattern of connections is more informative than a single projection.
- Connectivity places functional constraints on what a cortical area can receive and influence.
Source links:
- Cocomac macaque cortical connection database
20. Q: How does the chapter use the Cocomac database to study connectional fingerprints?
A: Show answer
Cocomac is described as a database of macaque cortical connections that aggregates findings from many anatomical studies. The chapter reports that the version discussed contained data from 413 studies and 39,748 connectional entries. Passingham and colleagues used these data to chart the connections of PF areas, represent connection strengths in polar plots, and compare patterns across regions. The scale of the database makes it possible to analyze an area's connections as a profile rather than relying on a few selected pathways, although the conclusions remain dependent on the coverage and quality of the underlying studies.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a connection database can turn individual anatomical tracing results into a comparative profile, and identify one source of possible bias in a literature-derived database.
Key point:
- Cocomac supports systematic comparison of macaque cortical connectivity.
- A large database improves coverage but does not remove limitations in the underlying evidence.
Source links:
- Cocomac macaque cortical connection database
21. Q: What do multidimensional scaling and hierarchical cluster analysis contribute to the study of PF connectivity?
A: Show answer
Multidimensional scaling represents similarity and difference among connectional patterns in a lower-dimensional space, making it easier to see how areas relate to one another. Hierarchical cluster analysis groups areas according to similarity in their connection profiles. The chapter reports that such analyses found no two PF areas with exactly identical connection patterns while still identifying clusters of regions with related connectivity. These methods therefore support both the uniqueness of individual area fingerprints and the existence of broader connectional groupings.
Study instruction:
- Describe what the distance between two areas would mean in a connection-based multidimensional plot and what a cluster represents.
Key point:
- Areas can be individually distinctive while belonging to larger connectivity-based clusters.
- Statistical grouping summarizes connectional similarity; it does not alone establish functional identity.
Source links:
- Cocomac macaque cortical connection database
22. Q: Which five broad PF regions does the book use, and how are they related to wider cortical networks?
A: Show answer
The book organizes its later regional chapters around medial, orbital, caudal, dorsal, and ventral PF cortex. Each region is treated as part of a wider network that can include premotor, parietal, temporal, and hippocampal cortex. The scheme combines connection-based clustering with anatomical location and resembles a framework discussed by Price and Drevets. The point is not that each region acts independently, but that a network account must still explain the distinctive contribution of each PF component.
Study instruction:
- Label the five regions on a brain diagram and draw at least one network partner for each; avoid treating the network as a single undifferentiated unit.
Key point:
- The book uses five broad PF regions as an organizing framework.
- Each region participates in a wider network but retains a distinctive connectional profile.
Source links:
- Cocomac macaque cortical connection database
23. Q: Why is it insufficient to say that a PF region belongs to a distributed network?
A: Show answer
Distributed processing explains why multiple areas can be active and interconnected during a task, but it does not specify what each area contributes. The chapter uses the mid-lateral PF cortex and posterior parietal cortex as an example: the regions share many connections and show similar delay-related cell activity, yet lesions can have sharply different effects on delayed alternation. A useful theory must therefore compare their complete connectional fingerprints and explain why one region is necessary under conditions in which the other is not. Network membership is a starting point, not a complete functional explanation.
Study instruction:
- For one network discussed in neuroscience, write separate statements for the network-level capacity and the proposed contribution of each node.
Key point:
- A network description must be supplemented by an account of component-specific roles.
- Shared activity and connectivity do not imply interchangeable functions.
24. Q: What does the delayed-alternation lesion comparison reveal about mid-lateral PF cortex and posterior parietal cortex?
A: Show answer
In the classical delayed-alternation task, a monkey must alternate its choice between left and right food wells across successive trials. The chapter reports that lesions of mid-lateral PF cortex, particularly area 46, impair relearning the task, whereas posterior parietal lesions in the cited comparison do not. This difference is important because the two regions are connected and can show sustained activity during delay periods. The behavioural dissociation indicates that similar task-related activity does not establish that the regions make the same necessary contribution.
Study instruction:
- Write the alternating response rule and explain why comparing lesions in two connected regions is more informative than observing activation in only one.
Key point:
- Area 46 and posterior parietal cortex can show related activity yet have different lesion consequences.
- Necessity must be assessed behaviourally, not inferred from activity alone.
25. Q: What is a physiological fingerprint in the framework of this chapter?
A: Show answer
A physiological fingerprint is a comparative profile of the kinds of cell activity found in an area. The chapter describes profiles based on properties such as auditory or visual responses, somatosensory or proprioceptive responses, activity resembling muscle-related patterns, temporal correlations with movement, and persistent delay-period activity. These properties can be represented as proportions or distributions across cell classes. Comparing such profiles across areas can reveal differences that are obscured when researchers describe a region with a single broad label such as 'active during movement'.
Study instruction:
- List the physiological dimensions used in the chapter and explain why a profile across several dimensions is more informative than one response category.
Key point:
- Physiological fingerprints compare multiple properties of neuronal responses.
- A region's functional profile should not be reduced to one task-related response.
26. Q: What does the comparison of SMA and ventral premotor cortex show about visually guided and memory-guided movement sequences?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes cells classified by their preference for visually guided versus memory-guided movement sequences. Cells at one end of the scale were specific to visual guidance, those at the other end were specific to memory guidance, and intermediate classes responded under both conditions. The SMA showed a relative preponderance of activity for memory-guided sequences, whereas lateral premotor cortex showed the opposite bias. This illustrates how a physiological fingerprint can express a graded distribution of response preferences rather than an all-or-none division between areas.
Study instruction:
- Reproduce the seven-class logic in words and explain what a class near the midpoint indicates about a cell's response preference.
Key point:
- SMA and lateral premotor cortex differ in the distribution of guidance-related cell preferences.
- Intermediate response classes matter; regional specialization is often relative rather than absolute.
27. Q: What are behavioural fingerprints, and why are they needed alongside connectional and physiological fingerprints?
A: Show answer
Behavioural fingerprints compare the effects of lesions across a range of tasks. They are needed because anatomical connections and cell responses describe what information an area receives or processes, but do not by themselves establish what behaviour depends on that area. The chapter argues that a broad task comparison can reveal a distinctive pattern of impairments and spared abilities. Together, connectional, physiological, and behavioural fingerprints provide complementary evidence about an area's constraints, operations, and necessary contribution.
Study instruction:
- For one PF region, make a table with three columns: connectional properties, cell-activity properties, and lesion effects across tasks.
Key point:
- Behavioural fingerprints are comparative profiles of lesion effects.
- The three fingerprint types answer related but non-identical questions.
28. Q: Why can an excessive focus on delayed-response and delayed-alternation tasks distort theories of PF function?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that a large literature had concentrated on a small number of delay tasks, encouraging the conclusion that PF cortex functions primarily or exclusively in working memory. Those tasks are important and PF lesions can cause severe, lasting impairments in them, but their demands do not represent the full range of PF-dependent behaviour. The authors use the analogy of inferring that all ripe apples are green after examining only Granny Smith apples. The problem is not that the observed task effect is false; it is that the inference from a narrow sample to a fundamental function is too broad.
Study instruction:
- Identify what the delayed-response task measures, then name at least three other behavioural domains that a comprehensive PF theory should consider.
Key point:
- A robust impairment on one task does not establish that the task demand is the cortex's fundamental function.
- Broad behavioural sampling reduces the risk of overgeneralization.
29. Q: What is the generality test proposed for evaluating theories of PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The generality test asks whether a theory can account for findings beyond the narrow class of tasks from which it was derived. A working-memory account, for example, should be evaluated against a broad set of lesion effects, physiological findings, and human imaging results rather than only delay tasks. The chapter treats this as a safeguard against theories that explain their motivating data but fail to cover the broader behavioural and neural evidence. Passing the test requires explanatory breadth without turning the theory into an unfalsifiable label.
Study instruction:
- Take a theory from the PF literature and assess whether it explains at least three findings that were not central to its original formulation.
Key point:
- A fundamental theory must explain a wide range of relevant findings.
- Generality is not permission to become vague; breadth and falsifiability must be retained.
30. Q: Why does an imaging activation not prove that an area is necessary for a task?
A: Show answer
An activation shows that a region's signal changes during task performance, but the task may still be performed without that region. Other parts of the brain may be sufficient, or the activated area may provide information that is useful but not indispensable. The chapter therefore distinguishes evidence of participation from evidence of necessity. Lesion or temporary-inactivation experiments are needed to test whether performance is impaired when the area is unavailable, while recognizing that such interventions also have their own interpretive limitations.
Study instruction:
- For an imaging result, write two alternative interpretations: one in which the activated region is necessary and one in which it is engaged but dispensable.
Key point:
- Activation is evidence of task-related processing, not proof of necessity.
- Causal claims require evidence about what happens when the region is disrupted.
31. Q: How does the semantic-categorization example combine imaging and lesion evidence?
A: Show answer
In the example discussed from Price and colleagues, participants selected an item associated with a target picture, such as choosing a wrench rather than a saw for pliers. Imaging showed activation in temporal regions and left ventral PF cortex. A patient with a complete lesion of left ventral PF cortex could nevertheless perform the task, and the remaining PF cortex showed no activation. The authors infer that ventral PF cortex was not necessary for this task under those conditions and that temporal cortex was sufficient to support performance. The example demonstrates why activation and lesion evidence answer different questions.
Study instruction:
- Explain what the imaging result established and what the patient's lesion result added; avoid claiming that the PF region is never involved in semantic processing.
Key point:
- The lesion case tested necessity where imaging alone could show only engagement.
- A spared task after a focal lesion supports sufficiency elsewhere for that particular task and condition.
32. Q: What are the strengths and limitations of repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation as a temporary lesion method?
A: Show answer
Repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation can temporarily disrupt cortical processing and thus provide a causal complement to imaging without requiring a naturally occurring patient lesion. However, the chapter notes that much human cortex lies deep within sulci and may not be reached selectively. The precise anatomical boundaries of the stimulation effect are also uncertain. rTMS can therefore support causal inference, but it does not provide the same anatomical precision as a carefully assessed focal lesion in an experimental animal.
Study instruction:
- Compare rTMS with permanent lesions on reversibility, anatomical selectivity, and certainty about the tissue affected.
Key point:
- Temporary disruption is useful but spatial selectivity is limited.
- The location and extent of the stimulation effect must be treated cautiously.
33. Q: Why do the authors rely heavily on selective cortical lesions in monkeys?
A: Show answer
Experimental monkey studies can use surgical methods to remove selected cortical tissue while leaving underlying white matter relatively intact. Investigators can target particular areas, often using sulcal landmarks, and later assess lesion boundaries with reasonable accuracy. Areas can also be inactivated temporarily. These methods provide greater anatomical control than is commonly available in human patients, although monkey findings still require careful interpretation and comparison with human evidence.
Study instruction:
- List the design features that make an experimental lesion more anatomically informative, including tissue selectivity and post-operative verification.
Key point:
- Selective monkey lesions can isolate cortical contributions more precisely than many clinical lesions.
- Anatomical precision improves inference but does not eliminate behavioural or network-level confounds.
34. Q: Why can matching and nonmatching tasks be difficult to interpret as pure recognition-memory tests?
A: Show answer
In delayed matching-to-sample, a subject sees a sample and later chooses the matching item; in delayed nonmatching-to-sample, the correct choice is the alternative item. Impaired performance after a PF lesion could reflect loss of the sample, difficulty judging sameness or difference, or loss of knowledge about the task rule. Task names can mislead because animals may solve the same nominal task through recognition, familiarity, recency, or another strategy. Human patients can sometimes clarify whether the rule is still understood, but animal studies also need control conditions that distinguish alternative strategies.
Study instruction:
- Design one control that would help separate memory for the sample from knowledge of the matching or nonmatching rule.
Key point:
- Task labels do not guarantee that subjects use the cognitive strategy implied by the label.
- Lesion interpretation requires analysis of the rules and strategies available to the subject.
35. Q: What is a double dissociation, and why is it considered strong evidence for functional specialization?
A: Show answer
A double dissociation occurs when a lesion in area A impairs task X but not task Y, while a lesion in area B impairs task Y but not task X. The chapter illustrates this logic with ventral and orbital PF cortex: ventral lesions impair a strategy task in the cited comparison, whereas orbital lesions do not; orbital lesions impair a devaluation task, whereas ventral lesions do not. This pattern is stronger evidence for differentiated contributions than a single impairment because it shows that the two regions are not simply producing a general reduction in task performance. It still requires careful task analysis and lesion verification.
Study instruction:
- Draw a two-by-two matrix of lesion site and task outcome, then explain why the crossed pattern supports specialization.
Key point:
- A double dissociation demonstrates complementary patterns of impairment and sparing.
- The inference is strongest when lesions and task demands are well characterized.
36. Q: Why does the chapter caution that double dissociations are less common in PF research than is sometimes assumed?
A: Show answer
Although double dissociations provide powerful evidence, the chapter reports that they are not as frequent in the PF literature as broad claims of functional modularity might suggest. It also notes evidence that manipulations affecting PF cortex as a whole can produce much larger effects than manipulations restricted to particular parts. These observations are compatible with specialized regional contributions operating within an integrated system. They caution against assuming that every PF subdivision maps neatly onto one independent cognitive faculty.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish evidence for regional specialization from evidence for system-wide integration, and identify what kind of experiment could inform both.
Key point:
- Regional differentiation can coexist with whole-system dependence.
- The scarcity of clear double dissociations limits overly modular interpretations.
37. Q: Why can lesion effects and cell-recording results appear to disagree?
A: Show answer
Cell recording shows activity in neurons during behaviour, whereas a lesion reveals what happens when an area and its contributions to the wider network are removed or disrupted. An area may contain cells that encode information relevant to a task without being necessary for successful performance, because another region can provide the required computation. Conversely, removing an area can disrupt inputs to other regions and thereby change their activity and capabilities. The two methods therefore measure different aspects of the system and should be interpreted together rather than treated as competing verdicts.
Study instruction:
- For one example in the chapter, state what the cell recordings showed and what the lesion experiment showed, then explain why the results need not conflict.
Key point:
- Neural correlates of behaviour and necessary causal contributions are different kinds of evidence.
- Lesions alter network interactions as well as local processing.
38. Q: How does the posterior parietal delay-activity example illustrate the difference between neural correlates and necessity?
A: Show answer
Cells in both mid-lateral PF cortex and posterior parietal cortex can show sustained activity during a delay when information must be retained for a later response. Yet the chapter reports that lesions of mid-lateral PF cortex impair delayed alternation, whereas large posterior parietal lesions in the cited work do not. The sustained firing in posterior parietal cortex is therefore a neural correlate of task performance but does not, in that task, establish a necessary contribution. The comparison shows why cell activity must be evaluated alongside lesion effects and alternative sources of information.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the delay-period finding and lesion outcome for each region in two separate sentences, then state the inferential difference.
Key point:
- Delay-period activity does not automatically mean that a region is indispensable for working-memory performance.
- Causal necessity depends on the effect of disrupting the region.
39. Q: How can a lesion change the function of another area even when that other area contains task-responsive neurons?
A: Show answer
A lesion removes more than the local computational resources of the damaged tissue: it also removes the area's outputs and can deprive connected regions of inputs that normally shape their activity. The chapter uses medial and lateral premotor regions to illustrate this point. Some lateral premotor cells respond during self-guided movement in intact animals, but if that response depends on input from medial premotor cortex, those cells may lose the relevant properties after a medial lesion. The intact-network recording therefore cannot be used to assume that the remaining network can simply take over after disruption.
Study instruction:
- Draw a two-area circuit and mark how removal of one area's output could alter the response profile of neurons in the other area.
Key point:
- Compensation cannot be assumed from intact-brain response properties.
- Disconnection and loss of input can alter the capacities of spared regions.
40. Q: What does the conditional visuomotor task reveal about ventral and mid-lateral PF cortex?
A: Show answer
In the task described, different pictures instruct the monkey to make different responses, so the animal must use a picture-response mapping. Cells in both mid-lateral and ventral PF cortex can encode these mappings. However, lesions of ventral PF cortex cause a severe impairment, whereas mid-lateral PF lesions have only mild effects in the cited comparison. The chapter proposes that ventral PF damage also removes important inferior-temporal inputs to mid-lateral PF cortex, limiting the latter's ability to assume the function. This is another example of why activity in an intact region does not guarantee post-lesion compensation.
Study instruction:
- Explain the picture-response rule and identify the proposed network consequence of a ventral PF lesion.
Key point:
- Similar encoding in two areas does not imply equal necessity.
- Lesion effects depend on the connections that remain available to the network.
41. Q: What is intracortical microstimulation, and what can it contribute to understanding cortical function?
A: Show answer
Intracortical microstimulation applies electrical current through electrodes similar to those used for single-cell recording. It can synchronously activate at least hundreds of cells, providing a way to perturb local circuitry rather than merely observe naturally occurring activity. The chapter treats the method as informative on the assumption that the induced activity roughly mimics some aspects of normal neural activity. Interpretation is complicated because synchronous artificial activation is not the same as the distributed, patterned activity that occurs naturally.
Study instruction:
- Describe how microstimulation differs from single-cell recording and from a lesion, and state one assumption required when interpreting its effects.
Key point:
- Microstimulation is an intervention that drives local neural activity.
- Artificial synchronous activation must not be equated automatically with normal information processing.
42. Q: Why can the spatial peak of a BOLD signal be an imperfect indicator of the location of underlying neural processing?
A: Show answer
BOLD is a vascular signal, not a direct measurement of neuronal firing. Its spatial pattern can be influenced by major arteries, veins, and venous outflow, so the location of the strongest signal may correspond only roughly to the site of the underlying neural events. The chapter cites this as one reason that localization from imaging coordinates requires caution. The signal remains useful for surveying the brain, but vascular contributions complicate fine-grained claims about which cortical field is active.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a vascular measurement can be displaced from its neural source and list the vascular structures identified as possible influences.
Key point:
- BOLD localization is constrained by vascular anatomy.
- A peak coordinate should not be treated as an exact border-level localization of neuronal processing.
Source links:
- MRIcro coordinate-visualization software described in the chapter
43. Q: Why does the chapter distinguish regional activation from cell activity?
A: Show answer
Cell activity refers to neuronal discharge measured directly by recording from individual cells, whereas regional activation in imaging refers to a hemodynamic signal associated with local neural processes. The chapter argues that BOLD is often more closely related to synaptic inputs and local field potentials than to the output spiking of individual neurons. Consequently, a change in activation can occur without a corresponding change in action-potential rate, and activation should not be translated automatically into a claim about what individual neurons are firing. The authors therefore use 'activity' and 'activation' as distinct terms.
Study instruction:
- Write a one-sentence operational definition of cell activity and one of imaging activation; avoid using the terms interchangeably.
Key point:
- Single-cell discharge and BOLD activation are different measurements.
- The relationship between synaptic input, local field potentials, and spiking is not one-to-one.
44. Q: How can BOLD activation change when single-cell firing does not?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses evidence that BOLD can be sensitive to modulatory synaptic influences, including inhibitory inputs or excitatory inputs that remain below the threshold for driving action potentials. In the generalized flash-suppression example, suppressive visual stimuli altered the BOLD signal in primary visual cortex without changing the recorded single-cell activity in the same way. This suggests that BOLD can reflect synaptic processing that does not appear as a change in spike rate. An increase in activation therefore cannot automatically be interpreted as increased neuronal output.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between synaptic input and action-potential output, then describe how one can change without the other.
Key point:
- BOLD can reflect local synaptic influences even when spiking is unchanged.
- Activation increases do not necessarily mean that neuronal output has increased.
45. Q: What does the cerebellar example contribute to the argument about BOLD and neuronal activity?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes evidence that BOLD in cerebellar cortex correlates closely with synaptic activation but not with the discharge of Purkinje cells, the principal output neurons discussed. Inputs carried by climbing and parallel fibres can drive local field potentials and oxygen consumption, while Purkinje-cell firing does not track the BOLD signal in a simple way. This example reinforces the broader point that hemodynamic signals can be more closely tied to synaptic input than to the output spikes of a selected neuronal population. It cautions against assuming a universal one-to-one mapping between BOLD and firing rate.
Study instruction:
- Identify the input signals, the recorded output neurons, and the measured hemodynamic response in the example; state what relationship was observed.
Key point:
- BOLD may track synaptic input rather than the discharge of output neurons.
- The mapping between imaging and neuronal signals depends on circuit physiology.
46. Q: Why is sparse coding especially important when interpreting imaging data from PF cortex?
A: Show answer
In a sparsely coded network, nearby neurons can have diverse response properties, so a small population may carry a specialized signal that is diluted in a regional imaging measure. Cell recording can identify the tuning of individual neurons and reveal intermixed populations that an imaging voxel may not distinguish. Conversely, imaging can survey a much larger portion of the brain and capture synchronized regional influences. The chapter warns that results from densely coded sensory or motor areas may not transfer straightforwardly to PF cortex, where neuronal populations can be more heterogeneous.
Study instruction:
- Contrast dense and sparse coding, then explain one strength of imaging and one strength of cell recording for each type of network.
Key point:
- Sparse coding makes it difficult to infer single-neuron properties from an averaged regional signal.
- Methodological conclusions from homogeneous sensory systems may not generalize to PF cortex.
47. Q: What are the main advantages of brain imaging over cell recording, and what are the complementary advantages of cell recording?
A: Show answer
Imaging can survey most of the brain simultaneously, locate regional signal peaks, and appear particularly sensitive to synchronized activations. Cell recording, by contrast, directly identifies what individual information-processing elements encode, can distinguish intermixed neuronal populations, and can separate increases from decreases in spiking activity. Imaging cannot by itself determine whether an activation increase reflects increased output or suppression of output. The chapter therefore treats the methods as complementary rather than ranking one as universally superior.
Study instruction:
- Construct a comparison table with the methods' spatial coverage, unit of measurement, sensitivity to sparse coding, and ability to distinguish excitation-related from suppression-related effects.
Key point:
- Imaging offers broad coverage; cell recording offers cellular specificity.
- Combining methods provides a more complete account than relying on either alone.
48. Q: Why does the chapter argue that task comparisons and meta-analysis are necessary for interpreting cell activity?
A: Show answer
Neuronal responses depend partly on training history and task design, so an activity pattern observed in one task may have several interpretations. In the spatial-delay example, cells selective for a location were initially interpreted as encoding remembered locations. When monkeys were trained to remember one location while attending to another, a later comparison found that 61 percent of location-selective PF cells encoded the attended location alone, only 16 percent encoded the remembered location alone, and the remainder had mixed properties. Comparing tasks changed the interpretation from a simple memory account to a more differentiated account involving attention and memory.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a delay-period response is ambiguous, then reproduce the 61 percent, 16 percent, and mixed-cell result and its implication.
Key point:
- A response observed in one task can reflect attention, memory, or mixed coding.
- Cross-task comparison is essential before assigning a cognitive label to neural activity.
49. Q: How does meta-analysis function as a physiological fingerprint in human imaging research?
A: Show answer
Meta-analysis combines imaging findings across multiple tasks in a common anatomical coordinate framework to identify patterns of regional activation. The chapter describes this as a physiological fingerprint by another name because it summarizes how a region is recruited across a broad range of conditions rather than relying on one experiment. Such synthesis can reveal recurring patterns and reduce the risk of interpreting one task as the defining function of a region. Its conclusions still depend on the quality, comparability, and selection of the studies included.
Study instruction:
- Describe what information is pooled in an imaging meta-analysis and identify two decisions that could affect the resulting activation profile.
Key point:
- Meta-analysis broadens the evidence base beyond an individual task.
- A pooled activation profile is informative but remains sensitive to study selection and methodological differences.
50. Q: How should researchers integrate connectional, physiological, behavioural, lesion, and imaging evidence when evaluating a PF theory?
A: Show answer
The chapter's overall method is triangulation: connectional fingerprints constrain what information an area can receive and influence; physiological fingerprints describe the activity patterns present; behavioural fingerprints compare lesion effects across tasks; lesion experiments test necessity; and imaging surveys regional engagement across the intact brain. No one method answers every question. A credible theory should explain converging findings while accounting for disagreements that arise because the methods measure different aspects of neural function. It should also remain open to results that contradict its predictions.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed PF function, formulate one question each for anatomy, cell physiology, behaviour, lesion causality, and imaging; then identify which method can answer each question.
Key point:
- Different methods provide complementary constraints on the same theory.
- Convergence is strongest when methodological limitations and apparent mismatches are explicitly explained.
Source links:
- Cocomac macaque cortical connection database
- MRIcro coordinate-visualization software described in the chapter
51. Q: What does Chapter 1 establish about the structure of the book and the evidence needed to evaluate its central proposal?
A: Show answer
The chapter positions Chapter 2 as the comparative and evolutionary analysis; Chapters 3–7 as regional accounts organized around major PF divisions and their connections; Chapter 8 as the proposal for PF cortex as a whole; Chapter 9 as the account of human imaging activations; and Chapter 10 as the comparison with competing theories and the development of tests. This structure reflects the authors' view that the fundamental function cannot be inferred from one task, one area, or one method. It must emerge from a broad synthesis of comparative anatomy, connectivity, physiology, behaviour, and evidence capable of challenging the proposal.
Study instruction:
- Use the chapter outline to map where each of the five book aims is addressed and identify which later chapters would test the claims most directly.
Key point:
- The book's argument progresses from evolution and regional connectivity to whole-cortex function, imaging, and falsifiability.
- Chapter 1 establishes the methodological standards against which later claims should be judged.
Rows: 51
Table 2
Evolution of the Primate Prefrontal Cortex: Comparative Anatomy, Ecological Adaptation, and Foraging
1. Q: What is the chapter’s central account of how the primate prefrontal (PF) cortex evolved?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents PF evolution as a sequence of additions associated with changing ecological demands, rather than as the enlargement of one uniform structure. It distinguishes an early mammalian phase, which produced agranular PF areas; an early primate phase, which added the first granular PF areas; and a later anthropoid phase, which added further granular regions as some lineages became larger, diurnal, and dependent on dispersed, seasonally variable resources. The authors propose that each phase supported different components of foraging: selecting actions and objects, searching for and valuing food, reaching and feeding in a fine-branch niche, and making more predictive choices under resource volatility. The anatomical comparisons are presented as evidence; the proposed selective advantages are evolutionary explanations that remain hypotheses to be evaluated.
Study instruction:
- Draw a three-stage timeline. For each stage, record the lineage, newly identified cortical features, ecological context, and proposed behavioral advantage. Mark which statements are anatomical observations and which are evolutionary interpretations.
Key point:
- PF evolution is treated as a sequence of partly distinct innovations, not a single expansion.
- The proposed function of each innovation must be distinguished from the evidence that an area exists.
2. Q: What does the chapter mean by prefrontal cortex, and why does it distinguish agranular from granular PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses PF cortex as a comparative anatomical term that includes frontal cortical areas with different evolutionary histories. It does not claim that nonprimate mammals lack a prefrontal cortex: rodents and other mammals possess agranular PF areas that the authors regard as homologous to corresponding agranular regions in primates. The key distinction is that primates possess additional granular PF areas with a developed layer 4 and a different set of anatomical relationships and connections. The authors argue that nonprimate mammals do not have a miniature copy or a functional amalgam of the entire primate PF cortex. This distinction matters because findings from a rodent agranular area cannot automatically be treated as evidence about a primate granular area merely because the regions share some physiological properties.
Study instruction:
- Create a two-column comparison in your notes for agranular and granular PF cortex, then list the evidence the chapter uses to distinguish them.
Key point:
- Nonprimate mammals have PF cortex, but the chapter argues that they lack the primate granular PF innovation.
- Anatomical and functional equivalence must not be assumed from a shared broad label.
3. Q: What are agranular and granular cortical architectures?
A: Show answer
Agranular cortex has little or no distinct layer 4, whereas granular cortex has a more conspicuous layer 4 with a higher density of cell bodies. The chapter also discusses weakly dysgranular regions, in which layer 4 is present but less developed than in strongly granular areas. It cautions against treating the presence or absence of a sharply bounded layer 4 as an all-or-none criterion. Quantitative work described in the chapter found a gradual caudal-to-rostral increase in layer 4 cell density along parts of the orbital and medial frontal surfaces. The authors therefore treat cytoarchitecture as a graded feature and argue that regions below an appropriate density threshold can be considered sufficiently agranular for their comparative purpose.
Study instruction:
- Sketch a simplified cortical cross-section showing layers and annotate what distinguishes agranular, dysgranular, and granular patterns. Explain why a graded measure is preferable to a rigid binary label in some regions.
Key point:
- Layer 4 density is a major architectural distinction, but it varies along a continuum.
- A weakly developed layer 4 does not by itself establish that a region is homologous to a granular primate PF area.
4. Q: What is a homologue in comparative neuroanatomy, and how does the chapter infer homology?
A: Show answer
Homologous areas are structures found in related species because they were inherited from a common ancestor. The chapter infers homology by comparing multiple diagnostic features, including cytoarchitecture, myeloarchitecture, position relative to neighboring areas, projection patterns, sensory inputs, and physiological outputs. It also uses phylogenetic distribution: a feature shared by strepsirrhines and anthropoids may have been present in their common primate ancestor, whereas a feature found in anthropoids but not in bushbabies may have evolved after those lineages diverged. This reasoning depends on assumptions about ancestral states and must allow for independent changes after lineages split. Similarity alone is not enough; a useful homology claim needs evidence that distinguishes the candidate areas from other regions that share the same general property.
Study instruction:
- For one proposed homology in the chapter, list at least three independent anatomical features that support it and identify any phylogenetic assumption involved.
Key point:
- Homology concerns inheritance from a common ancestor, not simply resemblance.
- Converging diagnostic evidence is stronger than a single shared physiological property.
5. Q: Why does the chapter reject the idea that rats have a miniature or compressed version of the primate PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that the rat frontal cortex lacks the granular PF areas that characterize primates and that the primate PF cortex contains differentiated areas not represented in rodents. A small rodent area may share some functions or cellular properties with a primate granular area, but that does not show that it is the same evolutionary structure. The authors also reject the claim that a few rodent areas somehow combine all the properties of several primate areas. Their concern is methodological as well as anatomical: treating nonhomologous regions as equivalent can lead researchers to use rodent findings to support claims about a primate region whose organization and connections differ. The chapter nevertheless recognizes the value of rodents for studying homologous agranular PF areas and for investigating general mechanisms shared across species.
Study instruction:
- Find an example in the chapter where rodent and primate regions share a task-related effect but differ in anatomical identity. Explain what conclusion is justified and what conclusion would overreach.
Key point:
- Functional resemblance does not establish anatomical homology.
- Cross-species comparisons should specify the exact cortical subdivision, not just the broad term PF cortex.
6. Q: What makes a feature diagnostic for identifying a cortical homologue?
A: Show answer
A diagnostic feature helps distinguish one candidate cortical area from other areas that could otherwise be confused with it. The chapter notes that valuation-related cell activity, a projection from the mediodorsal thalamus, dopaminergic input, or impairment on a delayed-response task cannot independently identify a granular PF homologue. These features occur in multiple frontal regions or in areas outside the PF cortex. For example, the mediodorsal thalamus projects broadly across granular and agranular frontal areas, and dopaminergic inputs also reach agranular, premotor, and posterior parietal regions. Likewise, similar lesion-related impairment can arise after damage to different regions. A feature becomes useful for homology when its distribution and organization distinguish the candidate area from alternatives, preferably in combination with independent anatomical evidence.
Study instruction:
- Make a table of the chapter’s rejected candidate diagnostic features. For each, write where else the feature occurs and why that weakens its value for homology.
Key point:
- A feature shared broadly across the brain has low diagnostic value for a specific cortical area.
- Lesion effects and neurochemical inputs need anatomical context before they can support homology.
7. Q: How does cortical topology help distinguish agranular from granular PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Topology refers to the relative spatial arrangement of cortical areas within the cortical sheet, conceptually unfolded to expose sulci and gyri. The chapter emphasizes that agranular PF areas tend to border allocortex, which has a simpler three-layer organization and includes structures such as the hippocampus and piriform cortex. Examples include the relationship of agranular insular cortex to piriform cortex and the anterior olfactory nucleus, and the adjacency of medial agranular regions to smaller allocortical territories. Granular PF cortex, by contrast, lies next to agranular PF cortex rather than directly adjoining allocortex. The authors use these neighborhood relationships as one independent clue that granular and agranular PF areas are distinct. Topology is informative because relative positions among neighboring structures can remain evolutionarily conservative even when individual areas change.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simplified medial or orbital frontal map showing allocortex, agranular PF cortex, and granular PF cortex. Annotate the adjacency relationships that the chapter treats as informative.
Key point:
- The location of an area relative to its neighbors contributes evidence about its identity.
- Topology complements, but does not replace, cytoarchitectonic and connectional evidence.
8. Q: What do corticostriatal projections reveal about the evolutionary distinction between agranular and granular PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a systematic difference in the striatal targets of agranular and granular PF areas. In rats, infralimbic and prelimbic projections terminate mainly in the shell of the nucleus accumbens, part of the ventral striatum. Corresponding primate agranular regions, including areas 25 and 32, also project to the accumbens shell. Granular PF areas in monkeys, however, do not project to the ventral striatum in the same way; they project principally to the dorsal striatum, especially the caudate nucleus. A related pattern appears in orbital cortex: agranular orbital regions project toward ventral striatum, while granular orbital regions project mainly to dorsal striatum. The authors interpret this organized difference as evidence for the appearance of new granular PF areas alongside a reorganization of corticostriatal circuits.
Study instruction:
- Trace the pathways described for infralimbic/prelimbic and granular PF regions. Explain why the target territory is more informative than merely noting that both regions project to the striatum.
Key point:
- Agranular PF projections are associated mainly with ventral striatal targets; granular PF projections are associated mainly with dorsal striatum.
- The projection pattern provides a connectional test of proposed cortical homologies.
9. Q: What is the ‘ventral shift’ in corticostriatal projections described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses ‘ventral shift’ to describe the position of projections from homologous agranular PF regions in the striatum. In rats, these projections terminate around the middle of the striatal extent, whereas in primates the corresponding agranular regions terminate more ventrally, in the ventral third. At the same time, primates possess additional dorsal striatal territory, including much of the caudate nucleus, that receives projections from newly evolved granular PF areas. The authors regard the shift as part of a broader reorganization of the frontal-striatal system rather than as evidence that rodent dorsal striatum is simply equivalent to the primate caudate. They acknowledge that homology within the rodent dorsal striatum is difficult because its internal organization differs from the primate arrangement.
Study instruction:
- Use a simple diagram to compare the rat and primate striatum. Mark the ventral targets of agranular PF regions and the dorsal targets of granular PF regions.
Key point:
- The ventral shift is a comparative pattern in projection location, not a claim that every striatal subdivision maps one-to-one across species.
- Striatal homologies remain more difficult to resolve where internal anatomical landmarks differ.
10. Q: How do sensory inputs help distinguish agranular from granular orbital PF areas?
A: Show answer
Agranular PF regions in rodents and primates receive relatively direct olfactory, gustatory, and visceral information. The chapter identifies olfactory input from piriform cortex and gustatory and visceral input conveyed through brainstem and thalamic relays. These shared inputs support the proposed homology of agranular orbital regions across rodents and primates. Granular parts of the primate orbital PF cortex do not show the same direct input pattern; they receive such sensory information more indirectly through agranular frontal areas. This distinction provides another connectional criterion that complements the cytoarchitectonic and corticostriatal evidence. It also illustrates why a broad statement that two regions both receive sensory information is insufficient: the source and route of the input matter.
Study instruction:
- Compare the direct sensory routes described for agranular orbital cortex with the more indirect route to granular orbital PF cortex. Explain how this difference contributes to the homology argument.
Key point:
- The origin and route of sensory input are more informative than the mere presence of sensory responsiveness.
- Shared direct olfactory, gustatory, and visceral inputs support an agranular homology across species.
11. Q: What do autonomic effects of cortical stimulation contribute to the chapter’s argument?
A: Show answer
Electrical stimulation of agranular PF regions in monkeys can evoke changes in respiratory rate, blood pressure, pulse rate, pupil diameter, and piloerection. The chapter reports such effects from agranular insular, caudal orbital, anterior cingulate, prelimbic, and infralimbic regions. Stimulation of granular PF cortex, including granular orbital areas, did not produce the same pattern in the cited experiments. Comparable autonomic effects have also been reported after stimulation of agranular PF regions in several nonprimate mammals. The authors therefore treat autonomic output as an additional line of evidence linking agranular areas across mammals. They do not claim that every autonomic response is exclusive to PF cortex or that stimulation alone proves homology; it is one part of a convergent anatomical case.
Study instruction:
- List the autonomic measures reported in the chapter and distinguish stimulation findings from lesion findings about modulation of stress-related responses.
Key point:
- Agranular PF areas have more direct autonomic influence in the comparisons described.
- Physiological stimulation evidence strengthens the case only when interpreted alongside anatomy and connectivity.
12. Q: What does the bushbaby comparison show about the early evolution of granular PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Bushbabies are strepsirrhine primates and provide a crucial comparison with anthropoid monkeys. The chapter follows Preuss and Goldman-Rakic’s analysis that bushbabies possess homologues of the caudal PF cortex, including the frontal eye field, and granular portions of orbital PF cortex, but lack several granular areas found in anthropoids. These include clear homologues of mid-lateral area 46, dorsomedial area 9, and ventral area 12/47; the status of some other areas is less certain. The comparison supports the proposal that the first granular PF areas appeared in an early primate ancestor, before the strepsirrhine-haplorhine divergence, and that additional areas evolved later in haplorhine or anthropoid lineages. The inference depends on the phylogenetic distribution of traits and on the anatomical quality of the comparisons.
Study instruction:
- Create a phylogenetic sketch with strepsirrhine and anthropoid branches. Mark the PF areas inferred to predate their divergence and those proposed to have appeared later.
Key point:
- Bushbaby anatomy helps separate early primate granular innovations from later anthropoid additions.
- The evolutionary timing is inferred comparatively; it is not a direct observation of the ancestral brain.
13. Q: Which granular PF regions does the chapter attribute to early primates, and what functions are proposed for them?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies early primates as having a homologue of the caudal PF cortex, including area 8 and the frontal eye field, together with granular portions of orbital PF cortex. These regions receive strong visual inputs: the caudal PF cortex is connected with early visual areas such as V2 and V3, while granular orbital cortex has substantial connections with inferior temporal and perirhinal cortex. The proposed functions are complementary. The caudal PF cortex contributes to searching for food items and visual signs of food and to directing covert and overt attention toward them. The granular orbital PF cortex is proposed to encode the current value of food items and to link previous choices with their outcomes, including the sensory identity of foods and fluids. These are functional interpretations developed across the book, not functions demonstrated solely by the evolutionary comparison in this chapter.
Study instruction:
- Map each proposed early granular PF region to its visual inputs and hypothesized contribution to food search or valuation. Label the functional claims as proposals referenced by the chapter.
Key point:
- Early primate granular PF areas include caudal PF and granular orbital regions.
- Search/attention and current food valuation are proposed as complementary functions.
14. Q: What is uncertain about the proposed homologies of some bushbaby and anthropoid PF areas?
A: Show answer
The chapter treats several assignments with different degrees of confidence. The identification of a bushbaby area corresponding to the caudal PF cortex is relatively well supported, while assignments involving the most rostral orbital area 11 are described as less certain. The proposed correspondence of anterodorsal granular bushbaby cortex with rostral area 8Ad, area 9/46, or polar area 10 remains unresolved. The authors also state that evidence for bushbaby homologues of anthropoid areas 9/46 and 10 is incomplete. They call for comparative work across a wider diversity of primate species to resolve these questions. These uncertainties matter because the timing of a cortical innovation depends on whether the relevant area is truly absent from a lineage or simply difficult to identify with current methods.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a confidence-ranked list of the area correspondences discussed in the chapter. For each uncertain case, state what additional species or anatomical evidence could resolve it.
Key point:
- Not every proposed area-to-area correspondence has equal evidential support.
- Absence claims are strongest when sampling and anatomical methods are sufficiently comprehensive.
15. Q: What is the evolutionary significance of the ventral premotor cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents the ventral premotor cortex as another primate innovation that evolved alongside granular PF areas and new posterior parietal and temporal regions. Nudo and Masterton’s comparative work identified corticospinal neurons in a ventral premotor region across the primate species they examined, including strepsirrhines, platyrrhines, and catarrhines, but not in the nonprimate mammals in their sample. The region’s spinal projections are concentrated in upper cervical segments associated with neck and shoulder control, and it also projects to brainstem facial motor nuclei involved in movements of the lower face, lip, and jaw. The chapter interprets this distribution as evidence for a specialization involving forelimb, head, and mouth movements rather than a general controller of hindlimb locomotion.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the comparative species evidence and the target distribution of ventral premotor corticospinal projections. Explain why these findings suggest a role beyond ordinary limb locomotion.
Key point:
- The ventral premotor cortex is treated as part of a coordinated primate cortical innovation.
- Its outputs emphasize upper-body, head, and mouth-related motor systems.
16. Q: How might the ventral premotor cortex contribute to reaching and hand-to-mouth feeding?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that the ventral premotor cortex helps coordinate the hand, head, and mouth during feeding. On a narrow, flexible branch, a primate must stabilize its body, reach toward a food item that may move as the branch sways, grasp it, and bring it to the mouth without losing balance. The region’s projections to cervical spinal segments and facial motor nuclei are consistent with a role in coordinating shoulder, head, lip, and jaw movements. Electrical stimulation in macaques evoked coordinated hand closure, movement of the hand toward the mouth, mouth opening, and head orientation toward the approaching hand. The chapter interprets this as a pattern resembling feeding behavior, while noting that stimulation-induced movements do not by themselves establish the full natural function of the area.
Study instruction:
- Describe the motor sequence required to take food from a moving branch to the mouth. Identify which parts of that sequence could plausibly depend on ventral premotor output.
Key point:
- Reaching and feeding in arboreal settings require coordinated hand, head, mouth, and postural control.
- Stimulation findings are suggestive of a feeding-related motor organization, not a complete functional proof.
17. Q: What is the fine-branch niche, and why does the chapter regard it as important for primate evolution?
A: Show answer
The fine-branch niche consists of the small, thin, often distal branches of angiosperm trees, where flowers, nectar, fruits, seeds, nuts, and young leaves can be abundant. Young leaves are relatively nutritious because they contain more protein and less fibre than mature leaves. Exploiting these foods requires movement on unstable supports, precise reaching and grasping, visual search in clutter, and selection among food items that differ in quality. The chapter proposes that early primates and close relatives became specialized for a life spent in this niche, rather than entering it only occasionally. This ecological setting provides a framework for understanding the co-occurrence of grasping specializations, forward-facing eyes, altered locomotion, new premotor and posterior parietal areas, and the first granular PF areas.
Study instruction:
- Draw a tree-branch scene and label the food resources and behavioral problems described in the chapter. Link each problem to a relevant body or brain specialization.
Key point:
- The fine-branch niche links ecological demands to a suite of anatomical and behavioral innovations.
- The chapter’s account is a proposed evolutionary explanation, not a claim that every early primate ate an identical diet.
18. Q: What does the fossil evidence from Carpolestes contribute to hypotheses about early primate evolution?
A: Show answer
Carpolestes, which lived about 55 million years ago, is discussed as a close relative of early primates, although its exact status depends on competing definitions of when primates first appeared. Bloch and Boyer’s fossil analysis suggested that it had effective limb-grasping adaptations but lacked frontally directed eyes and the hindlimb and pelvic specializations needed for efficient leaping. This supports the possibility that grasping adaptations and use of fine branches preceded some of the visual and locomotor traits associated with modern primates. The chapter does not require a final choice between alternative phylogenetic placements of Carpolestes; its argument needs only the more limited conclusion that specialized grasping may have arisen early in the lineage leading toward primates.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between the fossil observations and the evolutionary sequence inferred from them. Note why the chapter avoids making the argument depend on one disputed placement of Carpolestes.
Key point:
- The fossil provides evidence about grasping and locomotor anatomy, not a direct record of cortical organization.
- The timing of grasping specializations may precede the full modern-primate combination of traits.
19. Q: Why does the chapter say that early primate vision should not be equated with modern human-like vision?
A: Show answer
