The chapter argues that early primates were nocturnal and lacked a fovea and full trichromatic color vision. Modern human visual experience is dominated by high-acuity foveal vision in bright light, so it can mislead attempts to reconstruct the ancestral primate visual niche. The early primate visual advantage is instead proposed to have involved frontally directed eyes and large binocular fields that improved depth perception, sensitivity in dim conditions through binocular summation, lines of sight around obstacles, and visibility beneath the head during manipulation. The fovea and later forms of enhanced color vision appeared in subsequent haplorhine and anthropoid evolution. Keeping these stages separate is important when explaining which visual changes may have accompanied the first granular PF areas.
Study instruction:
- Create a timeline that distinguishes early primate binocular adaptations, haplorhine foveal evolution, and later anthropoid color-vision changes. Avoid describing all three as one event.
Key point:
- Early primate visual adaptations were not the same as modern foveal, bright-light, trichromatic vision.
- The chapter separates visual changes by lineage and evolutionary timing.
20. Q: How could frontally directed eyes benefit animals living and feeding in fine branches?
A: Show answer
Frontally directed eyes increase the overlap of the visual fields and support stereoscopic depth perception, which can help an animal judge distances when leaping or reaching. The chapter also identifies several possible benefits beyond stereopsis: binocular summation may improve sensitivity in dim light; overlapping fields may allow one eye to see around leafy obstructions; and forward orientation improves visibility below and in front of the head, where reaching and object manipulation occur. Laterally directed eyes can be constrained by the orbital bones when trying to converge on close objects beneath the head. These advantages are presented as plausible selective benefits of the early primate visual arrangement, not as proof that one single visual function caused the evolution of forward-facing eyes.
Study instruction:
- For each proposed benefit, describe a behavior in the fine-branch niche that it could support. Distinguish stereopsis from the other benefits of binocular overlap.
Key point:
- Binocular vision can support depth judgment, dim-light sensitivity, and close-range manipulation.
- The chapter treats the visual advantages as a multi-factor explanation.
21. Q: How did primate locomotion free the forelimbs for feeding-related behavior?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes early primates as using hindlimb-dominated locomotion to a greater extent than many nonprimate ancestors. Reduced push-off forces and reduced forelimb stiffness helped limit branch oscillations, potentially reducing noise and predator attraction. Greater reliance on the hindlimbs freed the forelimbs for stabilization, steering, grasping, and manipulation. One hand could support the body while the other reached for food and brought it to the mouth. This pattern links locomotor mechanics to feeding and to the proposed roles of new premotor and posterior parietal areas. It also helps explain why primate motor specializations cannot be understood solely as improvements in travel speed or leaping performance.
Study instruction:
- Compare the functions of forelimbs and hindlimbs in the locomotor and feeding sequence described. Explain how freeing one hand changes the animal’s possible foraging actions.
Key point:
- Locomotor specialization altered what the forelimbs could do during feeding.
- Reduced branch disturbance and postural stability are important parts of the proposed ecological account.
22. Q: What is distinctive about the primate way of reaching, according to the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews evidence that primates represent reaching movements in a visual, often retinal, frame of reference rather than relying only on intrinsic body-centered coordinates. Some ventral premotor neurons encode target location and hand position in retinal coordinates, and movement trajectories can be better predicted in an extrinsic visual frame than in a motor-coordinate frame. As the eyes move, the brain recalculates the target and hand positions and adjusts the motor plan, even when the target and hand remain stationary relative to the body. Human experiments also show visual-field-related biases in reaching to acoustic targets, suggesting that visual coding influences reach computation beyond purely visual targets. The chapter treats these results as evidence for the strong dominance of vision in primate reaching.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between retinal/extrinsic and body-centered/intrinsic coordinates. Give an example of why an eye movement can require recalculation in a retinal frame.
Key point:
- Primate reaching is strongly influenced by visual reference frames.
- The chapter’s examples show that visual coding can influence even reaches toward nonvisual targets.
23. Q: What role do mirror neurons play in the chapter’s discussion of ventral premotor cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that ventral premotor neurons can encode the achievement of a goal, such as grasping or bringing an object toward the mouth, in a way that is not limited to whether the recorded animal itself performs the action. These cells are described as mirror neurons. In the chapter’s broader argument, their presence is consistent with a premotor system organized around goal-directed acts rather than only individual muscle contractions. The authors connect this organization to the demands of reaching and feeding in the fine-branch niche. The chapter does not attempt to provide a full theory of social cognition or action understanding from mirror-neuron activity, so such broader claims should not be inferred from this discussion alone.
Study instruction:
- Write a short distinction between coding a movement goal and coding a specific muscle command. Explain what the chapter does and does not conclude from mirror-neuron findings.
Key point:
- Goal-related coding fits the chapter’s account of coordinated reaching and feeding.
- Mirror-neuron observations should not be stretched into claims beyond the evidence discussed.
24. Q: How are the caudal PF cortex and granular orbital PF cortex proposed to divide early primate foraging functions?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that the caudal PF cortex, including the frontal eye field, contributes to visual search and attention, helping an animal locate food items and signs of food in a cluttered environment. This includes covertly directing attention and overtly orienting the eyes. The granular orbital PF cortex is proposed to represent the current biological value of food and to associate previous choices with their outcomes, including specific sensory features of food and fluid. A valuable item can then become the goal of reaching, grasping, and feeding. The chapter links these proposals to the strong visual connections of both regions, while emphasizing that their functions are developed in greater detail in other chapters of the book.
Study instruction:
- Construct a flow diagram from detecting a possible food item, directing attention, estimating its value, selecting it, and reaching for it. Place the proposed PF contributions at each stage.
Key point:
- Caudal PF is associated with search and attention; granular orbital PF with value and outcome associations.
- The proposed functions are complementary stages in goal-directed foraging.
25. Q: Which additional PF areas are proposed to have evolved in anthropoids?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies the mid-lateral PF cortex (area 46), dorsomedial PF cortex (area 9), and ventral PF cortex (area 12/47) as areas that lack clear homologues in strepsirrhine bushbabies and are therefore proposed to have appeared later in the haplorhine or anthropoid lineage. The authors also consider the postero-lateral PF cortex (area 9/46) and polar PF cortex (area 10) likely later additions, but they explicitly qualify these assignments because the comparative evidence is less comprehensive. These additions are discussed in the context of later anthropoid changes in body size, diet, home range, social organization, and resource volatility. The chapter proposes that they improved choices under demanding foraging conditions, but does not treat each proposed functional role as directly proven by the anatomical evidence.
Study instruction:
- Label the areas with strong versus qualified evolutionary claims. Record why area 10 and area 9/46 require additional comparative evidence.
Key point:
- Areas 46, 9, and 12/47 are prominent proposed anthropoid additions.
- Area 10 and area 9/46 are treated more cautiously because their early-primate homologies remain uncertain.
26. Q: What do fossil endocasts tell us about the timing of anthropoid frontal-lobe expansion?
A: Show answer
Fossil endocasts preserve impressions of the brain’s outer surface on the inside of the skull. The chapter discusses Radinsky’s analysis of early anthropoids such as Aegyptopithecus, which lived around 33 million years ago and had a relatively small frontal lobe despite evidence that visual cortical expansion had already progressed. Brain-size comparisons involving Aegyptopithecus, Parapithecus, and early platyrrhines are used to argue that early anthropoids retained a brain-to-body relationship closer to the strepsirrhine range. The authors infer that substantial anthropoid brain and frontal-lobe expansion occurred later, after the platyrrhine-catarrhine split. Endocasts cannot reveal cytoarchitecture directly, so they cannot identify granular PF areas; they inform overall shape and relative expansion, not the microscopic identity of cortical subdivisions.
Study instruction:
- Explain what an endocast can show and what it cannot show. Use Aegyptopithecus as an example of why a small early anthropoid frontal lobe matters to the evolutionary timeline.
Key point:
- Endocasts support inferences about gross brain shape and size, not cortical layer structure.
- The timing of granular PF expansion is inferred by combining fossil evidence with comparative anatomy of living species.
27. Q: What is meant by the anthropoid ‘upward grade shift’ in brain size?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses upward grade shift to describe the tendency for modern anthropoids to have larger brains than expected for their body size when compared with strepsirrhine primates. The fossil examples Aegyptopithecus, Parapithecus, and early platyrrhines are presented as evidence that this larger-brain condition was not fully present in the earliest anthropoids. The authors argue that brain expansion proceeded independently in catarrhine and platyrrhine lineages after their divergence. They further note that the granular PF cortex occupies a greater proportion of the neocortex in larger primates, suggesting that its expansion contributed substantially to frontal-lobe growth. The term ‘advanced anthropoid’ is used descriptively for descendants that attained this larger-brain condition, not to imply superiority or a predetermined evolutionary direction.
Study instruction:
- Define upward grade shift in your own words and explain why the chapter cautions against reading ‘advanced’ and ‘primitive’ as value judgments.
Key point:
- The upward grade shift concerns brain size relative to body size.
- Evolutionary labels describe lineage position or divergence, not overall superiority.
28. Q: How does the relative size of granular PF cortex change across primates?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that the proportion of neocortex composed of granular PF cortex tends to increase with primate brain size. In representative anthropoid monkeys, granular PF cortex is reported to comprise roughly 9–11% of the neocortex and 43–50% of the frontal lobe; corresponding representative strepsirrhine estimates are about 7–8% and 41–43%. The authors cite this pattern as evidence that PF expansion was disproportionate relative to some other cortical regions during primate evolution. They also acknowledge that expansion elsewhere in the frontal lobe cannot be ruled out. These percentages are sample-based comparative estimates, not universal values for every species, and the chapter’s evolutionary interpretation depends on how cortical areas are defined and measured.
Study instruction:
- Memorize the approximate comparative percentages and explain why they should be reported as representative estimates rather than fixed species-wide constants.
Key point:
- Granular PF cortex becomes proportionately larger in larger primate brains in the cited comparisons.
- Relative expansion is evidence of evolutionary change, but measurement and area definitions matter.
29. Q: When did diurnal activity and the fovea appear in the primate lineage?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that early haplorhines shifted toward diurnal activity and evolved the primate fovea before the origin and later expansion of advanced anthropoids. Early anthropoids inherited these traits. Comparative evidence from tarsiers and anthropoids is used to infer a haplorhine origin of the fovea, while fossil orbital size is discussed as evidence that early anthropoids were adapted to daytime activity. The chapter places later routine trichromatic vision in catarrhines, with a different, polymorphic form of trichromacy in many platyrrhines. This sequence matters because the authors argue that foveal vision and diurnal activity preceded the later anthropoid frontal expansion and therefore are unlikely, by themselves, to explain the origin of the additional anthropoid granular PF areas.
Study instruction:
- Build a dated but approximate lineage timeline for nocturnality, diurnality, fovea, routine trichromacy, and frontal expansion. Identify which transitions are inferred from fossils and which from living-species comparisons.
Key point:
- The fovea and diurnal life predate the later expansion of anthropoid frontal cortex.
- Chronology constrains which ecological changes can plausibly explain a later cortical innovation.
30. Q: What is the difference between routine trichromacy and polymorphic trichromacy?
A: Show answer
Routine trichromacy in catarrhine primates, including humans, is associated with duplication and diversification of the gene for a long-wavelength-sensitive cone photopigment, producing two distinct long-wavelength cone types in addition to the short-wavelength type. In many platyrrhine monkeys, trichromacy is polymorphic: different alleles at a photopigment gene can produce three cone classes in some individuals, particularly females, while other individuals may remain dichromatic. The chapter also notes that howler monkeys evolved routine trichromacy independently. The distinction is important because the two systems have different genetic and population-level distributions, even though both can support three-cone color discrimination. The chapter considers whether trichromacy contributed to later cortical expansion but does not regard it as a sufficient explanation.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simple schematic showing the three cone classes in routine trichromacy and the allele-dependent variation described for polymorphic trichromacy.
Key point:
- Routine and polymorphic trichromacy are different evolutionary and genetic routes to three-cone vision.
- Color-vision evolution should be distinguished from the timing and causes of PF-area expansion.
31. Q: Why does the chapter argue that foveal vision and diurnality alone do not explain anthropoid PF expansion?
A: Show answer
The chapter places the origin of diurnal activity, the fovea, and improved binocular vision before the later frontal-lobe expansion seen in advanced anthropoids. Those earlier visual changes are proposed to have driven important changes in visual cortex, including the proliferation of visual areas. The authors therefore doubt that they alone explain the later appearance of additional granular PF areas. Routine trichromacy arose later in catarrhines and could have contributed to visual demands, but the authors also doubt that it accounts for the broad expansion of frontal and other cortical systems. They instead emphasize the costs of diurnal life, larger body size, wider foraging ranges, competition, predation, heat stress, and volatile food resources as a combined selective context.
Study instruction:
- For each candidate pressure—fovea, diurnality, trichromacy, resource volatility—record its approximate timing and explain whether it precedes or coincides with the PF expansion being explained.
Key point:
- A plausible evolutionary cause must fit both the timing and the pattern of the anatomical change.
- The chapter favors a multi-factor ecological account over a single-visual-trait explanation.
32. Q: What possible role do social factors play in anthropoid PF evolution?
A: Show answer
The chapter does not dismiss social explanations. It notes that daytime activity may have increased predation risk and favored group living, which in turn created more choices involving other group members. It discusses findings that mid-lateral PF cortex size covaries with social-group size in monkeys and that a superior temporal sulcus region involved in processing gaze, facial orientation, and observed movement also varies with group size. This temporal region projects to anterior cingulate cortex, and the chapter reports covariation between activity in the two regions. It also describes medial PF neurons that distinguish self from others and lesion findings affecting interest in images of other monkeys. These observations suggest social relevance, but the chapter does not establish social complexity as the sole cause of granular PF expansion.
Study instruction:
- Separate the chapter’s evidence about social-group size, superior temporal processing, anterior cingulate connectivity, and lesion effects. Explain why these findings support a possible contribution rather than a complete evolutionary explanation.
Key point:
- Social demands may have contributed to PF evolution alongside foraging demands.
- Correlational brain-size relationships and lesion effects do not by themselves establish the evolutionary cause of an area.
33. Q: How did diurnal life increase ecological costs for anthropoids?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three interacting costs. First, daylight activity exposes animals to a greater risk of predation, requiring vigilance while searching and feeding. Second, it increases competition with other diurnal animals such as fruit bats, squirrels, birds, and members of the same social group. Third, daytime heat in tropical environments can restrict safe foraging time; the chapter notes that afternoon temperatures may reach 30–35°C or more and that many anthropoids concentrate foraging soon after waking and before night rest. These constraints make food acquisition time-limited and risky. The authors propose that selection therefore favored better choices and faster learning, because repeated poor choices waste energy and increase exposure to predators and competitors.
Study instruction:
- Construct a causal diagram linking daylight activity to predation, competition, heat stress, restricted foraging time, and the proposed value of improved decision-making.
Key point:
- Diurnal life brings benefits from distant visual information but also costs in predation, competition, and heat exposure.
- The ecological pressures interact, making poor foraging decisions especially costly.
34. Q: How did changes in anthropoid body size and diet alter foraging demands?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes early anthropoids as small animals, often under 100 grams, that likely ate fruits and insects. Later anthropoids became larger, with many modern species exceeding one kilogram, and their diets relied more heavily on fruits and young leaves from angiosperm trees. Larger bodies require more energy, while fruits and nutritious leaves are patchily distributed and seasonally variable. These changes increased the need to travel across larger home ranges and to select productive trees efficiently. The chapter links the shift in body size and diet, which became prominent after about 34 million years ago, with changes in locomotion and the expansion of the frontal lobe. The account is a broad comparative pattern, not a claim that every anthropoid lineage followed an identical dietary trajectory.
Study instruction:
- Compare the likely diet and body-size demands of early and later anthropoids. Explain how larger energy requirements can change the cost of selecting a poor foraging location.
Key point:
- Larger body size and reliance on patchy fruits and young leaves increased foraging demands.
- Diet, locomotion, home-range size, and brain expansion are discussed as interrelated evolutionary changes.
35. Q: Why did many larger anthropoids shift from leaping to quadrupedal locomotion?
A: Show answer
As anthropoids became larger, many moved through larger branches as arboreal quadrupeds rather than relying on the leaping and grasping locomotion associated with early primates. The chapter argues that this change extended their foraging range and allowed them to exploit resources beyond the fine-branch niche, but it also imposed substantial energy costs, especially when moving between elevations. Other locomotor patterns evolved in particular lineages, including terrestrial quadrupedalism, suspensory movement, and human bipedalism. These alternatives reflect different ecological opportunities and constraints. The chapter’s main point is that larger bodies and more costly movement increased the energy required to forage, making accurate selection of resource locations more consequential.
Study instruction:
- Compare arboreal quadrupedalism, terrestrial quadrupedalism, suspensory locomotion, and bipedalism as described. Focus on how each changes access to resources and movement costs.
Key point:
- Locomotor evolution diversified rather than following one universal path.
- High movement costs increase the value of selecting productive food locations.
36. Q: What is resource volatility, and why does the chapter treat it as a major selective pressure?
A: Show answer
Resource volatility refers to unpredictable variation in the availability of foods across time and space. Fruit production can be irregular within a tree species, vary substantially from year to year, and differ among locations within the same home range. Young nutritious leaves are also seasonal and available from relatively few plant species. The chapter cites estimates that a large monkey’s home range may contain about 100,000 trees, with no more than roughly 4% bearing ripe fruit at a given time; some field estimates for mangabeys suggest that only about 50 of those trees may be productive at one time. Under such conditions, a preferred food source can fail, and the animal must switch strategies or use information to predict where resources will become available. The authors propose that later granular PF areas improved this adaptive decision-making.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between spatial patchiness, seasonal variation, and year-to-year volatility. Use the chapter’s tree estimates to illustrate why a large home range does not guarantee abundant food.
Key point:
- Resource abundance at the habitat level can coexist with scarcity at the level of usable, ripe foods.
- The chapter proposes that resource volatility favored flexible and predictive foraging choices.
37. Q: How do anthropoids value individual trees when choosing where to forage?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that monkeys can distinguish and evaluate individual trees, including trees that appear very similar to less experienced observers. Prior experience with the quality of fruit or leaves influences whether an animal enters a particular tree again. Field studies cited in the chapter found that monkeys visited trees more often when they had previously obtained high-quality fruit there. This suggests that foraging value is not assigned only to a tree species in general; animals can use experience associated with individual locations. Such valuation allows them to prioritize productive sites while avoiding some wasted travel. The chapter presents these field observations as evidence for learned evaluation, while leaving the detailed neural mechanisms to other parts of the book.
Study instruction:
- Describe how a monkey’s past experience with one particular tree could alter a later route choice. Distinguish individual-tree valuation from a general preference for a tree species.
Key point:
- Foraging decisions can be based on the learned value of individual resource locations.
- Past outcomes provide information that can reduce unnecessary travel and search.
38. Q: How do anthropoids adapt their foraging strategies during food shortages?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes that different species or groups may respond to scarcity in different ways. Fruit- and insect-dependent anthropoids may increase foraging time, reduce rest, and compete more intensely. Folivores may instead conserve energy and forage less. The chapter’s vervet-monkey example shows flexibility even within one species: one group responded to a dry-season fruit decline by increasing foraging and concentrating more on insects, whereas another reduced foraging time and diversified its diet toward flowers and insects. The authors use these differences to illustrate that adaptability, rather than one fixed response, can help animals cope with irregular shortages. A successful strategy depends on available fallback foods, energetic costs, and local ecological conditions.
Study instruction:
- Compare the two vervet-group responses described in the chapter. Explain why neither strategy should be treated as universally optimal across all habitats or food shortages.
Key point:
- Adaptive responses to scarcity are context-dependent.
- Flexibility may involve changing foraging time, dietary breadth, or the balance between preferred and fallback foods.
39. Q: How can acoustic and visual cues from other animals guide anthropoid foraging?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes monkeys using cues associated with food availability, including sounds made by other primates and birds that exploit similar resources. In the mangabey observations discussed, monkeys approached a tree more quickly when it was already occupied by noisy chimpanzees or hornbills, apparently using the competitors’ presence as evidence that fruit had recently appeared or ripened. The behavior is notable because chimpanzees can also pose a predation risk. Thus, a cue can carry both opportunity and danger: the animal must interpret the information while weighing the costs of approaching. The chapter uses this example to show how distant or indirect signs can guide decisions before the food itself is directly inspected.
Study instruction:
- Analyze the mangabey example as an information-versus-risk problem. Identify the cue, the inferred resource state, the possible danger, and the decision that follows.
Key point:
- Other animals can act as informative cues to resource availability.
- A useful cue does not remove the need to assess the risks associated with following it.
40. Q: How can synchrony in fruiting help monkeys predict food availability?
A: Show answer
Some trees of the same species produce fruit in related or synchronized patterns. The chapter describes a Japanese macaque study in which placing ripe persimmons within a home range induced monkeys to visit persimmon trees even when the wild trees did not yet have ripe fruit. The interpretation is that the monkeys knew where trees of that species were located and used fruiting at one tree as information about the likely state of other trees. This is a form of prediction based on similarity and synchrony rather than direct inspection of every potential resource. The strategy can reduce search costs, but it will be useful only to the extent that the assumed relationship among trees holds in that ecological context.
Study instruction:
- Explain the information transfer from one fruiting tree to other trees of the same species. Identify a condition under which this prediction could fail.
Key point:
- A resource event at one location can inform expectations about similar locations.
- Predictive use of synchrony depends on the reliability of the underlying ecological pattern.
41. Q: How does elapsed time help anthropoids predict future food production?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that monkeys appear to remember the production history of individual trees and use elapsed time to predict when food may become available again. Milton’s observations are cited for the idea that monkeys monitor prior fruiting patterns, while other field studies found faster movement toward trees that currently contain fruit and especially toward trees with larger quantities. The proposed strategy allows an animal to use temporal memory even when immediate sensory cues are absent. It is not simply remembering that a tree was once productive; it involves relating past events and the time since those events to a prediction about current or future availability. The accuracy of this strategy will depend on how regular the tree’s production cycle is.
Study instruction:
- Create a hypothetical tree record with dates of past fruiting. Explain how an animal might use elapsed time to decide whether to revisit it and what uncertainty remains.
Key point:
- Temporal memory can guide resource prediction without an immediate visual cue.
- The usefulness of elapsed-time prediction depends on the regularity of the resource’s production history.
42. Q: How can recent environmental events, such as heat, guide predictions about fruit availability?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes evidence that monkeys use recent weather conditions when deciding when to revisit productive trees. Heat can accelerate plant metabolism and fruit ripening, so a period of hotter weather may signal that fruit will become available sooner. Janmaat and colleagues’ observations, as summarized in the chapter, found that monkeys returned more quickly to previously productive trees after hotter conditions. This is an example of event-based prediction: the animal uses a recent environmental change as information about the likely state of a resource. The chapter does not imply that heat always increases food availability in every species or habitat; the relationship depends on the biology of the plants and local conditions.
Study instruction:
- Explain the causal chain from recent heat to plant processes to expected ripening to a monkey’s route choice. State why this relationship should be tested locally rather than assumed universally.
Key point:
- Environmental events can provide predictive information about resource timing.
- Event-based prediction relies on learned or evolved relationships between environmental conditions and food production.
43. Q: Why are laboratory tasks useful for studying natural foraging, and what are their limitations?
A: Show answer
Laboratory tasks allow researchers to control choices among objects or locations and measure how animals learn, remember, attend, and select rewards. The chapter describes tasks involving effort and reward, object value, visual search, sequences of choices, conditional visuomotor mappings, and learning from single experiences. These tasks can isolate cognitive capacities that plausibly contribute to successful foraging. However, laboratory choices differ from wild foraging: the wild involves whole trees and locations, complex object manipulation, extended travel, social interaction, uncertain time boundaries, and natural risks. A laboratory trial with a saccade or reach is therefore a model of one component, not a complete replica of ecological behavior. Conclusions should be tested against field observations and should avoid equating laboratory errors with all forms of unsuccessful natural foraging.
Study instruction:
- Choose one laboratory task described in the chapter and identify the natural foraging problem it models. Then list at least three ecological features that the task omits or simplifies.
Key point:
- Controlled tasks can isolate cognitive processes but cannot reproduce the full ecology of natural foraging.
- Converging laboratory and field evidence is stronger than either approach alone.
44. Q: How could a laboratory task model the choice between exploiting a dwindling resource and exploring a more distant one?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights a task developed by Hayden and colleagues as an attempt to model a foraging trade-off. Monkeys choose among visual options associated with different rewards and delays, using saccadic eye movements to select targets. The task is intended to capture aspects of deciding whether to continue exploiting a resource that is becoming less productive or travel toward a more distant alternative. It can examine how animals respond to visual signs, estimate delay or travel time, and compare expected outcomes. The chapter stresses that the task is an abstraction: a colored shape and delay interval are not equivalent to a real tree, locomotion through a habitat, or the social and predatory context of wild foraging.
Study instruction:
- Write down the choice variables represented in the task—resource amount, delay, visual cue, and response. Explain which natural variables are simplified or left out.
Key point:
- The task models a decision structure rather than reproducing a complete natural foraging episode.
- A useful model preserves the relevant choice trade-off while making its limits explicit.
45. Q: How do the book’s later chapters connect PF subdivisions with distinct foraging computations?
A: Show answer
The chapter previews a division of labor across PF subdivisions. It states that the polar PF cortex is proposed to support learning a choice that is appropriate in a context after a single prior experience; the dorsal PF cortex is linked to using the order of visual events and elapsed time and to planning sequences of goals; the ventral PF cortex is linked to guiding choices with visual and acoustic signs of resources; and the newly evolved granular areas are proposed to work together in rapid learning and the use of abstract rules or strategies. The chapter’s own role is to establish the evolutionary and ecological context for these later functional arguments. These mappings should therefore be read as the book’s framework and hypotheses, not as conclusions fully demonstrated within Chapter 2 alone.
Study instruction:
- Make a matrix with PF subdivision, proposed computation, information used, and the later chapter that develops the claim. Mark that these are previewed functions.
Key point:
- The chapter links evolutionary anatomy to a proposed set of specialized decision computations.
- Functional assignments are developed in later chapters and should not be over-attributed to this chapter.
46. Q: What is the proposed relationship between single-experience learning and anthropoid resource volatility?
A: Show answer
The authors propose that when preferred foods are scarce, risky, or expensive to reach, repeated trial-and-error learning can impose substantial costs. A single poor choice may waste travel time, expose an animal to predators, or lead it to miss a short period of food availability. The chapter suggests that later granular PF areas helped anthropoids learn from single events and use context, signs, and abstract strategies to reduce the number of unproductive choices. This is an evolutionary hypothesis about the adaptive value of faster, more predictive learning. The chapter does not establish that every anthropoid species uses identical learning mechanisms or that single-trial learning evolved only because of food scarcity; other ecological and social demands may also contribute.
Study instruction:
- Describe a hypothetical sequence in which one poor foraging choice is costly. Explain how learning from a single experience could change the next decision and what alternative explanations might also matter.
Key point:
- High costs of error make rapid learning potentially advantageous.
- The proposed link between resource volatility and granular PF evolution is a hypothesis supported by converging ecological and anatomical observations.
47. Q: How should the chapter’s evolutionary claims be separated from its directly observed evidence?
A: Show answer
Direct observations discussed in the chapter include cortical architecture and connections in living species, stimulation and lesion effects, fossil endocast shape, body-size estimates, field observations of feeding behavior, and measured relationships between visual or social variables and brain anatomy. The evolutionary narrative then infers ancestral traits and proposes why certain areas may have been favored. For example, the absence of a clear area in bushbabies and its presence in anthropoids supports a later origin, but does not directly reveal the exact ancestral brain. Similarly, observed resource volatility and the expansion of granular PF cortex are compatible with a selective explanation but do not prove that volatility caused the expansion. A careful reader should distinguish what was measured, what was inferred phylogenetically, and what was proposed as an adaptive function.
Study instruction:
- Annotate a paragraph of the chapter with three labels: observation, ancestral-state inference, and adaptive hypothesis. Explain why the distinctions matter for scientific reasoning.
Key point:
- Anatomical, fossil, behavioral, and physiological observations are not identical to an explanation of why a trait evolved.
- Correlation and phylogenetic distribution constrain hypotheses but do not alone prove selection or causation.
48. Q: What are the principal methodological cautions when comparing PF cortex across species?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several cautions. First, cytoarchitectonic boundaries can be graded and observer-dependent, so apparent presence or absence of layer 4 should be assessed quantitatively where possible. Second, homology cannot be inferred from a single task deficit, neurochemical input, or shared cell property. Third, species samples matter: absence in one representative strepsirrhine does not settle every question about all early primates. Fourth, fossil endocasts reveal gross morphology but not microscopic cortical architecture. Fifth, lesion and stimulation effects can be influenced by neighboring regions and network connections. Finally, laboratory tasks simplify natural behavior. Together, these limitations argue for combining multiple anatomical methods, broad phylogenetic sampling, careful behavioral characterization, and appropriately qualified conclusions.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a checklist for evaluating a cross-species PF homology claim. Include architecture, topology, connectivity, phylogenetic sampling, functional evidence, and methodological limitations.
Key point:
- No single method resolves all questions of cortical homology.
- Strong comparative conclusions require convergent evidence and transparent uncertainty.
49. Q: How does the chapter integrate early mammalian, early primate, and later anthropoid PF evolution into one account?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s synthesis begins with agranular PF areas in early mammals, which it associates with the evolution of the mammalian neocortex and new foraging demands. Early primates later added granular caudal and orbital PF regions as part of a broader suite of adaptations for living and feeding in fine branches, including forward-facing eyes, specialized locomotion, and new premotor and posterior parietal areas. Later anthropoids added further granular PF regions as they became larger, diurnal, and dependent on dispersed food resources over wider home ranges. The proposed advantage of these later additions was to reduce costly, risky, or unproductive choices during resource shortages. The account treats PF cortex as a changing system whose components must be understood both individually and in relation to the whole.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the chapter’s three evolutionary phases in a one-page concept map. Include the proposed ecological problem, anatomical innovation, and behavioral consequence for each phase.
Key point:
- The PF cortex is an evolutionary mosaic of areas with different origins.
- Understanding the whole requires distinguishing the histories and functions of its constituent parts.
50. Q: What research would most directly test the chapter’s hypotheses about anthropoid PF evolution?
A: Show answer
A strong test would combine broader comparative sampling with methods that address the chapter’s acknowledged uncertainties. Researchers could examine cytoarchitecture, myeloarchitecture, cortical topology, corticostriatal and corticocortical connectivity, sensory inputs, and autonomic outputs across multiple strepsirrhine, tarsier, platyrrhine, and catarrhine species. Phylogenetic analyses could test whether particular granular areas track specific lineage divergences, body-size changes, or ecological transitions. Field studies could quantify resource volatility, travel costs, predation exposure, social competition, and the use of predictive cues in the same species. Carefully designed laboratory tasks could then measure the proposed learning, temporal prediction, attention, and strategy functions. Such work would not make the evolutionary account certain, but it could test whether anatomical innovations and ecological demands covary in the predicted ways.
Study instruction:
- Design a comparative study with at least three species and specify the anatomical, behavioral, ecological, and phylogenetic measures you would collect. Include one result that would weaken the chapter’s hypothesis.
Key point:
- The hypotheses are testable through converging comparative, ecological, and behavioral evidence.
- A useful test must include evidence that could disconfirm the proposed evolutionary explanation.
51. Q: What practical lesson does this chapter offer for interpreting rodent models of human prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
The chapter advises researchers to specify which prefrontal subdivision is being studied and whether it is homologous to the human or primate region of interest. Rodents are valuable models for agranular PF functions and for mechanisms that are broadly shared across mammals. They are not automatically valid stand-ins for primate granular PF regions, because the latter have no direct rodent homologue according to the chapter’s comparative account. A similar task impairment in rats and monkeys may arise from different circuits or computations. Translational claims should therefore be based on detailed anatomical and connectional correspondence, supported by comparable behavioral analyses, rather than on the fact that both species perform a task called working memory, valuation, or delayed response.
Study instruction:
- Take a familiar rodent prefrontal experiment and write what anatomical evidence would be needed before generalizing its result to a specific primate PF subdivision.
Key point:
- Model validity depends on the correspondence between the studied structures, not just task labels.
- Rodent findings can be highly informative without being direct evidence about every primate PF function.
52. Q: How should the chapter’s use of ‘advanced’ and ‘ancestral’ be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The chapter explicitly warns that terms such as ‘advanced’ and ‘primitive’ describe evolutionary relationships or degrees of divergence from an ancestral condition, not overall cognitive worth, complexity, or superiority. It uses ‘advanced anthropoids’ to distinguish larger-brained descendant species from earlier small-brained anthropoids. The terminology is intended to make the evolutionary sequence easier to discuss, not to imply that every descendant is better adapted in every respect or that evolution proceeds toward a predetermined endpoint. Different lineages retain, modify, or independently evolve traits in response to their own environments. This caution is important when comparing living species: a modern species is not a frozen ancestor, and a lineage with fewer derived traits is not necessarily inferior.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite one sentence using ‘advanced’ or ‘primitive’ in a way that makes the descriptive meaning explicit and avoids a value judgment.
Key point:
- Evolutionary terminology should not be confused with rankings of intelligence or worth.
- Living species are contemporary lineages, not literal replicas of ancestral forms.
53. Q: What is the overall significance of viewing PF cortex as part of a wider suite of primate brain innovations?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that PF evolution did not occur in isolation. Early primates also developed new visual, posterior parietal, premotor, and temporal areas, as well as changes in connected thalamic and striatal structures. The ventral premotor cortex is discussed as a key component of reaching and feeding, while posterior parietal and visual systems support spatial guidance and search. Later anthropoid expansion likewise occurred within broader changes in cortical organization, including motor and somatosensory specializations. This systems-level view prevents the mistaken assumption that a new PF function could evolve without changes in the sensory information it receives, the motor systems it influences, or the subcortical circuits with which it communicates.
Study instruction:
- Create a network diagram linking PF, visual, posterior parietal, premotor, temporal, thalamic, and striatal structures. Add one ecological behavior that depends on their coordination.
Key point:
- PF evolution is embedded in a wider network of cortical and subcortical changes.
- Behavioral innovation often depends on coordinated changes across multiple brain systems.
Rows: 53
Table 3
Medial Prefrontal Cortex: Outcome-Guided Action Selection
1. Q: What is the central function of the medial prefrontal cortex described in Chapter 3?
A: Show answer
The medial prefrontal (PF) cortex contributes to evaluating and choosing actions according to the outcomes those actions have produced or are expected to produce, with outcome value assessed in relation to current biological needs. It is especially relevant when external sensory cues do not specify what to do. Its connections provide access to memories of action-related events through the hippocampal system, updated valuations through the amygdala, and routes for influencing movement through medial premotor areas. The chapter's overall account is therefore not that the medial PF cortex directly issues every motor command, but that it biases competition among possible actions and action rules. In anthropoid primates, granular medial areas appear to extend this capacity through task-rule selection, feedback-time evaluation of self-generated goals, and learning from single events.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the proposed chain from memory and current value, through action selection, to premotor influence; distinguish biasing a choice from directly generating a movement.
Key point:
- Medial PF function is action selection guided by predicted outcomes and current needs.
- Its contribution depends on its distinctive connections and differs across agranular and granular subdivisions.
2. Q: Which anatomical regions does the chapter include in the medial prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
In primates, the agranular medial PF cortex comprises the anterior cingulate cortex (area 24), prelimbic cortex (mainly area 32), and infralimbic cortex (area 25). The granular component comprises the medial part of area 9 and area 10, also called the polar PF cortex. The chapter includes all of area 10 in the medial PF cortex of monkeys, but only the medial part of area 10 in humans. The agranular regions have homologues in rodents and other mammals, whereas the granular medial areas discussed here are described as primate specializations. The chapter uses these cytoarchitectonic and connectional distinctions to organize the functional evidence; it does not treat the medial PF cortex as a single undifferentiated field.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simple medial frontal map and label areas 24, 32, 25, medial 9, and 10; mark which are agranular and which are granular.
Key point:
- The medial PF cortex contains agranular and granular subdivisions with different comparative and functional evidence.
- Area 14 is not settled anatomically and is mostly deferred to the discussion of orbital PF cortex.
3. Q: Why does the chapter define the anterior cingulate cortex more narrowly than some other publications do?
A: Show answer
The term anterior cingulate cortex is used inconsistently across the literature. In this book, it refers to part of area 24 and excludes the prelimbic and infralimbic cortices, as well as the cingulate motor areas. The authors classify the cingulate motor areas as premotor cortex rather than as anterior cingulate PF cortex. This restricted terminology matters when interpreting lesion and recording studies: a paper using a broader definition may include tissue that the chapter assigns to a different functional or anatomical subdivision. The authors also note that area 14 is variably classified as medial PF or the most medial orbital PF cortex, so they avoid treating its status as settled in this chapter.
Study instruction:
- When reading a study, check its anatomical definitions, coordinates, and lesion or recording extent before comparing its results with this chapter's use of “anterior cingulate cortex.”
Key point:
- Anatomical labels are not interchangeable across studies.
- Interpret functional claims in light of the tissue actually included in each study.
4. Q: What is meant by a connectional fingerprint, and why is it useful for understanding medial PF function?
A: Show answer
A connectional fingerprint is a concise characterization of the prominent inputs and outputs that distinguish one cortical region from others. The chapter's diagrams are selective conceptual summaries, not exhaustive maps of every pathway or historical account of who first described each connection. For the medial PF cortex, the fingerprint emphasizes strong links with the hippocampal system and amygdala, connections with medial premotor areas, relatively sparse sensory input compared with other PF regions, and projections to hypothalamic and brainstem autonomic-control structures. These connections suggest what information the region can use and where its influence can be exerted. The authors argue that connectivity constrains function: the medial PF cortex is positioned to combine memories and current outcome values to bias internally guided actions.
Study instruction:
- Reproduce the connectional fingerprint from memory and explain the functional implication of each major input and output rather than memorizing a list alone.
Key point:
- Connectivity supplies a mechanistic basis for functional hypotheses.
- The chapter's diagrams are selective and should not be mistaken for complete anatomical inventories.
5. Q: How might hippocampal connections contribute to medial PF cortex function?
A: Show answer
The hippocampus and subiculum have dense reciprocal connections with prelimbic and infralimbic cortex. Other medial PF regions are linked with the hippocampal system through indirect cortical routes, including the retrosplenial, entorhinal, and parahippocampal cortices, and through subcortical relays involving the mammillary bodies and thalamus. The chapter interprets these pathways as providing information about navigation and past events involving actions. This information can support choices based on an animal's current location, remembered places, or a previously experienced conjunction of context, goal, action, and outcome. The authors contrast this strong medial relationship with the weak or absent hippocampal connections of some other PF regions, such as lateral orbital PF cortex.
Study instruction:
- Trace the direct and indirect hippocampal routes described in the chapter and connect them to a concrete navigation or remembered-action choice.
Key point:
- Hippocampal inputs can supply spatial and event-related information for action selection.
- The connection pattern is stronger in medial PF regions than in some other PF subdivisions.
6. Q: What information does the amygdala provide to the medial prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes the amygdala's contribution to updating the value of predicted outcomes according to current biological needs. A food or fluid may be valuable when an animal is hungry or thirsty but less valuable after the animal has consumed enough of it. Amygdala–cortical interactions help represent such changes, and the same general valuation principle can apply to negative outcomes, including harm and other biological costs. The chapter cautions against describing the amygdala as a general-purpose reward centre: conditional visuomotor learning can remain intact after bilateral amygdala lesions even though that learning involves reward associations. The more specific account is that the amygdala helps update outcome valuations, which can then influence action choices through medial PF circuitry.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between learning that an action predicts an outcome and updating the current value of that outcome; use satiety as an example.
Key point:
- Amygdala input conveys current, need-dependent outcome valuation.
- Reward processing alone is too broad a description of amygdala function in the chapter's account.
7. Q: How do medial PF connections with premotor cortex influence action?
A: Show answer
The medial PF cortex projects directly and indirectly to medial premotor areas. The chapter describes routes from granular medial area 9 to anterior cingulate cortex, from there to the rostral cingulate motor area, and onward through connections with the presupplementary motor area and supplementary motor area. Lesions involving cingulate motor areas or preSMA and SMA impair movements that monkeys make without an external sensory prompt. These findings support the idea that medial PF regions can influence action through premotor circuitry. The medial PF cortex is therefore positioned to bias which action is selected, while premotor and motor structures participate more directly in preparing and executing movement.
Study instruction:
- Diagram the medial PF-to-premotor route and distinguish action selection, motor preparation, and motor execution in your explanation.
Key point:
- Medial PF outputs provide a route for influencing internally guided movement.
- A role in action selection does not imply that medial PF cortex itself issues the final motor command.
8. Q: What does the relative paucity of sensory inputs imply about the medial PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Compared with ventral and orbital PF regions, the medial PF cortex receives relatively few prominent visual inputs from inferior temporal cortex. It does have some connections with perirhinal and superior temporal regions, including area TPO, which processes auditory and visual information, and it receives projections from rostral superior temporal cortex, including the temporal pole. Nevertheless, its sensory connections are not as prominent as those of regions specialized for processing external stimulus information. The authors interpret this pattern as consistent with a specialization for using internal states and memories to guide action, especially when the external environment does not directly indicate which action should be chosen. “Internal” is a functional contrast, not a claim that the medial PF cortex is isolated from all sensory information.
Study instruction:
- Compare the medial PF sensory profile with the stronger sensory connectivity attributed to ventral and orbital PF areas; note the exceptions rather than saying sensory input is absent.
Key point:
- The medial PF cortex is relatively, not absolutely, sparse in sensory inputs.
- Its internal-signal specialization is inferred from the balance of connections, not from sensory disconnection.
9. Q: What are the hypothalamic and brainstem connections of the medial PF cortex thought to contribute?
A: Show answer
The medial PF cortex projects to the hypothalamus and to brainstem reticular nuclei involved in visceromotor function. The chapter suggests that these pathways may influence autonomic arousal and other bodily responses. It gives the lateral hypothalamus as an example of a structure involved in autonomic arousal and the paraventricular hypothalamic nucleus as a controller of neuroendocrine and neurosecretory outputs. These connections broaden the meaning of action-related outcomes: choices can be influenced not only by external rewards but also by internal physiological states and bodily costs. The chapter presents the likely autonomic influence as an interpretation of the anatomy rather than a complete account of every pathway's specific function.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a motivational state could influence action selection through amygdala valuation and hypothalamic or brainstem control pathways.
Key point:
- Medial PF connectivity links action selection with systems regulating bodily state.
- The chapter describes likely autonomic roles without claiming that every projection has been functionally resolved.
10. Q: What does the chapter mean by “internal” signals, and how do they differ from external signals?
A: Show answer
“Internal” signals are those that convey internal states and memories, including motivational needs and remembered events. External signals are sensory inputs such as vision, hearing, smell, taste, and touch. A visible cue that indicates which tree contains fruit is an external guide to a foraging goal. Hunger, memory of a previously productive location, or knowledge of the outcome of an earlier action can instead guide the choice internally. The distinction concerns the information that determines the choice, not whether the animal is physically surrounded by sensory stimuli. An animal may see many cues while none of them specifies what action to take. In such cases, remembered action–outcome relations and current motivation can provide the basis for selection.
Study instruction:
- Classify several examples as primarily externally guided or internally guided, and explain cases in which both signal types compete.
Key point:
- Internal signals convey motivational state and memory; external signals convey current sensory information.
- Internal guidance is especially useful when external cues do not specify the appropriate action.
11. Q: How does the chapter distinguish decisions, choices, goals, actions, and outcomes?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses “decision” narrowly for a perceptual judgment based on sensory input, not as a synonym for an action choice. A choice may select a goal or may select an action directly. A goal is the object or location targeted by an action. The action is the behaviour performed toward that target, and the outcome is the feedback that follows, including what happened and how valuable or costly it is at that time. The authors deliberately reserve “outcome” for rewards and other feedback rather than using “goal” to mean reward. This terminology separates the target of behaviour from its consequences and helps distinguish choices among objects from choices among actions.
Study instruction:
- For a foraging example, identify the perceptual decision, selected location or object, action, and resulting outcome as four distinct elements.
Key point:
- A goal is a target; an outcome is the consequence or feedback.
- A perceptual decision is not the same thing as choosing or executing an action.
12. Q: What is an accumulator–racetrack model, and how can it explain action selection?
A: Show answer
An accumulator network integrates evidence or input favouring a represented decision, choice, or action. Activity may rise gradually, like a leaky integrator, until it reaches a threshold that releases an output. Multiple networks can operate in parallel and compete; the network that reaches threshold first wins control under the model. In the chapter's illustration, neurons in frontal eye field show ramping activity before a saccadic movement, with faster ramps associated with shorter reaction times. The model provides a way to explain how representations of alternatives compete, rather than assuming that a single cell independently determines behaviour. It is a proposed mechanism for understanding selection, not direct proof that every relevant neural population literally follows one identical threshold process.
Study instruction:
- Sketch two competing accumulators, label evidence input, ramping activity, threshold, and output, and explain how different ramp rates change reaction time.
Key point:
- Accumulators integrate competing evidence until a threshold is reached.
- The model links population-level competition to the timing of decisions, choices, and actions.
13. Q: How can top-down bias alter the outcome of competition among neural accumulators?
A: Show answer
A higher-order signal can change the starting activity, excitability, or rate of accumulation in one network relative to its competitors. In the chapter's example, intracortical microstimulation in area MT increases activity in a population representing upward motion, making that population more likely to reach threshold first. Competing populations can inhibit one another, so increasing one alternative may both speed its own accumulation and delay the competing alternative. The medial PF cortex is proposed to exert analogous top-down biases on lower-order systems using information about memories, current outcome values, and action rules. Such bias changes the probability that a particular system controls behaviour without requiring the medial PF cortex to directly generate the final motor pattern.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a small change in one accumulator's input can alter a winner-take-all outcome when competing networks inhibit each other.
Key point:
- Top-down bias changes the balance of competition among alternatives.
- Biasing a network is distinct from replacing its computations or directly executing its output.
14. Q: What are stimulus–response, response–outcome, and stimulus–response–outcome associations?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's shorthand, S denotes a stimulus, R a response or action, and O an outcome. Classical conditioning commonly establishes a stimulus–outcome association, in which a stimulus predicts an outcome. Instrumental conditioning establishes a response–outcome association, in which an action produces an outcome; the relation may also be learned in a particular stimulus context, yielding an S–R–O mapping. These association types can compete to control behaviour. An outcome-directed action depends on predicting its consequence, whereas a habit can be expressed through a strong S–R association without sensitivity to the current value of the predicted outcome. The chapter uses “response” and “action” with little distinction, while noting that response often implies an initiating stimulus.
Study instruction:
- Create one concrete example each of S–O, R–O, S–R, and S–R–O learning; identify which association is needed to predict a specific action's consequence.
Key point:
- S–R–O and R–O mappings retain information about consequences; S–R habits can guide action without current outcome evaluation.
- These are functional descriptions of learned associations, not mutually exclusive labels for every behaviour.
15. Q: How does the outcome-devaluation test distinguish outcome-directed behaviour from habit?
A: Show answer
Animals are first trained so that two different actions produce two different rewards. One reward is then devalued, commonly by allowing the animal to consume it to satiety. During the later choice test, the animal can select either action before receiving a new reward. If it avoids the action associated with the devalued reward, its behaviour is sensitive to the currently predicted value of the outcome and is described in the chapter as outcome-directed. If it continues to perform the action associated with the devalued reward, despite the change in value, the behaviour is consistent with a habit. The test is important because it assesses whether the animal uses an updated prediction, not simply whether it can discriminate or consume the reward in the test situation.
Study instruction:
- Write out the training, devaluation, and test phases in order; explain what each possible choice reveals about outcome sensitivity.
Key point:
- Outcome-directed action changes when the predicted outcome loses value.
- Habitual action persists despite devaluation of the associated outcome.
16. Q: Why is it important that the animal chooses before receiving the devalued reward during the test phase?
A: Show answer
The sequence demonstrates that the animal can use a prediction of the outcome's current value rather than needing to sample the reward again at the moment of choice. After one reward has been devalued by prior consumption, the animal can avoid the action that predicts that reward even though the test choice occurs before any new reward is delivered. The behaviour therefore reflects an association between the action and its outcome combined with an updated valuation. If the animal had to experience the reward in the test phase before adjusting its choice, the evidence for prospective outcome evaluation would be weaker. The procedure distinguishes using an internal prediction from reacting only to immediate sensory feedback.
Study instruction:
- Explain why the timing of devaluation relative to the choice test is essential to interpreting the task as a test of predicted outcome value.
Key point:
- The animal can choose using an internally retrieved, updated outcome value.
- The test is prospective rather than a simple reaction to the reward just received.
17. Q: How does the chapter explain the emergence of habits after extensive training?
A: Show answer
With extensive experience, stimulus–response associations can become strong enough to control behaviour without reference to the predicted outcome. In the chapter's accumulator account, the S–R network becomes increasingly likely to reach its threshold before competing S–R–O networks. Once the habitual association reliably wins, the action is expressed even when a different outcome would now be more valuable. The chapter calls the extensive training that produces this shift overtraining. It uses “habit” in this relatively specific sense, rather than as a broad label for any frequent or effortless behaviour. This distinction matters because habitual control is defined by reduced sensitivity to current outcome value, not simply by repetition.
Study instruction:
- Describe how repeated reinforcement can strengthen an S–R route and how the outcome-devaluation test can reveal the resulting shift in control.
Key point:
- Habit formation is a shift in which learned association dominates action selection.
- Repetition alone is not sufficient to establish that behaviour is habitual in the chapter's technical sense.
18. Q: What evidence suggests that medial PF cortex helps mammals overcome prepotent behaviour?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts inflexible responses in some nonmammalian vertebrates with the faster behavioural adjustment seen in mammals. Green lizards have an innate tendency to approach green stimuli; training them to choose a red stimulus associated with the more desirable food can require hundreds of trials, and some fail despite being able to discriminate the colours. Rats can learn a matching-to-position task, which requires returning to a recently rewarded location despite a tendency to explore other sites. Lesions of prelimbic and infralimbic cortex slow this learning, while performance on a nonmatching-to-position task is comparatively preserved. The authors interpret the pattern as evidence that medial PF cortex helps bias competition away from prepotent responses when a changed environment makes those responses less useful.
Study instruction:
- Compare the matching and nonmatching tasks and identify which one requires overriding the tendency to avoid a recently exploited site.
Key point:
- Medial PF circuitry can accelerate switching away from prepotent behaviour.
- The lesion effect is selective in the described comparison, not a general inability to learn spatial tasks.
19. Q: What distinct roles do the prelimbic and infralimbic cortices play in outcome-directed and habitual behaviour in rats?
A: Show answer
The chapter summarizes evidence that prelimbic cortex biases behaviour toward outcome-directed action, whereas infralimbic cortex biases behaviour toward habits. Prelimbic lesions make rats less sensitive to changes in reward value, so they continue responding for devalued outcomes. Infralimbic lesions have the opposite effect on the development or expression of habit: after extended training, the rats remain sensitive to outcome value when control animals would normally show habitual responding. The double dissociation supports the idea that these areas influence competing S–R–O and S–R systems. It does not mean that each area exclusively stores one association; the proposed role is to bias which learned control system prevails in a given context.
Study instruction:
- Build a two-column comparison of prelimbic and infralimbic lesion effects, including the expected outcome-devaluation result for each.
Key point:
- Prelimbic cortex favours outcome-directed control; infralimbic cortex favours habitual control.
- Their opposing biases can support flexible switching between behavioural modes.
20. Q: Why might two medial PF areas be needed to bias behaviour toward either habits or outcome-directed actions?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that prelimbic and infralimbic cortex may learn or represent contexts in which their preferred mode of behaviour is advantageous. A single general flexibility mechanism might not be sufficient if the animal needs to recognize when a stable environment favours rapid habitual responses and when a more volatile environment makes outcome-based evaluation more useful. Temporary inactivation of infralimbic cortex after overtraining reinstated outcome-dependent behaviour, which the authors interpret as consistent with loss of the context or bias that normally supports habit. They acknowledge that other interpretations are possible. The two-area account is therefore a functional hypothesis supported by lesion and inactivation patterns, not a complete demonstration of how context is encoded.
Study instruction:
- Explain how the same action could be adaptive in a stable resource context but disadvantageous after resource conditions change.
Key point:
- Opposing biases may allow the animal to select a behavioural mode appropriate to context.
- The context-learning explanation is plausible but not presented as conclusively established.
21. Q: How do intrinsic and extrinsic coordinate frames differ in the plus-maze tasks described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
An intrinsic rule specifies an action relative to the animal or its current movement, such as turning right at the junction. An extrinsic rule specifies a location in the environment, such as moving toward the east using external landmarks. In the plus-maze example, the correct turn under an extrinsic rule depends on the rat's starting arm and the spatial cue, whereas the intrinsic rule requires the same directional turn relative to the animal. The chapter warns against equating intrinsic guidance with habit. An animal can use either coordinate frame in an outcome-directed or habitual way. Coordinate frame describes how the action or goal is represented; habit describes whether behaviour depends on the predicted outcome's current value.
Study instruction:
- Draw a plus maze with two starting positions and mark the correct route under both rules; then classify each route separately for outcome sensitivity or habit.
Key point:
- Intrinsic guidance selects an action directly; extrinsic guidance selects a place or goal in the environment.
- Coordinate frame and habitual status are separate dimensions of behaviour.
22. Q: What does the evidence on switching between intrinsic and extrinsic rules suggest about prelimbic and infralimbic cortex?
A: Show answer
Rats can initially learn either rule, but inactivation of prelimbic and infralimbic cortex impairs switching from the established rule to the alternative one. The impairment is not simply a general inability to switch responses: rats can switch between odour choices in the comparison task, and in another study they can reverse choices within a rule while showing difficulty changing between rules. The chapter interprets the results as showing that medial PF cortex helps resolve competition between action-guidance systems. Prelimbic cortex supports the outcome-directed adjustment needed to learn a new rule, while infralimbic cortex may contribute to the influence of the established habitual rule. The timing of the deficit—more evident on the following day in one study—also suggests an effect on consolidating or applying the new rule rather than an immediate motor failure.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish rule switching from reversal within a rule and identify the control tasks used to test whether the deficit is general or specific.
Key point:
- Medial PF cortex contributes to selecting between competing action rules.
- Rule switching is not equivalent to changing a single response or reversing a choice within an unchanged rule.
23. Q: How does anterior cingulate cortex contribute to choices involving effort costs?
A: Show answer
In the T-maze task summarized in the chapter, rats choose between a small reward available without a barrier and a larger reward that requires climbing a substantial barrier. Control rats generally choose the larger reward despite the effort, whereas rats with anterior cingulate lesions choose the low-effort, smaller reward more often. The effect is not readily explained as simple laziness: in a separate task where rats must make increasing numbers of bar presses to earn food but do not choose between alternatives, anterior cingulate lesions do not produce the same deficit. The chapter therefore interprets the anterior cingulate contribution as evaluating effort in the context of a choice between actions and weighing that cost against expected benefit.
Study instruction:
- Compare the barrier-choice task with the repeated bar-press task and explain why the difference matters for ruling out a generalized loss of motivation.
Key point:
- Anterior cingulate cortex helps incorporate effort cost into action choices.
- Its lesion effect is especially evident when the animal must choose between alternatives with different costs and benefits.
24. Q: How does the chapter distinguish effort costs from delay costs in medial and orbital PF functions?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports a functional dissociation in rat lesion studies: anterior cingulate lesions disrupt choices that depend on effort costs, whereas orbital PF lesions disrupt choices involving delays before reward. This finding suggests that both medial and orbital PF regions contribute to cost–benefit evaluation but may specialize in different cost dimensions. The authors caution that the evidence comes from rats and that the same division should not automatically be assumed to apply identically in monkeys. Nor does the result establish that effort and delay are represented only in those respective areas. It supports a relative functional specialization within the broader network that evaluates predicted outcomes and guides choices.
Study instruction:
- Construct a comparison table for effort and delay manipulations, specifying the cost imposed, the relevant lesion result, and the limits of generalization.
Key point:
- The chapter associates anterior cingulate cortex more strongly with effort-based choice and orbital PF cortex with delay-based choice.
- The species and task limits of this dissociation should remain explicit.
25. Q: What did monkey action-reversal studies reveal about anterior cingulate cortex?
A: Show answer
In the action-reversal task, monkeys first learn one action on an object and then must switch to another action to obtain reward, with further reversals across trials. Lesions involving anterior cingulate cortex in the cingulate sulcus slow the acquisition of the new action. Trial-by-trial analysis showed that the impairment was not simply perseveration with the previously rewarded action. The lesioned monkeys were unusually poor at persisting with a correct action even after repeated positive feedback. The chapter therefore characterizes the deficit as reduced persistence with beneficial choices, rather than an inability to stop repeating an old response. This finding emphasizes the importance of analysing the sequence and informational value of feedback, not only average accuracy over blocks.
Study instruction:
- Explain why trial-by-trial analysis can distinguish perseveration from failure to maintain a newly successful action.
Key point:
- Anterior cingulate cortex supports efficient use of feedback to guide action reversals.
- The reported deficit included reduced persistence after positive feedback, not merely excessive persistence after negative feedback.
26. Q: Why is positive feedback important in the chapter's account of action reversal?
A: Show answer
Positive feedback indicates that the currently selected action has produced a beneficial outcome. The monkey lesion results summarized in the chapter show that anterior cingulate damage can impair the ability to use such feedback to maintain a newly correct action. The animals did not simply persist too strongly with an old action; instead, they often failed to continue selecting the action that had just been rewarded, even after several successful outcomes. This suggests that medial PF contributions to adaptive behaviour include both detecting when a strategy should change and sustaining a useful strategy when evidence supports it. A complete account must therefore consider how the system uses positive and negative feedback over time.
Study instruction:
- Review the trial sequence in the action-reversal study and identify what information each rewarded and unrewarded trial provides about the current action–outcome contingency.
Key point:
- Adaptive action selection requires using positive as well as negative feedback.
- Failure to persist with a successful action is distinct from perseveration with an unsuccessful one.
27. Q: What does resource volatility mean, and why does it matter for medial PF function?
A: Show answer
Resource volatility refers to how frequently or unpredictably the value or availability of a resource changes. In a stable environment, a well-learned habit can be efficient because repeated outcomes make rapid automatic responding advantageous. In a moderately volatile environment, outcomes become unreliable often enough that the animal benefits from evaluating predicted consequences and switching when conditions change. If the environment is completely unpredictable, however, even detailed outcome-based learning may have limited value.
