The chapter uses volatility to explain why medial PF regions may bias behaviour toward habits in some contexts and toward outcome-directed action in others, and why anterior cingulate signals may relate to monitoring whether continued exploitation remains worthwhile.
Study instruction:
- Compare stable, moderately volatile, and highly unpredictable resource environments; predict when habit, outcome evaluation, or exploratory switching would be most useful.
Key point:
- The best action strategy depends partly on how stable resource outcomes are over time.
- Medial PF biases are proposed to support context-sensitive adjustment rather than one universally preferred mode.
28. Q: How does the anterior cingulate cortex contribute to explore–exploit choices?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes foraging as a choice between continuing to exploit a current resource and switching to explore a new one. Cells in anterior cingulate cortex can encode the value of switching away from a diminishing resource. In the task discussed by Hayden and colleagues, a signal related to the option to switch rose toward a threshold associated with the choice, and longer delays before access to the new resource altered the threshold. Other studies described in the chapter found anterior cingulate activity around feedback that marked the beginning of an exploratory period, with that activity changing during subsequent exploitation. Together, these findings support a role in monitoring resource outcomes and evaluating when new information justifies changing course; they do not imply that every exploratory decision is controlled by a single cortical area.
Study instruction:
- Explain the trade-off between continued exploitation and switching, including the cost of travel or delay before the new resource becomes available.
Key point:
- Anterior cingulate activity is linked to evaluating and initiating shifts from exploitation to exploration.
- The evidence is consistent with a competitive threshold mechanism for stay–shift choices.
29. Q: What is the difference between a signed reward-prediction error and the unsigned outcome error discussed in the chapter?
A: Show answer
A signed reward-prediction error indicates the direction of the discrepancy between expected and received reward: the outcome may be better or worse than predicted. Such a signal can support reinforcement or weakening of a behaviour depending on the sign. The anterior cingulate signal described in the chapter is instead an unsigned indication that the outcome differed from expectation, without distinguishing whether it was an overestimate or underestimate. It therefore signals that adjustment may be needed but provides less direct information about whether to increase or decrease a particular response. The authors interpret this as an outcome-monitoring signal that may help determine whether to stay with a current course of action or consider an alternative.
Study instruction:
- Work through one better-than-expected and one worse-than-expected outcome; label the signed error and explain what an unsigned discrepancy signal would preserve or omit.
Key point:
- Signed errors encode direction; unsigned errors encode unexpectedness or discrepancy.
- The anterior cingulate signal described here is not equivalent to a conventional signed reward-prediction error.
30. Q: What do recordings of value-related activity in medial PF cortex show, and what remains uncertain?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that medial PF neurons can show activity related to reward magnitude, reward probability, and effort requirements such as the number of key presses. This is compatible with the proposed role in evaluating action outcomes and costs. However, some recording studies did not specify the cytoarchitecture of their recording sites, and lesion studies sometimes included both cingulate and adjacent premotor tissue. The authors therefore caution that the precise medial PF subregion responsible for a given signal is not always known. They also note that value-related signals occur in more than one PF region, so the presence of value coding does not by itself establish that a region performs the same function as another. Anatomical precision and task structure are essential to interpreting these findings.
Study instruction:
- Separate what the recordings establish about encoded variables from what they cannot establish about the exact cytoarchitectonic location or causal necessity.
Key point:
- Medial PF activity can reflect multiple components of expected outcome value.
- Recording location and task design limit how specifically these signals can be interpreted.
31. Q: How does the chapter distinguish action–outcome learning from object–outcome learning in primates?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that medial PF cortex is especially important when animals choose among actions based on their consequences, whereas orbital PF cortex contributes prominently when choices are made among objects or stimuli associated with outcomes. In the lesion comparison discussed by Rudebeck and colleagues, anterior cingulate damage impaired action-based learning but did not produce the same deficit in object-based learning. The distinction is not absolute: medial PF neurons can encode value-related information, and imaging can show medial PF activation during tasks involving stimulus choices. The authors therefore frame the distinction in terms of the dominant mapping and route to action, not a claim that each region processes only one kind of information.
Study instruction:
- Compare a task in which the animal selects between two actions on the same object with one in which it selects between two objects; identify the action–outcome or object–outcome mapping tested.
Key point:
- Medial PF is associated particularly with action–outcome choice; orbital PF is more associated with object–outcome choice.
- The specialization is relative and should not be interpreted as an exclusive division of all value processing.
32. Q: Why does the chapter treat rodent medial PF findings as informative but not conclusive for primates?
A: Show answer
Rodents and primates share homologues of the agranular medial PF regions, but their lineages have evolved separately for tens of millions of years. The chapter notes that relatively little was known about the corresponding agranular regions in monkeys, so rodent studies were used as an important evidence base while acknowledging the risk of species divergence. Similarities in broad behavioural effects do not guarantee identical connectivity or function. The authors therefore treat rodent findings as a provisional guide to agranular medial PF functions in primates, and they avoid extending rodent results to the granular medial areas, which rodents lack. Further comparative anatomical and functional work is needed to test the extent of conservation and divergence.
Study instruction:
- For each cross-species claim, identify the shared anatomical feature, the behavioural evidence, and the uncertainty introduced by evolutionary divergence.
Key point:
- Rodent data inform hypotheses about shared agranular regions but cannot settle all primate functions.
- Species comparison requires converging anatomical and functional evidence.
33. Q: How does the chapter evaluate claims that rodent medial PF cortex is homologous with primate mid-lateral PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Some researchers have proposed that rodent medial PF cortex corresponds to primate mid-lateral area 46, partly because lesions in both species can impair spatial-memory tasks and because some cell properties appear similar. The chapter argues that these features are too broadly distributed across frontal regions to diagnose homology. Shared performance deficits on a task do not establish that the underlying regions are evolutionarily equivalent, especially when the regions differ in cytoarchitecture. The authors distinguish homology, which concerns evolutionary correspondence, from analogy, which concerns similar function that may have evolved independently. They conclude that the evidence cited does not adequately demonstrate the proposed homology and that stronger arguments would require diagnostic anatomical and functional features.
Study instruction:
- Define homology and analogy, then assess why a shared deficit on a spatial task is insufficient evidence of homology on its own.
Key point:
- Similar task performance or lesion effects do not by themselves prove anatomical homology.
- Cytoarchitecture, connectivity, and multiple diagnostic features are needed for stronger comparative claims.
34. Q: What additional functions are proposed for the granular medial PF cortex in primates?
A: Show answer
The granular medial areas—medial area 9 and area 10—are proposed to elaborate the internal-signal functions of agranular medial PF cortex. The chapter suggests that they contribute to selecting between task rules, generating goals internally, evaluating self-generated choices at feedback time, and using a single prior event to guide a future choice. The evidence base is limited compared with that for agranular regions: it includes human imaging, a small number of monkey lesion and recording studies, and inferences from connectional anatomy. The chapter therefore presents these functions as a developing account rather than a fully settled map of granular medial PF specialization.
Study instruction:
- Organize the evidence for medial area 9 and area 10 under task-rule selection, self-generated goals, and single-event learning; mark which claims come from imaging, lesions, or recordings.
Key point:
- Granular medial PF cortex appears to extend action and rule selection using internally generated information.
- The evidence is comparatively sparse and should be interpreted cautiously.
35. Q: What is the proposed distinction between caudal and rostral medial PF contributions to task rules?
A: Show answer
Human imaging studies summarized in the chapter suggest that more caudal medial PF regions, including anterior cingulate cortex, are activated when feedback indicates which specific action should be taken. More rostral medial PF regions are activated when feedback indicates which task or rule should govern subsequent behaviour. This pattern is interpreted as a hierarchy in which rostral areas help select between task rules and influence specific action selection through more caudal regions. Monkey lesion observations involving polar PF cortex are also consistent with a role in resolving competition between rules and returning to an ongoing primary task after interruption. The evidence supports a possible division of labour, but it does not establish a simple one-way chain for every task.
Study instruction:
- Contrast feedback about “which action?” with feedback about “which task?” and explain why these may recruit different levels of the medial PF hierarchy.
Key point:
- Caudal medial PF regions are linked more closely to specific action guidance; rostral regions to task-rule selection.
- The proposed hierarchy is supported by converging but limited evidence.
36. Q: What does theta activity in medial areas 9 and 32 suggest about self-initiated behaviour?
A: Show answer
In the monkey study described in the chapter, theta oscillations in medial areas 9 and 32 increased before self-initiated movements and around the time of reward, and the two areas showed oscillatory coherence. Theta activity is described as rhythmic electrical activity in the 4–7 Hz range. The authors suggest that such oscillations could help organize information represented across cortical areas. The timing of the signal is relevant because it links self-initiated action and feedback, themes that recur in the chapter's discussion of self-generated goals. However, the findings do not by themselves specify the content of the information coordinated by theta activity or prove that the oscillation causes the subsequent choice.
Study instruction:
- State the frequency range and the two reported time points of increased theta power; distinguish observed correlation from proposed mechanism.
Key point:
- Theta activity was associated with both self-initiated movement and reward feedback.
- Its proposed role in organizing cortical information remains an interpretation of the reported observations.
37. Q: How do neurons in polar PF cortex represent self-generated goals at feedback time?
A: Show answer
In the strategy task described by Tsujimoto and colleagues, a cue instructed monkeys to repeat the previous successful saccade direction or shift to the opposite direction. The cue did not itself specify the spatial goal; the monkey had to combine it with memory of the previous goal. Neurons in polar PF cortex encoded the chosen goal around the time feedback arrived, and the signal was stronger for correctly executed strategy-based choices than for error trials. In a comparison task where spatial cues directly instructed the goal, the signal did not show the same persistence to delayed feedback. The chapter interprets this as evidence that polar PF cortex evaluates internally generated goals at outcome time, potentially helping assign feedback to the choice that produced it and guide future choices.
Study instruction:
- Compare the stay/shift strategy task with the externally cued delayed-response task; identify which information must be retrieved internally in each case.
Key point:
- Polar PF activity can represent a self-generated goal at feedback time.
- The timing and task comparison support a role in evaluating choices for future use, not simply maintaining a goal during the current movement.
38. Q: What is a single event in the chapter's account, and why is its structure important?
A: Show answer
An event is defined as the conjunction of a context, a goal choice, an action, and an outcome at a particular time and place. The event is more than an isolated stimulus or reward: it binds what the animal was doing, what it was trying to achieve, the circumstances in which it acted, and what followed. Such a representation can support later choices when a similar context recurs. The chapter proposes that granular PF cortex helps assign outcomes to self-generated goals and use one prior event to improve future decisions. This is especially valuable in environments where resource conditions change rapidly and learning from a single informative experience can be more adaptive than waiting for many repetitions.
Study instruction:
- For a remembered foraging episode, identify its context, selected goal, action, outcome, time, and place; explain how each element could guide a later choice.
Key point:
- An event binds context, goal, action, and outcome into a particular episode.
- Event-based learning can support rapid adjustment after one informative experience.
39. Q: What does the object-in-place scenes task reveal about single-event learning and polar PF cortex?
A: Show answer
In the object-in-place scenes task, monkeys encounter many distinctive background scenes, each containing two objects in fixed locations. They must learn which object to select in each scene to receive reward. Because many scenes occur before any one scene repeats, successful performance requires rapid learning of the specific context–choice–outcome relation. Monkeys with polar PF lesions show a marked impairment on the second presentation of each scene, when they must retrieve the choice made during the first encounter. Their learning rate after that point is described as more comparable to controls. The chapter interprets the second-trial deficit as evidence for impaired memory or use of a single prior event, while acknowledging that the task cannot by itself distinguish retrieval failure from failure to use the retrieved event to guide choice.
Study instruction:
- Explain why the second presentation is diagnostically important and identify the two alternative explanations for the lesion effect.
Key point:
- Polar PF lesions impair use of a single prior scene-specific experience on the next encounter.
- The task does not fully separate event retrieval from applying retrieved information to a new choice.
40. Q: Why does the chapter avoid calling event memory in monkeys “episodic memory”?
A: Show answer
In cognitive psychology, episodic memory often implies conscious recollection or awareness of a personally experienced event. The chapter avoids applying that term to nonhuman monkeys because the authors do not claim that monkeys possess human-like awareness of remembered episodes. They instead use “event memory” to describe the ability to retain and use a particular conjunction of context, goal, action, and outcome. For human participants, autobiographical recall can be assessed more directly by asking them to remember events from their lives, and imaging studies described in the chapter associate such retrieval with medial area 9 or area 10 as well as hippocampal and retrosplenial regions. The terminology is intended to avoid making unsupported claims about animal consciousness while preserving the functional comparison.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between a functional claim that an animal retains event information and a stronger claim that it consciously recollects an episode.
Key point:
- “Event memory” avoids assuming human-like conscious recollection in nonhuman animals.
- Human autobiographical-memory findings and monkey event-learning findings are related but not identical evidence.
41. Q: What are the main limitations of the evidence reviewed for granular medial PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter explicitly acknowledges that evidence for medial area 9 and area 10 is sparse. There are relatively few monkey lesion and neurophysiological studies, and much of the human evidence comes from functional imaging, which identifies task-related activation but does not by itself establish necessity or precise causal direction. Rodent research cannot fill the gap because rodents lack homologues of these granular regions. Even the monkey lesion findings may not isolate a single cytoarchitectonic field, and the recording evidence is limited in number. The authors therefore infer that granular areas elaborate agranular functions, but present the proposal as provisional and dependent on further targeted studies.
Study instruction:
- For each granular-area claim, record the species, method, anatomical precision, and whether the evidence is correlational or causal.
Key point:
- The granular medial PF account is promising but based on a relatively small evidence base.
- Imaging, lesion, and recording methods answer different questions and should not be treated as interchangeable.
42. Q: How can lesion, recording, and imaging studies be combined without overstating what each proves?
A: Show answer
Lesion studies test whether damage to a region disrupts performance, but their interpretation depends on lesion extent, neighbouring tissue, compensation, and the task used. Single-cell recordings reveal variables correlated with neural activity and their timing, but correlation does not alone establish causal necessity or the precise computational role of the recorded population. Functional imaging identifies regions whose activity changes with task conditions, but activation can reflect several component processes and does not prove that the activated region is uniquely responsible. The chapter illustrates these limits by noting broad lesion boundaries, recording sites without complete cytoarchitectonic descriptions, and the difficulty of distinguishing retrieval from use of an event memory. Strong conclusions require converging evidence with anatomically and behaviourally precise designs.
Study instruction:
- Take one claim from the chapter and state what lesion, recording, and imaging evidence each contributes, then identify the unresolved causal or anatomical question.
Key point:
- Lesions, recordings, and imaging provide complementary rather than interchangeable evidence.
- Anatomical precision and task controls determine how far a functional conclusion can be taken.
43. Q: How do the hippocampus, amygdala, and medial premotor areas work together in the chapter's proposed mechanism?
A: Show answer
The hippocampal system supplies spatial and event-related information, including remembered locations and action-related episodes. The amygdala contributes updated values of predicted outcomes in light of current needs, including positive benefits and negative costs. Medial premotor connections provide a route through which selected action biases can influence movement. The medial PF cortex integrates or uses these internal signals to bias competition among actions and rules, while its outputs can also influence autonomic-control structures. The chapter's model is therefore a network account: no single connection explains the full function, and the contribution of each pathway depends on the information it carries and the stage of action selection it influences.
Study instruction:
- Build a flow diagram linking hippocampal memory, amygdala valuation, medial PF competition, premotor influence, and behavioural outcome; label the information carried at each stage.
Key point:
- Memory, current value, and motor access are complementary ingredients in medial PF action selection.
- The chapter explains function through interacting pathways rather than an isolated “decision centre.”
44. Q: Why can other cortical regions not simply perform the same function as the medial PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that other regions lack one or more elements of the medial PF connectional fingerprint. Many PF regions have weaker hippocampal links, limiting access to the same spatial and event-related information. Posterior parietal cortex has direct premotor connections but lacks substantial amygdala input, at least in the account presented. Superior and inferior temporal cortices have amygdala connections but lack the same direct premotor links. The medial PF cortex is distinctive because it combines access to memory, current outcome valuation, and routes to influence action. This is a network-based explanation of functional specialization; it does not imply that other regions cannot contribute to related computations or that the medial PF cortex acts independently of them.
Study instruction:
- Compare medial PF cortex with posterior parietal and temporal cortex using three dimensions: hippocampal input, amygdala input, and premotor output.
Key point:
- Functional specialization depends on the combination of connections, not one isolated pathway.
- Other regions can contribute to behaviour but do not share the same complete connectional profile.
45. Q: How should the chapter's overall proposal be applied to a practical foraging scenario?
A: Show answer
Imagine an animal repeatedly visiting a food patch that has usually been productive. In a stable environment, a learned habitual action may allow quick, efficient returns. If the patch begins to yield less food, the animal must compare the expected benefit of continuing with the cost of effort or delay and the potential benefit of moving elsewhere. Hippocampal information can help represent alternative locations and remembered events; amygdala-related valuation can reflect current hunger and the changing worth of food; medial PF circuits can bias the competition between staying with the current action and switching to another. In primates, a remembered single event may provide useful evidence about a new location after one encounter. This scenario combines the chapter's proposed mechanisms without assuming that one structure independently calculates every variable.
Study instruction:
- Apply the model to a changing resource: specify the remembered event, current need, predicted benefits and costs, competing actions, and feedback that would justify switching.
Key point:
- Medial PF cortex helps select and revise actions using memory and current outcome value.
- Adaptive foraging requires balancing exploitation, exploration, effort, and context.
46. Q: What are the most important unresolved questions raised by Chapter 3?
A: Show answer
The chapter leaves several issues open. More comparative work is needed to establish how closely rodent and primate agranular medial PF connections and functions correspond. The precise contributions of individual medial subregions remain uncertain where lesions span multiple fields or recordings lack cytoarchitectonic localization. The granular medial PF account needs more causal and neurophysiological evidence, especially to distinguish retrieval of a single event from using that event to guide a choice. It is also unresolved how different internal signals are integrated dynamically, how task-rule selection interacts with action selection, and how the proposed mechanisms generalize across species and natural behaviours. These are not flaws that invalidate the framework; they define the experiments needed to refine it.
Study instruction:
- Turn each limitation into a testable research question, specifying the species, target area, method, behavioural task, and alternative explanations that should be controlled.
Key point:
- The chapter offers a coherent connection-based model while acknowledging important anatomical and causal gaps.
- Future work must improve subregional precision and test the proposed mechanisms directly.
Rows: 46
Table 4
Orbital Prefrontal Cortex: Sensory Outcomes, Value, and Choice
1. Q: What is the central function of the orbital prefrontal cortex described in this chapter?
A: Show answer
The orbital prefrontal cortex (orbital PF cortex or OFC) helps an animal evaluate and choose among external sensory stimuli, especially objects, by predicting the specific outcomes associated with them and evaluating those outcomes in relation to current biological needs. Its sensory inputs allow it to represent what an object is likely to produce; its interactions with the amygdala help update how desirable that outcome currently is. In primates, the granular OFC also supports unusually precise credit assignment: linking a particular outcome to the particular object choice that caused it, potentially from a single event. The chapter presents this as a complementary function to medial PF cortex, which is more strongly associated with choices among actions and action rules.
Study instruction:
- Explain the OFC function using one example that separates the identity of an expected outcome from its current motivational value.
Key point:
- OFC biases choices among stimuli using predicted outcomes and current needs.
- Granular OFC improves event-specific choice–outcome learning in primates.
Source links:
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Complementary prefrontal networks
- Walton et al. (2010), Orbitofrontal cortex in contingent learning
2. Q: How does the orbital PF cortex differ from the medial PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter's functional distinction is one of emphasis, not absolute isolation. The orbital PF cortex is especially important when an externally presented object or stimulus predicts an outcome; the medial PF cortex is especially important when an action or an action rule predicts an outcome. The orbital region therefore integrates object-related sensory information with outcome identity and motivational value, whereas medial regions contribute more directly to selecting actions using internally generated signals, action histories, and current needs. The two systems are densely interconnected and jointly support real foraging, where an animal must choose both what to approach and what to do with it. Recordings can reveal representations in both regions even when lesion experiments show that one region is necessary for a particular task.
Study instruction:
- Compare an object-choice task with an action-choice task and identify which association each task requires the subject to learn.
Key point:
- Orbital PF cortex emphasizes stimulus–outcome relationships.
- Medial PF cortex emphasizes action–outcome relationships, with substantial interaction between systems.
Source links:
- Bouret and Richmond (2010), Motivational values in orbital and ventromedial PFC
- Camille, Tsuchida and Fellows (2011), Stimulus-value and action-value learning
3. Q: What is the difference between agranular and granular orbital prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
Agranular and granular describe cytoarchitectonic organization, but in this chapter they also mark an important comparative distinction. The agranular OFC is present in rodents and primates and is treated as an older, evolutionarily conserved component. It is strongly connected with olfactory, gustatory, visceral, and amygdala-related systems and supports stimulus–outcome processing. The granular OFC is prominent in primates and receives richer visual and object-related input, including from inferior temporal and perirhinal cortex. The authors argue that this richer convergence supports more specific representations of objects and more accurate assignment of outcomes to individual choices. The distinction should not be reduced to a claim that all OFC functions are unique to primates; rodents display important outcome-directed abilities too.
Study instruction:
- Draw a two-column comparison of agranular and granular OFC, listing their principal inputs and the functions attributed to them in the chapter.
Key point:
- Agranular OFC supports core sensory-outcome functions across mammals.
- Granular OFC adds richer primate object representations and event-specific credit assignment.
Source links:
- Carmichael and Price (1995), Limbic connections of orbital and medial PFC
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Complementary prefrontal networks
4. Q: Which cortical fields and anatomical subdivisions are included in the chapter's account of the orbital PF cortex?
A: Show answer
In macaques, the chapter discusses orbital areas 11, 13, and 14, with area 11 lying more rostrally and areas 13 and 14 more caudally. Most of the primate orbital PF cortex is granular, but caudal portions of areas 13 and 14 and adjacent anterior insular cortex are agranular. The chapter follows Carmichael and Price in including these agranular orbital and insular territories in its broader orbital PF description. It excludes orbital-surface extensions of area 12/47 and orbital portions of area 10 from the definition used in the book. These are operational anatomical boundaries rather than universal labels; comparisons across studies must check each study's atlas, cytoarchitectonic criteria, and lesion reconstruction.
Study instruction:
- Locate areas 11, 13, and 14 on a macaque orbital-surface diagram and note which portions are described as agranular.
Key point:
- The chapter's OFC is not identical to every broader definition of ventral or orbital frontal cortex.
- Anatomical definitions and lesion boundaries matter when comparing studies.
Source links:
- Carmichael and Price (1994), Architectonic subdivision of macaque orbital and medial PFC
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Complementary prefrontal networks
5. Q: How do the connections of the orbital PF cortex support its role in evaluating objects?
A: Show answer
The OFC receives convergent information from several sensory and limbic systems. Olfactory and gustatory inputs convey smell and taste; visceral inputs convey information about the body's internal state; oral somatosensory inputs convey properties of food in contact with the mouth, lips, and tongue; and visual inputs from inferior temporal and perirhinal regions convey object features such as shape, colour, and texture. Connections with the amygdala allow outcome representations to be evaluated in relation to motivational state. This arrangement makes the OFC well placed to represent not merely a generic reward, but a particular food or fluid with sensory properties and a current value. The strength and distribution of these connections differ across orbital subdivisions.
Study instruction:
- Trace each sensory input to the kind of information it contributes to an expected food or fluid outcome.
Key point:
- OFC combines external object information with internal bodily and motivational signals.
- Its connectivity explains why it can evaluate sensory objects in context.
Source links:
- Carmichael and Price (1995), Sensory and premotor connections of orbital and medial PFC
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Complementary prefrontal networks
6. Q: What is meant by a high-dimensional sensory representation in the OFC?
A: Show answer
A high-dimensional representation preserves several distinguishable features of an outcome rather than compressing them immediately into a single score. A food can be represented through its visual appearance, texture, smell, taste, oral feel, and associated visceral consequences. Conjunctions of these features help distinguish one specific outcome from another that may have similar general desirability. Such specificity is useful when a choice predicts a particular food and the animal later needs to know which food was obtained or which one has become less desirable. The chapter argues that primate granular OFC can construct especially rich object-outcome representations because of its expanded visual inputs and its convergence with taste, smell, and visceral pathways.
Study instruction:
- Describe one food using at least four sensory dimensions and explain why preserving those dimensions could improve choice learning.
Key point:
- High-dimensional coding represents what a specific outcome is like.
- Feature conjunctions support discrimination among outcomes with similar general value.
Source links:
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Complementary prefrontal networks
- Critchley and Rolls (1996), Hunger and satiety effects on OFC neurons
7. Q: What is a stimulus–outcome association, and how is it different from an action–outcome association?
A: Show answer
A stimulus–outcome association links an external cue or object to the outcome it predicts: seeing a particular object may predict a particular food. An action–outcome association links an action to its consequence: pressing one lever may produce a specific food. Both associations can support goal-directed behaviour, but they answer different predictive questions. In the chapter's framework, orbital PF cortex is especially important for using stimulus–outcome mappings to guide object choices, whereas medial PF cortex is especially important for action–outcome mappings. The distinction is tested by changing object contingencies separately from action contingencies. It does not mean that OFC never represents actions or that medial PF cortex never represents objects; it concerns which region is necessary for particular forms of learning and choice.
Study instruction:
- For three everyday examples, label the cue or object, the action, and the predicted outcome separately.
Key point:
- Stimulus–outcome asks what an object predicts.
- Action–outcome asks what a behaviour produces.
Source links:
- Ostlund and Balleine (2007), OFC outcome encoding in Pavlovian conditioning
- Camille, Tsuchida and Fellows (2011), Stimulus-value and action-value learning
8. Q: Why does the chapter emphasize externally prompted choices when describing orbital PF cortex function?
A: Show answer
An external object or cue can prompt a choice by signalling what outcome is available. The OFC receives detailed sensory information about such stimuli and can combine it with learned predictions and current motivational value. This contrasts with choices that must be generated more internally, such as selecting an action without a sensory instruction. The distinction is useful experimentally because a task can hold the motor response relatively constant while changing the object selected, or hold the stimulus constant while changing the action. However, natural behaviour usually requires both systems: an animal must identify a desirable object, choose an action to obtain it, and update its behaviour as the environment or its needs change.
Study instruction:
- Design a simple experiment in which the object changes but the response movement remains the same, then explain what it isolates.
Key point:
- External cues make stimulus-based choice demands explicit.
- Natural foraging recruits orbital and medial PF systems together.
Source links:
- Tsujimoto, Genovesio and Wise (2011), Strategy signals in dorsolateral and orbital PFC
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Complementary prefrontal networks
9. Q: Why are the specific sensory properties of an outcome important for adaptive behaviour?
A: Show answer
Knowing only that an outcome is rewarding may be insufficient. An animal often needs to distinguish which food or fluid a cue predicts because different outcomes can have different nutritional, sensory, and motivational consequences. Specific sensory representations allow the animal to predict the taste or identity of a food, recognize when it has already consumed enough of that food, and choose another option that better meets current needs. They also support learning about distinct outcomes when two rewards are equally palatable but differ in taste or other features. The OFC's role is therefore not reducible to representing a generic amount of reward; it contributes information about the identity and expected properties of the outcome.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a generic reward signal would be insufficient in a choice between two equally preferred foods that have different sensory identities.
Key point:
- Outcome identity and outcome value are related but separable.
- Sensory specificity enables selective updating when one particular outcome changes in value.
Source links:
- Gallagher, McMahan and Schoenbaum (1999), OFC and incentive value
- Critchley and Rolls (1996), Sensory-specific satiety in OFC neurons
10. Q: What is outcome devaluation, and what does it reveal about OFC function?
A: Show answer
Outcome devaluation is an experimental procedure that reduces the current desirability of a previously learned reward, often by feeding an animal that food to satiety or pairing it with illness. The critical test then asks whether the animal changes its response to a cue or object that predicts the now-devalued outcome, usually without receiving that outcome during the test. A normal devaluation effect indicates that the animal can retrieve the predicted outcome and use its current value to guide behaviour. When OFC damage abolishes this effect, the animal may continue responding to cues associated with the reward despite its reduced value. The test therefore probes the use of updated outcome expectations, not merely whether the animal can detect or consume the reward.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the training phase, devaluation phase, and extinction test phase in a written outline of a devaluation experiment.
Key point:
- Devaluation tests whether current outcome value can influence a previously learned cue or choice.
- Testing without reward delivery helps separate prediction from new learning during the test.
Source links:
- Gallagher, McMahan and Schoenbaum (1999), OFC and incentive value
- Pickens et al. (2003), OFC and amygdala in reinforcer devaluation
11. Q: What did the rat conditioned-taste-aversion experiment by Gallagher and colleagues show?
A: Show answer
Rats first learned that a light predicted food and approached it when it appeared. The researchers then paired consumption of that food with lithium-chloride-induced gastrointestinal illness, creating a conditioned taste aversion and reducing the food's value. When later tested after the illness had passed, control rats reduced their approach to the light, showing that they used the changed value of the predicted food. Rats with OFC lesions continued approaching the light more often. The result suggests that the lesioned animals had difficulty using the stimulus–outcome association together with the updated value of the outcome. Because acquisition of the initial cue response and taste-aversion learning were assessed separately, the deficit is not simply described as an inability to learn the original cue or illness association.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the experiment and identify which phase establishes the cue association and which phase changes the reward's value.
Key point:
- OFC damage can impair the use of updated incentive value to guide cue-driven behaviour.
- Acquisition and later value-guided performance must be distinguished.
Source links:
- Gallagher, McMahan and Schoenbaum (1999), OFC and incentive value
12. Q: How do the OFC and basolateral amygdala contribute to outcome devaluation?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes evidence that lesions of either the agranular OFC or the basolateral amygdala can disrupt the normal devaluation effect in rats. In primates, granular OFC and amygdala lesions can also impair choices that should reflect a food's newly reduced value. The authors interpret these findings as evidence for an interacting circuit: the amygdala contributes to updating motivational value as biological state changes, while OFC uses sensory and associative representations to bring that updated value to bear on a particular cue or object. The structures should not be treated as interchangeable. They have different connectivity and can make dissociable contributions to reversal and feedback-guided learning, as lesion studies demonstrate.
Study instruction:
- Compare one finding from an OFC lesion study with one from an amygdala lesion study and identify the shared behavioural deficit and possible distinct contributions.
Key point:
- Adaptive valuation depends on interactions between OFC and amygdala.
- Similar task impairments do not establish identical neural functions.
Source links:
- Pickens et al. (2003), OFC and basolateral amygdala in devaluation
- Izquierdo et al. (2004), Orbital PF lesions and reward-guided choice
13. Q: What contribution may the agranular insular cortex make to remembering outcome identity?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews rat studies suggesting that the insular cortex contributes to retaining or retrieving specific sensory information about expected foods. In one experiment, rats with lesions involving gustatory and adjacent insular cortex could avoid an action associated with a devalued food when that food was actually delivered during choice training, but during an extinction test they pressed both levers similarly. The interpretation was that they had difficulty recalling which specific food each lever predicted when no food was present to provide direct sensory information. Other experiments found impaired anticipation of a more valuable delayed sucrose solution despite preserved ability to discriminate the solutions. Because some lesions targeted gustatory cortex and extended into neighbouring insular tissue, the chapter treats precise localization cautiously rather than attributing the effect exclusively to one sharply bounded field.
Study instruction:
- Explain why an extinction test is useful for testing memory of a predicted food's sensory identity, and identify the anatomical limitation of the lesion evidence.
Key point:
- Insular regions may help maintain specific outcome information used by OFC-related choice systems.
- Lesion spread limits claims about a single narrowly defined subregion.
Source links:
- Balleine and Dickinson (2000), Insular cortex and incentive memory
- Kesner and Gilbert (2007), Agranular insula and reward anticipation
14. Q: How does OFC involvement differ between Pavlovian stimulus–outcome learning and instrumental action–outcome learning?
A: Show answer
Pavlovian learning links a cue to an outcome that follows without requiring a particular instrumental action; instrumental learning links an action to an outcome that the action produces. Ostlund and Balleine found that OFC lesions impaired outcome-specific Pavlovian-to-instrumental transfer but did not abolish the effect of instrumental outcome devaluation in their task. This supports a role for OFC in representing specific outcomes predicted by cues, while cautioning against assuming that every form of action–outcome knowledge depends on OFC. The distinction is task- and training-dependent, and other chapter experiments show OFC contributions to choices among objects. A strong interpretation specifies the association being tested rather than treating all reward-guided behaviour as one process.
Study instruction:
- Describe the contingency in a Pavlovian task and an instrumental task, then state what an OFC lesion result would mean for each.
Key point:
- Pavlovian cue prediction and instrumental action control are separable learning demands.
- OFC involvement is strongest when specific outcomes predicted by stimuli guide behaviour.
Source links:
- Ostlund and Balleine (2007), OFC outcome encoding in Pavlovian conditioning
15. Q: What is conditioned reinforcement, and why is it relevant to OFC function?
A: Show answer
A conditioned reinforcer is a previously neutral cue that acquires the ability to reinforce a new response because it predicts a reward. The cue can be valuable because it evokes a representation of a particular expected outcome, not merely because it produces a general positive feeling. Burke and colleagues showed that OFC involvement is important when conditioned reinforcement depends on cues that evoke specific outcome representations. This fits the chapter's emphasis on sensory-specific outcome knowledge: a cue can guide behaviour by making the expected reward identity available to the decision system. It also helps explain why reward-predicting cues can exert strong control even when the primary reward is not immediately delivered.
Study instruction:
- Create an example of a conditioned reinforcer and distinguish the cue's learned predictive meaning from delivery of the primary reward.
Key point:
- Some cues reinforce behaviour by representing a specific expected outcome.
- OFC contributes to using such outcome-specific cue information.
Source links:
- Burke et al. (2008), OFC and specific conditioned rewards
16. Q: What role does the agranular OFC play in decisions involving delayed rewards?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes evidence that rat OFC lesions alter choices between a smaller immediate reward and a larger delayed reward. In the T-maze study by Rudebeck and colleagues, lesioned rats chose the immediate reward more often, which can be described as increased impulsive choice. Other experiments using different response formats produced more patient-looking choices. The chapter therefore does not assign the rat OFC a simple one-directional role in patience or impulsivity. Instead, it concludes that the region contributes to assessing delay-related costs and to biasing choice under particular task conditions. The observed direction can depend on how the delay is signalled, how options are presented, and individual differences in baseline choice behaviour.
Study instruction:
- Compare two delay-choice task designs and list the design features that could change the apparent effect of an OFC manipulation.
Key point:
- OFC contributes to evaluating delay costs, not to a universal impulsive or patient response.
- Task design and individual variation are essential to interpretation.
Source links:
- Rudebeck et al. (2006), Separate neural pathways for decision costs
- Roesch and Olson (2005), OFC activity and the value of time
17. Q: Why can OFC lesions or inactivation appear to produce either impulsive or patient choices in different experiments?
A: Show answer
The apparent contradiction arises because delay-choice tasks do not all impose the same information-processing demands. The chapter contrasts a T-maze study in which OFC-lesioned rats preferred the immediate reward with lever and goal-box studies in which lesioned rats chose delayed rewards more often. Zeeb and colleagues proposed that the effect of OFC inactivation depends partly on whether a clear cue bridges the waiting period and on the animal's baseline impulsivity. In their study, inactivation increased immediate-reward choice when delays were clearly cued; with uncued delays it decreased immediate-reward choice, but this latter effect was confined to rats already strongly biased toward impulsive choice. The defensible conclusion is that OFC contributes to delay assessment, while the direction of the behavioural change depends on task structure and individual strategy.
Study instruction:
- When reading a delay-discounting paper, record delay cues, reward amounts, response requirements, baseline preference, and the precise neural manipulation before interpreting the result.
Key point:
- Behavioural labels such as impulsive and patient depend on the task and the subject's baseline.
- Conflicting outcomes call for analysis of design and mechanism, not selective reporting.
Source links:
- Zeeb, Floresco and Winstanley (2010), OFC and impulsive choice
- Rudebeck et al. (2006), Separate neural pathways for decision costs
18. Q: How should delay discounting be distinguished from sensitivity to risk?
A: Show answer
Delay discounting describes a reduction in the value assigned to an outcome as the time before its receipt increases. But a preference for an immediately available resource does not necessarily mean that the subject misvalues a distant reward. Waiting may expose the subject to uncertainty that a larger payoff will remain available or that its value will not decrease. The chapter discusses a model by Hayden and Platt in which the value of a risky option depends on the expected time of the larger payoff and the risk that the payoff will decrease. The model accounted for choices in a monkey gambling task. A complete experiment should therefore distinguish the temporal delay itself from the probability that the delayed payoff will be lost or reduced. The same observed choice can arise from different computations, so delay and risk should be manipulated independently when the aim is to identify the mechanism.
Study instruction:
- Design a two-factor choice task that varies waiting time and payoff uncertainty independently, and predict how each factor could affect preference.
Key point:
- Choosing an immediate option may reflect adaptive risk sensitivity, not only temporal discounting.
- Delay and uncertainty should be experimentally separated.
Source links:
- Hayden and Platt (2007), Temporal discounting and foraging theory
- Rudebeck et al. (2006), Separate neural pathways for decision costs
19. Q: How could accumulator or racetrack models explain the influence of OFC on choice?
A: Show answer
In an accumulator model, evidence favouring each available choice builds over time until one option reaches a response threshold. The chapter proposes that outcome predictions and current motivational valuations can contribute evidence to the accumulator representing a stimulus choice. Stronger stimulus–outcome associations or greater current value can increase the rate of accumulation or alter the threshold, making that option more likely to win the competition. The model provides a computational bridge between OFC representations and observable choice without requiring the OFC itself to issue a complete motor command. It is an explanatory framework, not proof that every OFC effect is implemented by one specific accumulator architecture.
Study instruction:
- Draw two competing accumulators and annotate where predicted outcome identity and current value could influence their activity.
Key point:
- OFC can bias the evidence supporting a stimulus choice.
- Accumulator models describe a possible mechanism rather than an established unique circuit implementation.
Source links:
- Bouret and Richmond (2010), Motivational value signals in prefrontal cortex
- Rudebeck et al. (2006), Separate neural pathways for decision costs
20. Q: Why should the rat agranular OFC not automatically be treated as the equivalent of the entire primate OFC?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that similar lesion effects or cell responses do not establish anatomical homology. Rat OFC is agranular, lies adjacent to allocortical regions, and has connectional properties unlike the granular portions of primate OFC. Primate OFC contains granular fields with expanded visual and object-related connections. A rat model can therefore illuminate functions of conserved agranular circuitry without representing all of the specializations found in primate granular OFC. Claims of homology require diagnostic anatomical and connectional evidence, not simply a shared broad label or similar performance on one behavioural task. This distinction is especially important when translating findings from rodents to primates or humans.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a cross-species claim by separating cytoarchitecture, connectivity, topology, physiology, and behavioural evidence.
Key point:
- Functional similarity alone does not prove anatomical homology.
- Rodent OFC findings should be mapped to the appropriate primate subregion cautiously.
Source links:
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Orbital and medial prefrontal networks
- Rudebeck and Murray (2011), Primate OFC subregions
21. Q: What do probabilistic-outcome tasks reveal about granular OFC in monkeys?
A: Show answer
In probabilistic tasks, each object or stimulus has a changing probability of producing reward. Monkeys must learn which stimulus is currently the best option and update their choices as probabilities change. Rudebeck and colleagues found that monkeys with granular OFC lesions adjusted choices among stimuli more slowly than controls, while performance on comparable action-choice tasks was much less affected. This supports a selective role in learning or updating stimulus–outcome mappings. The task is informative because reward probabilities can be manipulated independently of the animal's choice history, allowing researchers to distinguish learning about the environment from the consequences of its own actions.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a probabilistic task differs from a deterministic reversal task and identify what information each provides about learning.
Key point:
- Granular OFC is important for adapting object choices to changing reward probabilities.
- Comparing stimulus and action versions helps identify the association being learned.
Source links:
- Rudebeck et al. (2008), Frontal subregions in action and stimulus choice
- Walton et al. (2010), Contingent learning in macaques
22. Q: What is the three-arm bandit task, and why is it useful for studying credit assignment?
A: Show answer
In a three-arm bandit task, a subject chooses among three stimuli, each associated with a different probability of reward. The probabilities can change over time, requiring the subject to discover which option currently offers the highest payoff. In the Walton et al. experiment discussed in the chapter, the reward schedule was predetermined rather than caused by the monkey's choices. This design allowed researchers to examine whether a reward following a particular choice was attributed to that choice or blended into a broader history of recent choices. By changing the relative values of options and analysing trial-by-trial behaviour, the task tests the precision of choice–outcome learning rather than only general flexibility.
Study instruction:
- Sketch three changing reward-probability curves and mark a point at which a formerly poor option becomes the best option.
Key point:
- The bandit task measures learning under changing probabilistic contingencies.
- Predetermined outcomes help isolate assignment of feedback to specific choices.
Source links:
- Walton et al. (2010), Separable learning systems and contingent learning
23. Q: What does an object reversal task measure, and what do OFC lesions do to performance?
A: Show answer
In an object reversal task, an animal learns that one object is rewarded and another is not; after learning, the reward contingencies switch without a new explicit instruction. The animal must use feedback to update which object predicts reward. Large aspiration lesions encompassing the OFC can produce substantial deficits on serial object reversal, whereas restricted excitotoxic lesions targeting either areas 11/13 or area 14 did not reproduce the same serial-reversal impairment in Rudebeck and Murray's study. Initial discrimination can remain relatively intact because it requires learning a simple positive-versus-neutral distinction, whereas reversal requires updating a previously established stimulus–outcome relationship. The contrast suggests that broad lesion effects may depend on combined subregional damage or disruption of fibres passing through the region, rather than on one small field alone.
Study instruction:
- Separate acquisition, reversal, and serial-reversal phases in a task diagram, and identify what each phase tests.
Key point:
- Whole-region aspiration lesions can impair reversal even when restricted subregion lesions do not.
- Reversal performance depends on lesion extent and potentially on fibres of passage.
Source links:
- Izquierdo et al. (2004), Orbital PF lesions and reward-guided choice
- Rudebeck and Murray (2011), Subregional OFC lesions
24. Q: What is extinction learning, and how does it differ from reversal learning?
A: Show answer
In extinction, a previously rewarded stimulus or response is presented but the expected reward is no longer delivered. The subject must reduce responding because the old predictive relationship no longer holds. In reversal learning, by contrast, the formerly unrewarded alternative becomes rewarded, so the subject must shift toward a new positive option. Both tasks involve changes in expected outcomes, but their feedback structure differs: extinction removes the reward, whereas reversal redirects reward to another option. The chapter reports that restricted lesions of area 14 can mildly slow extinction of responding to a previously rewarded object. Because extinction can be influenced by several learning systems and lesion boundaries, it should not be treated as a direct synonym for response inhibition.
Study instruction:
- Write the contingency table for acquisition, extinction, and reversal and identify the feedback available in each condition.
Key point:
- Extinction suppresses an old prediction; reversal requires learning a new alternative.
- Similar behavioural slowing can arise from different learning demands.
Source links:
- Rudebeck and Murray (2011), Subregional OFC lesions and reward-guided behaviour
- Rudebeck and Murray (2008), Object reversal learning
25. Q: Why does the chapter challenge the traditional explanation that OFC reversal deficits are simply perseveration?
A: Show answer
Perseveration means continuing to make a previously reinforced response despite evidence that it is no longer appropriate. The human lesion evidence reviewed in the chapter shows a more specific problem: people with OFC damage use negative feedback to leave a previously correct choice nearly normally, but they use positive feedback inefficiently to maintain a newly correct choice. Their impairment can therefore involve insufficient persistence with a newly rewarded response rather than a general inability to inhibit the old one. The distinction matters because the same total error count can conceal different learning processes. Researchers must examine the sequence of choices and feedback, not infer inhibition failure from reversal errors alone.
Study instruction:
- Compare errors after negative feedback with choice persistence after one, two, or more rewarded correct trials in the human lesion task.
Key point:
- Reversal impairment is not automatically perseveration.
- Trial-by-trial feedback analysis can reveal deficient use of positive evidence.
Source links:
- Camille, Tsuchida and Fellows (2011), Human stimulus-value and action-value learning
- Rudebeck and Murray (2008), Amygdala and OFC in object reversal
26. Q: What does the use of positive and negative feedback reveal about OFC-dependent learning?
A: Show answer
Positive feedback is evidence that a chosen stimulus produced the expected reward; negative feedback is evidence that the expected reward did not occur. In the human lesion study described in the chapter, participants with OFC damage were relatively able to switch away from a previously rewarded choice after negative feedback but were less able to use positive outcomes to reinforce a newly correct choice. This pattern indicates a problem in learning from successful choices, rather than a global inability to detect feedback. A careful analysis should distinguish whether the subject notices an outcome, attributes it to the correct choice, updates its estimate of value, and maintains the choice on later trials. Those stages can fail independently.
Study instruction:
- Build a trial sequence containing an error followed by several rewarded correct choices and predict the next choice for a control and an OFC-lesioned participant.
Key point:
- OFC contributes to learning from positive feedback in a choice-specific way.
- Outcome detection, causal attribution, and response persistence are distinct processes.
Source links:
- Camille, Tsuchida and Fellows (2011), Stimulus-value and action-value learning
27. Q: What does the human lesion study by Camille and colleagues add to the animal evidence?
A: Show answer
Camille and colleagues compared people with OFC damage, people with dorsal anterior cingulate damage, and control participants on stimulus-value and action-value reversal tasks. OFC damage selectively impaired learning from changing stimulus-value contingencies, while anterior cingulate damage impaired action-value learning more clearly. The result parallels the animal evidence for partly dissociable stimulus–outcome and action–outcome systems. It provides converging evidence across species, but the human lesions often involved more than one small cortical field, and patient lesions are not experimentally assigned. The study therefore supports a functional distinction without proving that every human orbital or cingulate subregion has the same role in every context.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the two task types, the lesion groups, and the direction of the principal double dissociation.
Key point:
- Human lesion evidence supports a distinction between stimulus-value and action-value learning.
- Lesion extent and patient heterogeneity constrain anatomical precision.
Source links:
- Camille, Tsuchida and Fellows (2011), Double dissociation of stimulus-value and action-value learning
28. Q: What is a reward-prediction error, and how can it contribute to object reversal learning?
A: Show answer
A reward-prediction error is the difference between the outcome that occurs and the outcome that was expected. An unexpected reward produces a positive prediction error; omission of an expected reward produces a negative one. During reversal, either event can signal that the established stimulus–outcome mapping has changed. Dopamine neurons are known to show phasic responses that depend on whether reward is better or worse than predicted, and such signals can support learning in downstream circuits. The chapter also reviews human imaging evidence for prediction-error-like activity in OFC and ventral striatum. These signals should not be conflated with the OFC's more specific representation of which stimulus was chosen and which outcome followed it.
Study instruction:
- For a rewarded and an unrewarded reversal trial, state the expected outcome, actual outcome, and sign of the prediction error.
Key point:
- Prediction errors indicate that expectations need updating.
- A global teaching signal and a stimulus-specific credit representation perform different roles.
Source links:
- Schultz (1998), Predictive reward signal of dopamine neurons
- O'Doherty et al. (2003), Temporal-difference models and reward learning
29. Q: What kinds of variables are encoded by neurons in the orbital PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews neuronal activity related to reward amount, reward probability, effort cost, time cost, risk, relative preference, confidence, and the outcomes of rejected alternatives. Some cells respond to the value of a particular object or reward; others encode the probability or cost associated with obtaining it. Cells may also reflect the value of an option relative to the other options currently available. These findings suggest that OFC activity is heterogeneous and depends on task structure, the timing of measurement, and the variable being analysed. It is therefore misleading to describe every OFC neuron as a generic reward cell or to assume that one scalar value explains all observed firing patterns.
Study instruction:
- Make a list of value-related variables in a decision task and identify how each could be manipulated independently.
Key point:
- OFC neurons represent multiple dimensions relevant to reward-guided choice.
- Neural coding is heterogeneous and context dependent.
Source links:
- Kennerley et al. (2009), Multiple decision variables in frontal neurons
- Roesch and Olson (2005), OFC activity and the value of time
- O'Neill and Schultz (2010), OFC coding of reward risk
- Abe and Lee (2011), Actual and hypothetical outcomes in prefrontal cortex
- Kepecs et al. (2008), Neural correlates of decision confidence
- Morrison and Salzman (2009), Rewarding and aversive information in OFC neurons
30. Q: What is the difference between absolute and relative value coding in the OFC?
A: Show answer
Absolute or menu-independent value coding represents the value assigned to an option without requiring that it be defined solely by the other options currently available. Relative value coding reflects an option's desirability in comparison with alternatives. Tremblay and Schultz found OFC neurons whose responses reflected monkeys' relative preferences among available foods. Padoa-Schioppa and colleagues later showed that many value-related responses were invariant to changes in the menu of alternatives, while subsequent work demonstrated adaptation to the overall range of values across behavioural conditions. These coding schemes describe different aspects of a heterogeneous population and should not be treated as mutually exclusive across the whole region. Analyses must specify whether the measured neural variable predicts choice, subjective value, or comparison among simultaneously available options.
Study instruction:
- Keep one food option fixed while varying its competing alternative, then separately vary the overall value range to distinguish menu effects from range adaptation.
Key point:
- Relative value depends on the comparison set; menu-invariant value does not.
- OFC value coding can adapt to broad value ranges without depending on each immediate alternative.
Source links:
- Tremblay and Schultz (1999), Relative reward preference in primate OFC
- Padoa-Schioppa and Assad (2008), Menu-invariant economic value
- Padoa-Schioppa (2009), Range-adapting representation of economic value
31. Q: What is a common-currency representation of value?
A: Show answer
A common currency is an abstract, usually one-dimensional representation that allows unlike outcomes to be compared on a shared scale. An animal that is both hungry and thirsty may need to compare the value of food with the value of fluid. A common scale makes it possible to select between outcomes that differ in sensory identity. The chapter associates this form of comparison particularly with medial portions of granular OFC, including area 14. Human imaging studies have reported ventromedial OFC responses to different reward types such as money and juice, while monkey studies have identified neurons that track economic value. These findings support common-currency coding but do not imply that every OFC neuron or every decision uses only one dimension.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a thirsty and hungry animal could compare a food reward with a fluid reward without treating them as sensory-identical.
Key point:
- Common currency enables comparison across different kinds of outcomes.
- Abstract value complements, rather than replaces, specific outcome representations.
Source links:
- Padoa-Schioppa and Assad (2006), OFC neurons encode economic value
- O'Doherty et al. (2001), Abstract reward and punishment representations
- Kim, Shimojo and O'Doherty (2011), Overlapping juice and money value responses
32. Q: What evidence supports the existence of common-currency coding in human OFC?
A: Show answer
O'Doherty and colleagues used a monetary reward and punishment task and reported reward-related and punishment-related activations in human OFC, including ventromedial regions. Kim and colleagues scanned participants while visual cues predicted either monetary outcomes or juice outcomes; they found partially overlapping value-related activity in ventromedial prefrontal cortex during anticipation of both reward types. These results are consistent with an abstract value signal that can respond to different kinds of outcomes. However, neuroimaging activation is correlational and spatially coarse; it does not establish that a single neuronal population literally converts every reward into an identical currency. Strong evidence requires comparing reward types while controlling subjective preference, sensory properties, task demands, and motor responses.
Study instruction:
- Identify what must be matched across monetary and primary-reward conditions before claiming that their neural values are comparable.
Key point:
- Cross-reward activation is consistent with abstract value comparison.
- Imaging alone cannot establish the exact neuronal code or causal necessity.
Source links:
- O'Doherty et al. (2001), Abstract reward and punishment representations
- Kim, Shimojo and O'Doherty (2011), Overlapping responses for juice and money
33. Q: Does the OFC represent negative outcomes as well as rewards?
A: Show answer
Yes. The chapter describes OFC contributions to negative valuation, not only positive reward. In a monkey study involving a rubber snake, control animals hesitated or sometimes refused to reach over the snake to obtain food, whereas monkeys with granular OFC lesions reached more quickly and treated the snake more like a neutral object. Electrophysiological work also shows that information about rewarding and aversive conditioned stimuli can converge in individual OFC neurons. These results are consistent with OFC participation in evaluating appetitive and aversive significance. They do not prove that OFC alone creates fear or that all negative emotions are encoded in the same way; the region contributes to evaluating stimuli and outcomes across different motivational contexts.
Study instruction:
- Contrast a positive food-predictive object with an aversive or threatening object and identify what value information each choice requires.
Key point:
- OFC valuation includes aversive as well as appetitive significance.
- A behavioural effect should not be equated with a complete theory of emotion.
Source links:
- Izquierdo et al. (2004), Orbital PF lesions and reward-guided choice
- Morrison and Salzman (2009), Convergence of rewarding and aversive information
34. Q: How can OFC support choices among abstract visual rules rather than concrete objects?
A: Show answer
A rule can function as a stimulus-like option when its use predicts a particular outcome. In the matching-to-sample experiment reviewed in the chapter, monkeys had to choose whether to match a sample by colour or by shape. After one rule was established, the correct rule changed and could be inferred only from reward or nonreward feedback. Monkeys with OFC lesions used positive feedback less effectively to maintain the newly correct rule. This suggests that OFC-related outcome learning can apply to abstract visual rules as well as tangible objects. The result does not mean that OFC stores every rule representation; other prefrontal regions contribute to maintaining rules in working memory and implementing them.
Study instruction:
- Describe how the same sample can support two different matching rules and identify the feedback needed to learn which rule is currently rewarded.
Key point:
- OFC can link outcomes to stimulus-based rules, not only physical objects.
- Rule maintenance and reward-based updating are separable functions.
Source links:
- Buckley et al. (2009), Dissociable components of rule-guided behaviour
35. Q: How does selective satiation update the motivational value of an expected outcome?
A: Show answer
Selective satiation occurs when an animal consumes one particular food until its incentive value declines. If an object has been associated with that food, a normal animal can later avoid the object even when the food is not delivered during the choice test. This demonstrates that the animal retrieves the expected food identity and combines it with its current motivational state. The chapter reports that monkeys with granular OFC lesions often continue choosing objects associated with the food they previously preferred, despite having just consumed it to satiety. The deficit is not necessarily a loss of hunger or taste discrimination; it is a failure to make object choice reflect the updated value of the predicted outcome.
Study instruction:
- Explain why the object test should occur without delivering the associated food if the aim is to measure use of an updated prediction.
Key point:
- Selective satiation changes outcome value without changing the learned object–food mapping.
- OFC helps use that updated value when selecting an object.
Source links:
- Izquierdo et al. (2004), Reward value and contingency after orbital lesions
- Gottfried, O'Doherty and Dolan (2003), Predictive reward value in human amygdala and OFC
36. Q: What does the timing of amygdala inactivation tell us about motivational value updating?
A: Show answer
Wellman and colleagues temporarily inactivated the basolateral amygdala either during selective satiation or only after satiation, during the later choice test. Inactivation during satiation blocked the normal devaluation effect, whereas inactivation after satiation did not. This timing indicates that basolateral amygdala activity is required while the animal registers the change in the food's value, but is not required for expressing the already updated preference at test. The chapter uses this finding, together with evidence for OFC involvement in object choice, to support a division of labour within an interacting circuit: the amygdala helps register the changed motivational state, while OFC uses outcome representations to guide later object selection. The result identifies a critical temporal contribution in this procedure rather than proving that all value updating occurs only during feeding.
Study instruction:
- Draw a timeline for baseline learning, selective satiation, neural inactivation, and later choice; mark the phase in which basolateral amygdala activity was necessary.
Key point:
- The timing of neural manipulation can reveal when a circuit contributes to updating.
- Basolateral amygdala activity is critical during satiation in the studied devaluation procedure.
Source links:
- Wellman, Gale and Malkova (2005), Amygdala inactivation and reward devaluation
- Izquierdo et al. (2004), Orbital PF lesions and reward-guided choice
37. Q: What does credit assignment mean in the context of orbital PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Credit assignment is the process of attributing an observed outcome to the choice or event that caused it. In a sequence of foraging choices, a reward may follow one particular object selection even though many other objects were chosen recently. Effective learning requires linking the reward to the relevant choice rather than spreading credit indiscriminately across the entire recent history. The chapter argues that granular OFC improves this causal attribution in primates. It can represent the chosen object near the time feedback arrives, allowing the outcome to be linked to the correct event. This is more specific than simply detecting reward or computing whether the latest outcome was better than expected.
Study instruction:
- Given a sequence of object choices and outcomes, identify which choice should receive credit for each outcome and explain why temporal order matters.
Key point:
- Credit assignment links an outcome to the choice that produced it.
- Granular OFC supports more precise, event-specific choice–outcome learning.
Source links:
- Walton et al. (2010), OFC and contingent learning
- Noonan et al. (2010), Value comparison and learning mechanisms in OFC
38. Q: How did the three-arm bandit experiment demonstrate single-event credit assignment?
A: Show answer
In the experiment by Walton and colleagues, a stimulus that had previously been a poor option became the highest-value option when its reward probability increased. When normal monkeys selected that newly best stimulus and received a reward, they became more likely to choose it again. Monkeys with granular OFC lesions did not use that single rewarded choice as effectively; their subsequent choices were more influenced by a longer, recency-weighted history of previously selected stimuli. The critical analysis examined what happened after a specific choice–reward event, rather than relying only on average performance over blocks. This allowed the researchers to test whether one outcome was attributed to the choice that immediately preceded it.
Study instruction:
- Recreate the newly-best versus previously-best comparison and predict how a single rewarded choice should affect the next trial.
Key point:
- Normal monkeys used a single successful event to update choice preference.
- Granular OFC lesions weakened this precise event-to-outcome attribution.
Source links:
- Walton et al. (2010), Separable learning systems in macaques
39. Q: What learning strategy may replace precise credit assignment after granular OFC damage?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a recency-weighted, cumulative strategy in which choices are guided by a broader history of which options have recently produced reward. Rather than attributing a particular outcome to the single choice that preceded it, the animal behaves as if reward information is averaged across recent choice history. This can produce apparently reasonable behaviour when contingencies are stable, but it is slow and can be misleading when the best option changes abruptly. The chapter relates this pattern to melioration: increasing responses that have recently been effective without representing each individual choice–outcome event accurately. It is an approximation to causal learning, not evidence that the animal has stopped learning altogether.
Study instruction:
- Compare an event-specific update with a moving average of recent rewards and identify which adapts faster after a sudden contingency change.
Key point:
- OFC damage can shift learning toward slower, recency-weighted averaging.
- Cumulative reward history is not equivalent to precise causal credit assignment.
Source links:
- Walton et al. (2010), OFC in contingent learning
40. Q: What is melioration, and why is it relevant to the chapter's evolutionary argument?
A: Show answer
Melioration is a process in which behaviour increasingly favours options that have been more effective recently, based on an accumulated history rather than explicit attribution of each outcome to a particular event. The chapter uses this idea to characterize the kind of learning that may remain after granular OFC lesions.
