Monkeys can still improve their choices over many trials, but they lose some of the advantage of linking a single outcome to the specific choice that caused it. The authors propose that granular OFC gave primates a more efficient form of causal learning, especially useful when many objects compete in a changing foraging environment. This evolutionary interpretation is a hypothesis built from comparative anatomy and behaviour, not a direct observation of ancestral brains.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a recency-weighted strategy could succeed in a stable environment yet fail after a sudden change in reward contingencies.
Key point:
- Melioration uses recent aggregate success rather than precise event-level causation.
- The proposed primate advantage is faster, more selective learning from individual outcomes.
Source links:
- Walton et al. (2010), Contingent learning in macaques
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Orbital prefrontal connectivity
41. Q: What evidence indicates that OFC neurons represent a choice near the time feedback arrives?
A: Show answer
Tsujimoto and colleagues recorded neuronal activity while monkeys followed visual cues instructing them to stay with or shift from a previous spatial response. OFC neurons encoded the chosen response mainly near feedback time, after the movement had been completed. Unlike some dorsolateral prefrontal neurons, OFC neurons represented the chosen response whether it was rewarded or not. The chapter interprets this timing as potentially useful for linking the choice to the outcome that follows. It does not show that every OFC neuron performs credit assignment or that the task proves a single-cell causal mechanism; it provides a plausible neural representation that could support the required association.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a representation of the just-completed choice at feedback time could help associate that choice with the outcome.
Key point:
- OFC can represent the selected response when feedback is delivered.
- Feedback-period coding is a plausible component of credit assignment.
Source links:
- Tsujimoto, Genovesio and Wise (2009), Monkey OFC encodes response choices near feedback
42. Q: Why can OFC choice signals appear on both correct and error trials?
A: Show answer
In the cued-strategy task discussed in the chapter, monkeys sometimes made errors even though the rule was clear. Tsujimoto and colleagues found that granular OFC neurons encoded the selected choice around feedback on both correct and incorrect trials. This differs from regions in which choice-related activity may depend on whether the subject used the intended strategy successfully. The chapter suggests that OFC may encode which choice was made regardless of the cognitive process that generated it, a property that could be useful when assigning the subsequent outcome to that choice. The conclusion is bounded by the task: reward probabilities were fixed and the experiment did not require the same kind of learning as the bandit task.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish neural coding of the action actually selected from neural coding of whether the subject followed the instructed strategy correctly.
Key point:
- OFC choice coding can be outcome-period and strategy independent in the studied task.
- Generalization to tasks requiring learning must be tested separately.
Source links:
- Tsujimoto, Genovesio and Wise (2011), Strategy signals in dorsolateral and orbital PFC
- Tsujimoto, Genovesio and Wise (2009), OFC response coding near feedback
43. Q: How do lateral and medial subdivisions of granular OFC differ in the chapter's account?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that lateral granular OFC, including areas 11 and parts of 13, is especially important for representing specific outcomes, learning choice–outcome mappings, and updating the value of particular objects in light of current needs. Medial granular OFC, centred on area 14, is more involved in comparing the values of different outcomes using a common currency. The distinction is supported by lesion, neurophysiological, and imaging findings, but it is a functional specialization rather than a claim that the regions never share information. Their activity and connectivity are embedded in interacting networks, and the exact boundary between areas can vary with the anatomical definition used in a study.
Study instruction:
- Make a diagram linking lateral OFC to specific outcome identity and medial OFC to value comparison, then add the amygdala and sensory association inputs.
Key point:
- Lateral OFC emphasizes learning and representing specific outcomes.
- Medial OFC emphasizes comparing outcome values in a common currency.
Source links:
- Noonan et al. (2010), Separate value comparison and learning mechanisms
- Rudebeck and Murray (2011), Distinct OFC subregions
44. Q: What do selective lesion studies reveal about areas 11/13 and area 14?
A: Show answer
Rudebeck and Murray compared monkeys with restricted excitotoxic lesions targeting areas 11/13 or area 14. Damage to areas 11/13 disrupted rapid updating of object value after selective satiation, whereas area 14 lesions did not produce the same devaluation impairment. Conversely, area 14 lesions mildly slowed extinction of responding to a previously rewarded object more than areas 11/13 lesions did. Neither restricted lesion disrupted serial object reversal learning, even though aspiration lesions encompassing the whole OFC produce severe deficits on that task. These results support a division between specific object-value updating and other forms of reward-guided learning, while also showing that large lesions and damage to fibres of passage can produce a different behavioural profile from restricted excitotoxic lesions.
Study instruction:
- Build a lesion-by-task matrix for areas 11/13 and area 14, marking which functions were impaired and which were spared.
Key point:
- OFC subdivisions make dissociable contributions to reward-guided behaviour.
- Lesion size and damage to fibres of passage can change the observed deficit.
Source links:
- Rudebeck and Murray (2011), Subtotal lesions within macaque orbital PFC
- Noonan et al. (2010), Value comparison and learning mechanisms
45. Q: How should the chapter's evidence be integrated into a cautious model of orbital PF cortex function?
A: Show answer
The most defensible synthesis is that OFC participates in choosing among sensory stimuli by combining learned stimulus–outcome relationships with current motivational value. Agranular circuitry contributes conserved sensory and interoceptive associations; granular primate OFC adds richer visual-object conjunctions and more accurate credit assignment. Within granular OFC, lateral regions are more strongly associated with specific outcome learning and updating, while medial regions contribute to comparing outcomes in a common currency. OFC interacts with the amygdala, insula, temporal cortex, and medial PF systems rather than operating alone. The evidence comes from anatomy, lesions, single-cell recordings, and human imaging, each with different inferential strengths. The chapter's evolutionary and computational proposals are explanatory models that should be tested against new tasks and more selective interventions.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the model in a flow diagram from sensory input, through outcome prediction and value updating, to stimulus selection and feedback-based learning.
Key point:
- OFC integrates what an object predicts with how valuable that outcome is now.
- Subregional, cross-species, and methodological qualifications are essential to interpretation.
Source links:
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Complementary prefrontal networks
- Walton et al. (2010), OFC and contingent learning
- Rudebeck and Murray (2011), Distinct subregions for value comparison
46. Q: What are affordances, and why are they important for understanding prefrontal representations of objects?
A: Show answer
An affordance is an action associated with an object or class of objects. A cup affords grasping and drinking; a tool affords a particular manipulation; a food item may afford picking up, carrying, biting, or peeling. The chapter proposes that medial PF cortex can combine object–outcome associations with action–outcome associations to represent the outcome expected from acting on an object in a particular way. Such a representation contains information about the object's features and the actions those features make possible. It may also represent more abstract classes of action, rather than one fixed movement. Affordances therefore provide a bridge between identifying an object and selecting a useful action toward it.
Study instruction:
- Choose three familiar objects and list their perceptual features, possible actions, and outcomes that could follow from each action.
Key point:
- Affordances link object identity to possible actions.
- Combining object and action information supports flexible, context-sensitive behaviour.
Source links:
- Camille, Tsuchida and Fellows (2011), Stimulus-value and action-value learning
- Saleem, Kondo and Price (2008), Orbital and medial prefrontal networks
47. Q: Why do recordings of stimulus–outcome activity in medial PF cortex not invalidate the lesion-based distinction between medial and orbital PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Single-cell recordings show what information a neuron carries under a particular task, whereas a lesion tests whether a region is necessary for successful performance. The chapter notes that medial PF neurons can reflect stimulus–outcome conjunctions and that medial PF activity can occur during object choices. This does not mean that medial PF cortex has the same primary function as OFC. Dense interconnections allow the two networks to combine stimulus–outcome and action–outcome information, and task demands may recruit both systems even when only one is essential for a specific association. A region can therefore encode information supplied by another network without being the critical site for learning or using that information in every task.
Study instruction:
- For one neural-recording result and one lesion result, state separately what each method establishes and what it cannot establish.
Key point:
- Neural representation is not identical to causal necessity.
- Interconnected networks can share information while retaining specialized contributions.
Source links:
- Rudebeck et al. (2008), Frontal subregions in action and stimulus choice
- Camille, Tsuchida and Fellows (2011), Stimulus-value and action-value learning
48. Q: How can OFC neurons encode action choices without making OFC the principal action-selection system?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that granular OFC neurons can encode a monkey's chosen action around the time feedback arrives, even though lesion evidence identifies OFC most strongly with stimulus-based choice and medial PF regions with action-based choice. The timing and context are important: a transient representation of the action that was just performed can help associate the subsequent reward or nonreward with that event. It need not mean that OFC generated the action, maintained the action rule, or was the only region representing it. This is a good example of how a region can carry information needed for learning about another system's choices while remaining specialized for a different causal contribution to behaviour.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish action generation, action representation at feedback, and assignment of the outcome to the action in a three-stage diagram.
Key point:
- OFC action signals may support feedback-based credit assignment.
- Encoding a choice does not prove that a region is the primary action-selection mechanism.
Source links:
- Tsujimoto, Genovesio and Wise (2009), OFC response coding near feedback
- Camille, Tsuchida and Fellows (2011), Stimulus-value and action-value learning
Rows: 48
Table 5
Caudal Prefrontal Cortex: Goal Search and Visual Attention
1. Q: What is the principal function of the caudal prefrontal cortex proposed in this chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that the caudal prefrontal (PF) cortex searches for objects and locations that have acquired value through learning, then directs overt or covert attention toward them. It is not defined simply as an eye-movement generator or a store for spatial memories. Its distinctive contribution is to use information about a goal's learned value, in the context of current biological needs, to bias visual search and selection. Eye movements can bring the fovea to a selected target, while covert attention enhances processing without a gaze shift. The selected object or place is a goal for a later action; attending to it does not itself obtain the reward or outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter's proposal in one sentence, then distinguish finding and attending to a goal from performing the action that obtains its outcome."
Key point:
- Caudal PF cortex selects and attends to learned, valuable visual goals.
- Attention identifies or prioritizes a goal; motor systems achieve it.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
2. Q: What does the chapter mean by a goal?
A: Show answer
A goal is an object or location that can serve as the target of an action. It is not the reward, outcome, or biological benefit produced by the action. A food item, a place where food is usually found, or a laboratory target can be a goal; eating the food or obtaining the resource is the action's outcome. This distinction matters because the caudal PF cortex is proposed to search for and orient attention toward goals, whereas premotor and motor systems help transform a selected target into movements that achieve it. A stimulus can therefore be behaviorally important as a goal even before the animal acts on it.
Study instruction:
- "Classify examples into goal, action, and outcome, and explain why looking at a food item is not equivalent to consuming it."
Key point:
- A goal is an action target, not the reward or result of the action.
- Goal selection and goal achievement are separable functions.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Milner and Goodale: Two visual systems re-viewed
3. Q: Which cortical territories are included in the caudal PF cortex in the chapter's working definition?
A: Show answer
In macaque monkeys, the chapter defines the caudal PF cortex as including area 8 and, for the purposes of its argument, the caudal portion of the principal sulcus. The latter territory is associated with area 9/46 in the scheme of Petrides and Pandya and is called the postero-lateral PF cortex by the authors. The grouping is functional and connectional rather than a claim that all included tissue is cytoarchitectonically identical. The chapter notes that the postero-lateral region can reasonably be classified with the dorsal PF cortex instead; its placement is therefore an explicit organizational choice, not an uncontested anatomical fact.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch the arcuate and principal sulci on a macaque frontal-lobe diagram and label area 8 and the postero-lateral area 9/46 region."
Key point:
- The chapter's caudal grouping includes area 8 and postero-lateral PF cortex.
- The classification of area 9/46 is acknowledged to be debatable.
Source links:
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Amiez and Petrides: Anatomical organization of primate eye fields
4. Q: Why does the chapter include the postero-lateral PF cortex with the caudal PF areas?
A: Show answer
The authors group the postero-lateral PF cortex with caudal area 8 because the regions are densely interconnected and many neurons in the caudal principal-sulcus territory show activity related to eye movements. This arrangement also fits the chapter's evolutionary and functional account of a caudal network involved in selecting and orienting toward goals. The authors do not present the grouping as anatomically mandatory: area 9/46 has granular features and could be discussed with the dorsal PF cortex. Conclusions about this territory should therefore be read with the chapter's stated boundary qualification in mind.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the connectional and physiological reasons for grouping area 9/46 with area 8, then state the anatomical qualification."
Key point:
- Dense interconnections and eye-movement-related activity motivate the grouping.
- Area 9/46 can also be classified with dorsal PF cortex.
Source links:
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Chafee and Goldman-Rakic: Area 8a and parietal activity
5. Q: How is the frontal eye field located and defined in macaque monkeys and humans?
A: Show answer
In macaques, the frontal eye field (FEF) lies around the rostral bank of the arcuate sulcus near the caudal end of the principal sulcus. Low-current intracortical microstimulation in this region evokes saccadic eye movements; the low-threshold zone is the operational basis for identifying the FEF. In humans, electrical stimulation and individual-subject imaging place the FEF near the superior precentral sulcus, especially its ventral branch. The exact anatomical correspondence is not a simple one-to-one mapping, so human localization should combine functional evidence with each person's cortical anatomy rather than assume that a single surface landmark is universally precise.
Study instruction:
- "Review how microstimulation and imaging define the FEF, and distinguish a functional localization from a cytoarchitectonic boundary."
Key point:
- The FEF is functionally identified by low-threshold saccade-evoking stimulation.
- Human and macaque locations correspond broadly, not perfectly.
Source links:
- Bruce and Goldberg: Primate frontal eye fields
- Amiez and Petrides: Anatomical organization of primate eye fields
6. Q: How does the frontal eye field differ from the supplementary eye field?
A: Show answer
Both the FEF and supplementary eye field (SEF) are frontal regions associated with eye movements, and neurons in both can become active before saccades. The chapter places the macaque FEF in area 8 near the arcuate sulcus, whereas the SEF lies more dorsomedially in medial frontal cortex within area 6. Their existence illustrates that saccade-related functions are distributed across a network rather than confined to one cortical patch. The chapter's central argument is not that the FEF lacks oculomotor involvement, but that its contribution should be interpreted in a broader framework of goal search and attention.
Study instruction:
- "Mark the approximate FEF and SEF locations on a frontal cortical map and list one functional reason not to treat them as a single field."
Key point:
- FEF and SEF are distinct frontal eye-related territories.
- Saccade control is distributed across a network.
Source links:
- Amiez and Petrides: Anatomical organization of primate eye fields
- Bruce and Goldberg: Primate frontal eye fields
7. Q: How do the dorsal and ventral visual streams differ, and why does the distinction matter for caudal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The dorsal visual stream, extending toward posterior parietal cortex, is strongly associated with spatial information used to guide actions. The ventral stream, extending toward inferior temporal cortex, is strongly associated with object properties such as colour, shape, and texture. These are broad functional emphases, not absolute divisions in which one stream processes only space and the other only objects. Caudal PF subdivisions connect with parts of both streams, allowing search and selection to draw on spatial and object-related information. Area 8Av is especially notable in the chapter because it has ventral-stream as well as dorsal-stream connections.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the two pathways from early visual cortex and identify which information types each contributes to goal search."
Key point:
- Dorsal and ventral streams provide complementary spatial and object information.
- Caudal PF cortex can integrate information from both pathways.
Source links:
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Milner and Goodale: Two visual systems re-viewed
8. Q: What is distinctive about the visual inputs to the FEF compared with other caudal PF subdivisions?
A: Show answer
The FEF receives direct inputs from relatively early visual areas, including V2 and V3, whereas area 8Ad and the postero-lateral PF cortex do not show the same direct low-order visual input pattern in the chapter's account. This gives the FEF access to visual signals at an earlier stage of cortical processing. The authors use this anatomy to help explain why some FEF neurons respond to visual stimuli even when no saccade is made. It is consistent with a role in detecting, selecting, and orienting toward visual goals, although anatomy alone cannot prove the precise computation performed by those neurons.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the input patterns of FEF, 8Ad, 8Av, and postero-lateral PF cortex using the chapter's connection summary."
Key point:
- FEF has direct input from early visual areas V2 and V3.
- Connectivity constrains possible functions but does not prove them alone.
Source links:
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Bruce and Goldberg: Primate frontal eye fields
9. Q: What are the principal corticocortical connections of the caudal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes connections with posterior parietal regions involved in visuospatial and oculomotor processing, including the lateral intraparietal area and area PG, as well as connections with temporal visual areas such as MST and, for area 8Av, TEO and other inferior temporal territories. The subdivisions of the caudal PF cortex are also densely interconnected with one another. These links provide routes for combining spatial target information, visual motion, and object features. The pattern supports a network account: no single input or output explains the function; the contribution emerges from interactions among frontal and posterior cortical regions.
Study instruction:
- "Use an anatomical diagram to trace the links from area 8 subdivisions to LIP, PG, MST, TEO, and neighbouring PF subdivisions."
Key point:
- Caudal PF cortex is embedded in a distributed visual-attentional network.
- Its subdivisions have different but overlapping connection patterns.
Source links:
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Amiez and Petrides: Anatomical organization of primate eye fields
10. Q: How can the FEF influence eye movements through the superior colliculus and basal ganglia?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes three routes. The FEF projects directly to the superior colliculus (SC), which participates in orienting the eyes and other sensory receptors. It also projects to the caudate nucleus, which influences the substantia nigra pars reticulata; the nigral output normally inhibits the SC, so basal-ganglia pathways can regulate when collicular orienting commands are released. In addition, the FEF projects directly to brainstem oculomotor nuclei. These parallel pathways provide multiple ways for frontal signals to influence saccades. They do not imply that the FEF alone is sufficient for every eye movement.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the direct FEF-to-SC route and the indirect FEF-caudate-SNr-SC route, noting where inhibitory influence is exerted."
Key point:
- FEF influences the SC directly and through basal-ganglia circuitry.
- Nigral output provides an inhibitory gate on SC activity.
Source links:
- Hikosaka and Wurtz: Substantia nigra and superior colliculus
- Dias and Segraves: FEF inactivation and saccades
- Amiez and Petrides: Anatomical organization of primate eye fields
11. Q: Why does the chapter classify the FEF as prefrontal rather than simply premotor cortex?
A: Show answer
The authors distinguish mechanisms that generate and select goals from mechanisms that convert a selected goal into the movements that achieve it. The FEF receives visual and task-relevance information, helps identify a valuable target, and directs overt or covert attention toward it. Premotor and primary motor systems contribute more directly to movement implementation, including the transformations needed to move the hand or other effectors to a target. The distinction is functional rather than a denial that FEF activity can precede or influence saccades. A cortical region can participate in orienting attention through eye movements while still serving a broader goal-selection role.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between selecting a target and computing the movements needed to reach or manipulate it."
Key point:
- The FEF is framed as a goal-search and attention system.
- Motor implementation is distinguished from selecting what to act toward.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Astafiev et al.: Attending, looking, and pointing
- Milner and Goodale: Two visual systems re-viewed
12. Q: What is the difference between overt and covert attention?
A: Show answer
Overt attention involves orienting the sensory receptors, especially the fovea, toward a selected location, commonly through a saccadic eye movement. Covert attention involves prioritizing information at a location while gaze remains fixed elsewhere. Both can enhance processing of relevant stimuli, and both can help identify objects or places that may become goals for action. The chapter treats foveation as one form of overt attention, not as the entire function of the FEF. Covert attention is particularly important to the evolutionary argument because early primates are proposed to have searched visually before the fovea evolved.
Study instruction:
- "Give one example of covert attention and one of overt attention, then explain what changes in gaze and what changes in sensory priority."
Key point:
- Overt attention shifts gaze; covert attention shifts priority without a gaze shift.
- Both can support selection of action goals.
Source links:
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Astafiev et al.: Attending, looking, and pointing
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
13. Q: How does the FEF contribute to visual search rather than merely executing a saccade?
A: Show answer
FEF neurons can respond to visual stimuli and distinguish behaviorally relevant targets from distractors before an eye movement is made. Some cells encode the location of a stimulus even when the required saccade is directed elsewhere, indicating that their activity is not reducible to the final motor vector. Other cells combine visual and movement-related signals. The chapter interprets this mixture as part of a process that identifies a candidate goal, biases attention toward it, and then supports overt orienting when appropriate. The FEF therefore participates in the selection process that precedes or accompanies a saccade, not only in its execution.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a task in which a visual target is present but no saccade is made with a task requiring a saccade to it."
Key point:
- FEF activity can represent visual target information independently of the executed saccade.
- Search and movement signals coexist within the FEF.
Source links:
- Bruce and Goldberg: Primate frontal eye fields
- Hasegawa et al.: Search target selection in prefrontal cortex
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
14. Q: What do visual, movement, and visuomovement neurons in the FEF represent?
A: Show answer
Visual neurons respond to stimuli in their receptive fields even when the animal does not make a saccade to them. Movement neurons show activity associated with purposive saccades, including movements without a visual target. Visuomovement neurons combine visual and movement-related properties. These categories are useful descriptions of response patterns, but the chapter notes that they may form a continuum rather than three perfectly separate cell classes. Their coexistence helps explain why FEF recordings can reflect sensory selection, preparation, and movement-related processing at different times within the same task.
Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison table of visual, movement, and visuomovement response properties, including whether activity requires a visible target or a saccade."
Key point:
- FEF contains sensory, motor, and mixed response patterns.
- Cell categories describe response tendencies, not necessarily rigid modules.
Source links:
- Bruce and Goldberg: Primate frontal eye fields
- Dias and Segraves: FEF inactivation and saccades
15. Q: What evidence shows that FEF activity can encode a stimulus location independently of the saccade goal?
A: Show answer
In the antisaccade logic discussed in the chapter, a salient stimulus appears in one location but the required eye movement is directed to the opposite location. If neural activity represented only the saccade vector, it should immediately reflect the opposite goal. Instead, many task-related FEF cells initially reflect the location of the stimulus, with a later shift in some cells toward the movement goal. This temporal pattern supports the interpretation that FEF activity can first represent a selected visual event and later contribute to response preparation. It does not mean every FEF neuron has the same coding sequence.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the cue and antisaccade target on opposite sides of fixation and predict how purely visual, purely motor, and mixed cells would respond."
Key point:
- Stimulus coding can precede and differ from saccade-goal coding.
- The FEF contains evolving sensory-to-action representations.
Source links:
- Bruce and Goldberg: Primate frontal eye fields
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
16. Q: How does FEF inactivation test whether the region contributes to covert attention?
A: Show answer
Inactivation temporarily reduces activity in a targeted cortical region, allowing investigators to test whether normal performance depends on it. Wardak and colleagues inactivated the FEF while monkeys performed a visual-search task that required target detection without eye movements. Detection became slower for targets in the affected visual field, showing that the FEF contributes to covert search even when a saccade is not the required response. Because the manipulation also affects oculomotor functions, the no-eye-movement condition is crucial: it helps separate an attentional contribution from a deficit caused solely by an inability to execute the eye movement.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why fixation must be monitored and eye movements prevented when using a covert-search task to test attentional function."
Key point:
- FEF inactivation affects covert target detection as well as saccades.
- A no-eye-movement condition helps separate attention from motor execution.
Source links:
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Dias and Segraves: FEF inactivation and saccades
17. Q: What does the evolutionary argument about the fovea imply for the original function of caudal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that caudal PF cortex appeared early in primate evolution, before the fovea and full trichromatic vision. If this chronology is correct, its original visual function could not have depended on foveating a target in the modern human manner. The authors therefore propose that early caudal PF cortex supported covert search and attention across the visual field. Later, when a fovea evolved, the system could also direct overt attention through eye movements. This is an evolutionary hypothesis inferred from comparative anatomy and visual ecology, not a direct recording from extinct primates.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the evolutionary premises from the functional inference, and identify which parts are direct evidence versus historical reconstruction."
Key point:
- The proposed early function is covert visual search before foveation.
- The evolutionary account is a hypothesis, not direct observation of extinct primates.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Amiez and Petrides: Anatomical organization of primate eye fields
18. Q: Why does covert attention remain important in primates that have a fovea?
A: Show answer
A fovea provides high-resolution processing for the point of gaze, but it cannot deliver equally detailed processing of every location in a cluttered scene at once. Covert attention allows the brain to prioritize selected extrafoveal objects while the eyes remain directed elsewhere. Those objects may be future action goals, even when they are not currently foveated. The chapter therefore treats the evolution of foveal vision as an addition to, rather than a replacement for, covert attention. This distinction is important in natural settings where several potentially valuable objects compete for selection.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a situation in which an object outside the current point of gaze must be monitored because it may become the next action goal."
Key point:
- Foveation does not eliminate the need to prioritize peripheral information.
- Covert attention can prepare selection before gaze moves.
Source links:
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Astafiev et al.: Attending, looking, and pointing
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
19. Q: What are the phases of the oculomotor delayed-response task?
A: Show answer
A typical oculomotor delayed-response (ODR) trial has a cue phase, a delay phase, and a choice phase. A brief peripheral cue identifies a location. The monkey then maintains fixation on a central point while the cue is absent for a delay that can last hundreds of milliseconds to several seconds. After a go signal, the monkey makes a saccade toward the previously cued location. Reward is delivered for an accurate response. Because the target is no longer visible at the choice phase, performance requires maintaining task-relevant information, but the delay also allows covert attention, response selection, and preparation to influence neural activity.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a trial timeline and label cue onset and offset, fixation, delay, go signal, saccade, and reward."
Key point:
- ODR separates cue presentation, a blank delay, and a memory-guided saccade.
- Delay activity may support more than one cognitive operation.
Source links:
- Funahashi et al.: Mnemonic coding of visual space
- Chafee and Goldman-Rakic: Area 8a and parietal activity
20. Q: Why is the ODR task not a pure measure of retrospective spatial working memory?
A: Show answer
The task is often called a spatial-memory task because the cue disappears before the response. However, the delay interval can also contain covert attention to the cued location, selection of a future saccade goal, preparation of the response, and maintenance of task rules. A sustained neural signal during the delay therefore cannot be assigned to retrospective sensory memory merely because it occurs while the cue is absent. The chapter's central methodological warning is to identify the processes required by the task rather than infer them from the task's name. Different experimental manipulations are needed to separate these competing interpretations.
Study instruction:
- "List at least four processes that may occur during the ODR delay and identify one experimental manipulation that could distinguish each."
Key point:
- Delay-period activity is not synonymous with memory maintenance.
- Task labels do not establish the cognitive process being measured.
Source links:
- Lebedev et al.: Attended versus remembered locations
- Curtis and D'Esposito: Selection and maintenance of saccade goals
- Funahashi et al.: Mnemonic coding of visual space
21. Q: What is the difference between retrospective and prospective coding in a delayed-response task?
A: Show answer
Retrospective coding represents information about the earlier sensory event, such as the location where a cue appeared. Prospective coding represents the selected future goal or intended response, such as the location toward which the animal will later make a saccade. Both can persist during a delay, and a task may permit a transition from a sensory representation to a selected action goal. Consequently, evidence that a neuron is spatially tuned during a delay does not by itself establish whether it stores the past cue, the future movement goal, or both. Experimental designs must vary cue and response locations independently to distinguish these possibilities.
Study instruction:
- "Use a cue-to-saccade diagram to mark which spatial variable is retrospective and which is prospective."
Key point:
- Retrospective coding refers to the past cue; prospective coding refers to the selected future goal.
- A spatial delay signal may reflect either or both.
Source links:
- Curtis and D'Esposito: Selection and maintenance of saccade goals
- Funahashi et al.: Mnemonic coding of visual space
- Tsujimoto and Postle: Reconsidering the mnemonic scotoma
22. Q: How can prosaccade and antisaccade conditions help distinguish cue coding from goal coding?
A: Show answer
In a prosaccade condition, the animal looks toward the cue; in an antisaccade condition, it looks to the opposite location. The cue and the required saccade goal are therefore dissociated. If a neuron's delay activity represents only the remembered cue, it should maintain the cue location across both conditions. If it represents only the planned saccade, it should track the response goal. Studies reviewed in the chapter found cells with each pattern and many with mixed or changing signals. The method is informative, but it does not isolate memory from attention unless attentional demands are independently controlled.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a two-condition table showing cue location and response goal, then predict the coding expected under each hypothesis."
Key point:
- Prosaccade and antisaccade trials separate sensory cue location from movement goal.
- Mixed coding means neither simple account explains every neuron.
Source links:
- Funahashi et al.: Prefrontal lesions and oculomotor delayed response
- Funahashi et al.: Mnemonic coding of visual space
- Tsujimoto and Postle: Reconsidering the mnemonic scotoma
23. Q: What did the spatial matching-to-sample experiments contribute to the debate about delay-period activity?
A: Show answer
Matching-to-sample designs require an animal to compare a later test stimulus with a previously presented cue, while the response can be made with a fixed movement rather than a movement toward the remembered location. This reduces the confound between cue location and the direction of the reporting movement. The chapter reviews studies in which many prefrontal neurons continued to show cue-related delay activity under such conditions, a result consistent with a memory contribution. However, these experiments did not independently separate remembered location from covert attention to that location, so they did not establish that memory was the dominant source of the activity.
Study instruction:
- "Explain which motor confound is reduced by a fixed reporting movement and which attentional confound remains."
Key point:
- Fixed-response matching tasks reduce the link between cue location and movement direction.
- They do not by themselves separate attention from memory.
Source links:
- Lebedev et al.: Attended versus remembered locations
- Funahashi et al.: Mnemonic coding of visual space
24. Q: What was the key design of Lebedev and colleagues' attended-versus-remembered experiment?
A: Show answer
Lebedev and colleagues trained monkeys to maintain information about one spatial location while covertly attending to a different location at the same time. Both locations could serve as potential saccade targets, but the task instructions made one location relevant for memory and the other for attention. Recording from prefrontal neurons allowed the researchers to compare spatial tuning for these two demands within the same general task. The design directly addressed a limitation of earlier ODR experiments: memory and attention had often been confounded because the animal had to remember and attend to the same location.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the task with two distinct locations and explain how it separates memory demands from attentional demands."
Key point:
- The experiment dissociated the remembered location from the attended location.
- It directly tested two competing interpretations of prefrontal delay activity.
Source links:
- Lebedev et al.: Attended versus remembered locations
25. Q: What did Lebedev and colleagues find about attended and remembered locations?
A: Show answer
Among spatially tuned prefrontal neurons in the experiment, the largest group preferentially represented the attended location, while a smaller group selectively represented the remembered location and others showed mixed properties. The chapter reports approximately 61 percent attention-selective cells and 16 percent memory-selective cells among the relevant population. The result challenges the claim that most delay-period activity in the sampled caudal and postero-lateral PF regions is retrospective spatial memory. It does not show that memory is absent; rather, it indicates that attention accounted for more of the measured spatial coding under those task conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Read the original article's methods and figures, and state the denominator used for each reported percentage before interpreting the result."
Key point:
- Attention-selective coding predominated in the sampled population.
- A minority memory-selective population remained.
Source links:
- Lebedev et al.: Attended versus remembered locations
26. Q: How should the 61 percent attention and 16 percent memory figures be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The percentages describe the distribution of coding preferences among neurons that met the study's spatial-tuning criteria; they are not proportions of all neurons in the prefrontal cortex and should not be generalized to every task or PF subdivision. The remaining cells included mixed or intermediate patterns. The findings support the claim that attentional coding was more prevalent than selective memory coding in that experiment, but they do not prove that every delay signal is attentional. Population sampling, task design, recording location, and operational definitions of selectivity all constrain the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Write a short critique that identifies the sampled population, the classification criteria, the mixed category, and limits on generalization."
Key point:
- Percentages apply to the study's analyzed neuronal population, not the whole brain.
- Mixed cells and task design matter when interpreting the distribution.
Source links:
- Lebedev et al.: Attended versus remembered locations
- Chafee and Goldman-Rakic: Area 8a and parietal activity
27. Q: What do human imaging studies add to the interpretation of FEF delay activity?
A: Show answer
Human imaging studies show that FEF activity can occur when participants covertly attend to a peripheral location while maintaining central fixation, even when no eye movement is made during the delay. A two-delay design can further separate remembering several possible locations from selecting one location as the future saccade goal. In the study reviewed by the chapter, FEF activity persisted after a goal was selected, consistent with maintaining an attended or prospectively coded goal rather than merely retaining the original array. Imaging identifies task-related regional activity, however, and cannot by itself specify the code carried by individual neurons.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the information required in the first and second delay periods of a delayed-selection task."
Key point:
- FEF activation can accompany covert attention and selected-goal maintenance.
- Regional imaging does not identify a single neuronal code.
Source links:
- Astafiev et al.: Attending, looking, and pointing
- Curtis and D'Esposito: Selection and maintenance of saccade goals
28. Q: Why does a lesion-related accuracy error not necessarily demonstrate loss of spatial memory?
A: Show answer
An inaccurate saccade may move toward the correct location but fall short or land to one side. That pattern suggests that the animal retains at least some information about the correct goal, even though it cannot translate that information into a precise orienting response. The chapter distinguishes such accuracy errors from frank errors, in which the animal selects a different candidate location. Inactivation of FEF or adjacent caudal PF regions can impair saccade accuracy even when a visible target is present, indicating a sensorimotor or attentional contribution that is not specific to maintaining a cue across a delay.
Study instruction:
- "Classify saccade endpoints as accurate, inaccurate toward the target, or frank errors, and explain what each pattern can and cannot reveal about memory."
Key point:
- Inaccuracy toward the correct goal is not equivalent to forgetting it.
- Visible-target controls help identify basic oculomotor impairments.
Source links:
- Funahashi et al.: Prefrontal lesions and oculomotor delayed response
- Dias and Segraves: FEF inactivation and saccades
- Tsujimoto and Postle: Reconsidering the mnemonic scotoma
29. Q: What is the difference between an accuracy error and a frank error in the ODR task?
A: Show answer
An accuracy error occurs when the saccade is directed toward the correct goal but does not land precisely on it; its endpoint is still closer to the cued location than to alternative candidate locations. A frank error occurs when the animal chooses the wrong spatial goal. The distinction matters because a broad distribution of endpoints around the correct direction can reflect impaired precision, attention, or movement calibration, whereas a wrong-goal choice raises different questions about selection, interference, or memory. Neither error type should be interpreted without examining the full endpoint distribution and the animal's behavior after the response.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect example endpoint plots and identify whether errors reflect directional selection or precision of the movement."
Key point:
- Accuracy errors preserve the correct general direction; frank errors select another goal.
- Error categories help distinguish mechanisms.
Source links:
- Funahashi et al.: Prefrontal lesions and oculomotor delayed response
- Tsujimoto and Postle: Reconsidering the mnemonic scotoma
30. Q: What do corrective saccades after a frank error imply about memory?
A: Show answer
Tsujimoto and Postle reported that when monkeys made an incorrect saccade after prefrontal inactivation, the next saccade often corrected toward the appropriate goal. The chapter uses this behavior to argue that the animals had not simply lost the remembered cue location or the correct target. A corrective movement implies that information sufficient to identify the intended goal remained available after the initial error. The initial failure may instead reflect selection, response control, interference, or conversion of goal knowledge into an accurate orienting movement. The result challenges a simple retrospective-memory explanation but does not eliminate all working-memory contributions.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the sequence of the first saccade, feedback or continued viewing, and corrective saccade, then infer what information remained accessible."
Key point:
- A rapid correction is evidence that the correct goal can still be represented.
- Initial wrong choices may reflect selection or control, not simple forgetting.
Source links:
- Tsujimoto and Postle: Reconsidering the mnemonic scotoma
- Funahashi et al.: Prefrontal lesions and oculomotor delayed response
31. Q: How can interference from the previous trial explain some frank errors?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that monkeys sometimes chose the goal that had been correct and rewarded on the preceding trial. This pattern suggests difficulty distinguishing the current goal from a recently relevant one, rather than a simple inability to retain the current cue. In a sequence of similar trials, a previously successful response can compete with the newly instructed response. This is a form of proactive interference or response-selection difficulty. The interpretation highlights why trial history, cue changes, and response repetition must be controlled when using delayed-response tasks to infer memory mechanisms.
Study instruction:
- "Design a trial sequence that alternates and repeats goal locations, then predict when previous-goal interference should be greatest."
Key point:
- Some errors reflect confusion between current and previous goals.
- Trial history is a critical control in delayed-response experiments.
Source links:
- Tsujimoto and Postle: Reconsidering the mnemonic scotoma
- Funahashi et al.: Prefrontal lesions and oculomotor delayed response
32. Q: What evidence from tasks without a delay period challenges a memory-only account of caudal PF function?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews tasks in which a cue specifies the goal and the animal responds without waiting through a blank retention interval. FEF neurons still show task-related activity during immediate decisions, and temporary inactivation or lesions of caudal PF regions impair performance on conditional saccade and reaching tasks that do not require retrospective spatial retention. If the core function were only to bridge a delay by maintaining a sensory cue, such deficits would be difficult to explain. The findings instead support a role in selecting and attending to the currently relevant goal, although some no-delay tasks still require learning, rule use, or prospective preparation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a delayed-response task with an immediate conditional-choice task and identify which processes are shared and which are unique."
Key point:
- Caudal PF activity and deficits occur even without a memory delay.
- Goal selection and attention are required in both delayed and immediate tasks.
Source links:
- Hasegawa et al.: Search target selection in prefrontal cortex
- Lawler and Cowey: Prefrontal cortex and visuospatial attention
- Petrides: Conditional associative learning after periarcuate lesions
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
33. Q: How do reaching tasks support the chapter's account of caudal PF function?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites studies in which monkeys learned to reach to different locations or objects depending on a visual cue. Bilateral caudal PF lesions impaired performance even though the task did not require holding a spatial cue across a delay. Because the required response was a reach rather than a saccade, the deficit is not easily reduced to a specialized inability to generate eye movements. The authors interpret the result as difficulty selecting and attending to the visual goal that determines the action. The exact impairment can still include perceptual, associative, and response-selection components, so the task should not be treated as a pure attention assay.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a reaching response broadens the interpretation beyond eye-movement execution, while identifying other processes the task still requires."
Key point:
- Caudal PF lesions affect cue-guided goal selection for reaching as well as looking.
- The evidence supports a broader attentional account, not an isolated process.
Source links:
- Lawler and Cowey: Prefrontal cortex and visuospatial attention
- Petrides: Conditional associative learning after periarcuate lesions
- Astafiev et al.: Attending, looking, and pointing
34. Q: Why should apparent visual neglect after a caudal PF lesion be interpreted cautiously?
A: Show answer
A failure or delay in responding to a peripheral target can look like neglect, but it does not necessarily mean that the animal cannot detect the target or that an entire region of visual space is unavailable. The chapter notes evidence that FEF or related lesions can slow search and target detection while leaving performance possible. This pattern is compatible with impaired deployment of attention toward a goal rather than complete loss of visual sensation. Investigators must distinguish detection threshold, search latency, attentional orienting, fixation control, and motor execution before diagnosing the nature of the deficit.
Study instruction:
- "For a lesion study, specify separate measures of detection accuracy, reaction time, fixation stability, and saccade precision."
Key point:
- Slowed search is not the same as an absolute inability to see a location.
- Behavioral decomposition is essential before inferring neglect.
Source links:
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Lawler and Cowey: Prefrontal cortex and visuospatial attention
- Dias and Segraves: FEF inactivation and saccades
35. Q: What is goal-oriented attention in the chapter's framework?
A: Show answer
Goal-oriented attention is the learned selection of stimuli because they are relevant to an action goal and have acquired value through experience, instruction, or feedback. The target may be valuable because of its expected relation to food, fluid, safety, or another outcome. In the chapter's model, orbital PF regions contribute information about outcome value and current biological needs, while caudal PF regions use that information to search for and prioritize the corresponding objects or places. Goal-oriented attention is therefore top-down and dependent on learned relevance, rather than being driven solely by a stimulus's physical salience.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a target whose relevance is learned rather than physically conspicuous, and explain what feedback or instruction establishes its value."
Key point:
- Goal-oriented attention depends on learned relevance and current goals.
- Value information helps guide what is selected.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Desimone and Duncan: Neural mechanisms of selective visual attention
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
36. Q: How does goal-oriented attention differ from stimulus-driven attention?
A: Show answer
Stimulus-driven attention is captured by physical properties such as abrupt onset, brightness, or motion and can operate without learning which stimulus is useful for the current task. Goal-oriented attention depends on learned or instructed relevance: the animal or person selects a stimulus because it predicts an important action or outcome. The two processes can operate together. A bright stimulus may capture attention initially, but learned task information can subsequently bias selection toward a less conspicuous target. The chapter argues that caudal PF cortex is especially important for the learned, goal-oriented component.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a sudden flash with a learned food cue and identify which attentional process each example primarily illustrates."
Key point:
- Salience can capture attention without learning; goal relevance depends on learning.
- Real search can combine bottom-up and top-down influences.
Source links:
- Desimone and Duncan: Neural mechanisms of selective visual attention
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
37. Q: Why are pop-out search and conjunction search not equivalent to stimulus-driven and goal-oriented attention?
A: Show answer
Pop-out search describes a display in which a target differs conspicuously from otherwise similar distractors, whereas conjunction search requires finding a target defined by a combination of features among distractors that share some of those features. These are descriptions of display structure and search difficulty, not direct labels for the source of attentional control. In the experiments reviewed by the chapter, the target was learned through feedback or instruction in both types of search. Thus both can require goal-oriented attention, and FEF inactivation can impair performance in both. A visually salient pop-out target is not automatically a purely stimulus-driven target.
Study instruction:
- "Create one pop-out and one conjunction display, then specify how the target identity is learned and what makes it relevant to the task."
Key point:
- Search-display geometry and attentional control are different dimensions.
- Both pop-out and conjunction tasks can involve learned goals.
Source links:
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Desimone and Duncan: Neural mechanisms of selective visual attention
- Hasegawa et al.: Search target selection in prefrontal cortex
38. Q: What is the enhancement effect in visual attention?
A: Show answer
The enhancement effect is the increased neural response to a stimulus when it appears at an attended location or has an attended feature, compared with an otherwise comparable unattended stimulus. It has been observed in several visual and oculomotor structures, including superior colliculus, posterior parietal cortex, FEF, and extrastriate visual areas. Enhancement is a physiological correlate of selective processing; by itself it does not reveal whether the recorded region initiates attention or merely receives its effects. The chapter uses the effect to explain how selecting a goal can increase the representation of that goal across a distributed visual network.
Study instruction:
- "Define the attended-versus-unattended comparison and identify the stimulus and task variables that must be held constant."
Key point:
- Enhancement is a stronger response for attended than unattended information.
- A neural correlate alone does not establish causal direction.
Source links:
- Desimone and Duncan: Neural mechanisms of selective visual attention
- Treue and Maunsell: Attention in areas MT and MST
- McAdams and Maunsell: Attention and orientation tuning in V4
39. Q: How can attention enhance neural responses in the FEF and posterior visual cortex?
A: Show answer
When a location or feature is behaviorally relevant, neurons representing it can show stronger sensory responses than when the same information is irrelevant. The chapter reviews findings of enhanced responses in FEF and in posterior visual regions such as V4, MT, and MST. This enhancement can occur during overt attention, when the eyes orient to a target, and covert attention, when gaze remains fixed. A plausible mechanism is that frontal signals increase the competitive advantage of representations matching the current goal. The exact size and form of enhancement depend on the visual area, stimulus, task, and experimental measure.
Study instruction:
- "Compare response changes reported in FEF, V4, and motion-sensitive areas, noting whether the study measured spatial or feature attention."
Key point:
- Attention modulates sensory processing at multiple levels of the visual system.
- The effect depends on the relevant stimulus dimension and task.
Source links:
- Treue and Maunsell: Attention in areas MT and MST
- McAdams and Maunsell: Attention and orientation tuning in V4
- Armstrong and Moore: FEF microstimulation and visual cortical responses
40. Q: What did FEF microstimulation studies reveal about top-down influence on area V4?
A: Show answer
Armstrong and Moore applied intracortical microstimulation to the FEF and found that responses in V4 could be enhanced for stimuli at a particular region of visual space. Because stimulation was delivered to the FEF while visual responses were measured elsewhere, the experiment provided causal evidence that FEF activity can influence visual cortical processing. The result is consistent with a mechanism in which a selected spatial goal biases sensory competition in favour of stimuli at that location. Microstimulation is an artificial perturbation, however, so it does not prove that natural attention always uses an identical pattern or that FEF is the only source of top-down bias.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how microstimulation differs from correlational recording and identify what causal conclusion it supports and what it leaves open."
Key point:
- FEF stimulation can modulate V4 sensory responses in a spatially specific way.
- Causal influence does not establish that FEF is the sole attentional controller.
Source links:
- Armstrong and Moore: FEF microstimulation and visual cortical responses
- Desimone and Duncan: Neural mechanisms of selective visual attention
41. Q: How does biased competition explain the top-down effect of caudal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Biased competition proposes that multiple visual representations compete for limited processing and behavioural influence. Signals reflecting the current goal can bias this competition so that neurons representing the relevant object, location, or feature gain an advantage over competing distractors. In the chapter's account, caudal PF activity can represent a selected goal and send biasing signals directly or indirectly to posterior visual areas. Sensory populations then process goal-matching information more strongly or reach decision thresholds sooner. This is a mechanistic framework for linking learned goal selection with enhanced sensory responses, not a claim that every detail of the circuit has been established.
Study instruction:
- "Draw two competing stimulus representations and add a goal-dependent bias; predict how the winning representation and response latency should change."
Key point:
- Goal signals bias competition among sensory representations.
- Biased competition links frontal selection to enhanced posterior processing.
Source links:
- Desimone and Duncan: Neural mechanisms of selective visual attention
- Armstrong and Moore: FEF microstimulation and visual cortical responses
- Zhou and Desimone: Feature-based attention in FEF and V4
42. Q: How can learned stimulus features influence caudal PF activity?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews evidence that the proportion of caudal PF neurons representing a stimulus dimension changes with task demands. When colour, shape, or another feature becomes relevant for reward, more neurons may encode that dimension; when it is irrelevant, fewer may do so. More rostral PF regions can represent the currently relevant dimension and influence caudal regions through their interconnections or direct projections to sensory cortex. The caudal network can then help direct attention toward the feature that identifies the learned target. This is a flexible task-dependent representation, not a fixed preference for one sensory feature.
Study instruction:
- "Design a task in which colour and motion relevance alternate across blocks, and predict how neural coding should change when the rule changes."
Key point:
- Task relevance can reshape which stimulus dimensions are represented.
- Rostral PF signals can influence caudal selection and posterior sensory processing.
Source links:
- Zhou and Desimone: Feature-based attention in FEF and V4
- Treue and Maunsell: Attention in areas MT and MST
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
43. Q: What evidence suggests that FEF signals can precede feature-related enhancement in V4?
A: Show answer
Zhou and Desimone recorded simultaneously from FEF and V4 while monkeys performed visual search. Both areas showed feature-attention effects, but the timing of the changes was consistent with FEF providing a top-down bias that influenced V4 processing. Earlier occurrence in FEF is compatible with a directing role because a source signal should precede its downstream effect. Timing alone is not conclusive proof of causation: shared input, recurrent interactions, and other control regions can also contribute. The chapter therefore combines latency evidence with stimulation and lesion findings to build a stronger, but still qualified, causal account.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why temporal precedence is suggestive but not sufficient for causation, and identify a perturbation that could strengthen the inference."
Key point:
- FEF enhancement preceded the corresponding V4 effect in the reported task.
- Latency evidence should be combined with causal intervention.
Source links:
- Zhou and Desimone: Feature-based attention in FEF and V4
- Armstrong and Moore: FEF microstimulation and visual cortical responses
- Morishima et al.: Task-specific prefrontal signals to visual cortex
44. Q: What role might gamma-band synchrony play in FEF–V4 communication?
A: Show answer
Gregoriou and colleagues recorded from FEF and V4 while monkeys attended to visual stimuli. Attention increased oscillatory coupling between the areas, particularly in the gamma-frequency range, and the temporal relationship was consistent with FEF-leading interactions. Synchrony may help coordinate when signals from one area arrive relative to the excitability of neurons in another, thereby improving communication of goal-relevant information. The chapter presents this as a possible mechanism rather than a settled explanation. Oscillatory coupling is evidence of coordinated activity; it does not by itself identify the full causal pathway or show that gamma synchrony is necessary for every attentional effect.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish spike-rate enhancement from inter-area synchrony, and summarize what paired recordings can reveal about communication timing."
Key point:
- Attention is associated with increased FEF–V4 gamma coupling.
- Synchrony is a candidate communication mechanism, not definitive proof of necessity.
Source links:
- Gregoriou et al.: Prefrontal–visual cortical coupling during attention
- Zhou and Desimone: Feature-based attention in FEF and V4
45. Q: What does stimulation of human prefrontal cortex suggest about feature-specific top-down control?
A: Show answer
Morishima and colleagues used transcranial magnetic stimulation over human prefrontal cortex while participants prepared to judge either motion or face-related information. The stimulation influenced activity in posterior visual regions relevant to the attended dimension, including motion-sensitive and face-processing areas. The result supports the idea that prefrontal signals can be routed according to the feature or task dimension currently selected. Because the experiment used a perturbation and measured downstream responses, it strengthens the causal case for top-down modulation. It does not imply that the FEF alone controls every form of feature attention or that the same pathway operates identically in all tasks.
Study instruction:
- "Map each task instruction to its relevant posterior visual region and explain why task-specific effects are stronger evidence than a uniform response."
Key point:
- Prefrontal perturbation can influence posterior processing in a task-specific manner.
- Top-down control is selective rather than a generic increase in all visual activity.
Source links:
- Morishima et al.: Task-specific prefrontal signals to visual cortex
- Zhou and Desimone: Feature-based attention in FEF and V4
46. Q: Why does an enhancement effect not automatically prove that the FEF caused attention?
A: Show answer
An enhanced response in FEF may be a cause of attentional selection, a consequence of selection initiated elsewhere, or part of a recurrent loop involving frontal, parietal, and visual regions. Recording a larger response at an attended location shows a relationship but cannot establish direction. Evidence becomes stronger when methods are combined: anatomical pathways establish plausible routes, timing can identify which signal leads, inactivation tests whether a region is necessary for a behavior, and stimulation tests whether perturbing it can influence downstream processing. Even these methods have limits, because perturbations can affect connected structures and artificial stimulation may not reproduce normal activity.
Study instruction:
- "For one attentional claim, list the separate contributions of anatomy, recording, inactivation, microstimulation, and TMS."
Key point:
- Correlation, temporal precedence, necessity, and causal influence are distinct evidential claims.
- Converging methods provide stronger inference than any one method.
Source links:
- Armstrong and Moore: FEF microstimulation and visual cortical responses
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Morishima et al.: Task-specific prefrontal signals to visual cortex
- Gregoriou et al.: Prefrontal–visual cortical coupling during attention
47. Q: How does the orbital PF cortex contribute value information to caudal PF search?
A: Show answer
The chapter links caudal PF goal search to value information supplied indirectly by orbital PF regions. Orbital PF cortex is proposed to represent specific sensory properties of outcomes, such as the sight, smell, taste, and texture of foods, and to represent value in a more common currency. It can update these representations as biological needs change. Through interconnections within prefrontal cortex, information about which visible objects are currently desirable can influence caudal PF search and attentional priority. The caudal region is therefore not proposed to calculate all outcome value itself; it uses value-related information to guide the search for candidate goals.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a hypothetical food choice from current need, to outcome valuation, to visual search and attentional selection."
Key point:
- Orbital PF contributes outcome-value information; caudal PF uses it to guide search.
- The value of a goal depends on current biological needs.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
48. Q: How do orbital and caudal PF cortex work together in the chapter's proposed circuit?
A: Show answer
The proposed circuit begins with orbital PF representations that associate stimuli with outcomes and update their value according to current needs. Interconnections among prefrontal areas convey this value information to caudal PF regions. Caudal PF cortex then searches visual scenes, prioritizes objects or places that match valuable goals, and directs covert or overt attention toward them. Posterior visual areas provide detailed sensory information, while oculomotor pathways allow the FEF to orient the eyes. The selected target can then guide premotor and motor mechanisms that achieve the outcome. This is a distributed, recurrent model rather than a one-way chain with a single controlling centre.
Study instruction:
- "Build a circuit diagram showing valuation, goal selection, sensory enhancement, eye orienting, and action execution as distinct stages with feedback connections."
Key point:
- Value, attentional selection, and action execution are complementary functions.
- The proposed circuit depends on interactions among multiple cortical and subcortical regions.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Desimone and Duncan: Neural mechanisms of selective visual attention
49. Q: Why might posterior parietal cortex be less able than caudal PF cortex to prioritize goals by specific outcome value?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that posterior parietal cortex has strong links to visuospatial and action-related processing but receives relatively little detailed information about specific food and fluid outcomes from orbital PF cortex or other sources. It therefore may represent where a target is and how to orient toward it without integrating the same detailed outcome information that caudal PF cortex can receive through prefrontal connections. This is a comparative claim about connectivity and functional specialization, not a claim that parietal cortex has no role in attention or value-guided behavior. Both regions contribute to attentional networks, but their inputs and computations differ.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the outcome-related inputs and visuospatial connections of caudal PF and posterior parietal cortex."
Key point:
- Parietal cortex contributes spatial selection but has less direct access to specific outcome representations.
- Connectivity may explain differences in value-guided goal selection.
Source links:
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Astafiev et al.: Attending, looking, and pointing
50. Q: How does the chapter distinguish caudal PF cortex from temporal visual cortex in value-guided attention?
A: Show answer
Temporal visual cortex contributes important information about object identity and visual features, and it receives some inputs related to emotional or motivational significance. The chapter argues, however, that it does not combine detailed olfactory, gustatory, and visceral outcome information with visual object representations in the same way as orbital and other PF regions. Caudal PF cortex can receive prefrontally mediated information about the current desirability of a goal and use it to direct attention. The distinction is therefore not that temporal cortex cannot represent objects, but that the prefrontal network is better placed to link those objects to specific outcomes and current needs.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the sensory and outcome information available to temporal cortex and to orbital PF cortex, then explain how their contributions differ."
Key point:
- Temporal cortex supports object representation; PF networks integrate object information with current outcome value.
- Different inputs support different forms of goal selection.
Source links:
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
51. Q: How does the chapter's model explain visual foraging in early primates?
A: Show answer
The authors propose that early primates faced cluttered visual environments in which they needed to locate food, signs of food, and useful places. Orbital PF regions could assign learned value to visible items according to current needs, while caudal PF cortex could search for those items and direct covert attention toward them. Because the chapter argues that early primates lacked a fovea, this search would have relied on covert attention rather than modern foveation. Together, the two PF systems could improve the selection of valuable objects and locations before an action was undertaken to obtain the food or other outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Translate the proposed circuit into a foraging sequence, distinguishing value learning, search, attention, and the action that obtains food."
Key point:
- The model links learned outcome value with covert visual search.
- The evolutionary account is a proposed explanation of early primate foraging.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Amiez and Petrides: Anatomical organization of primate eye fields
52. Q: What are the main limitations of the chapter's account of caudal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter offers a synthesis and functional proposal, not a complete mechanistic proof. Anatomical connections identify possible pathways but do not uniquely specify their computations. Delay-period activity can mix attention, memory, rule coding, and response preparation, so task interpretation remains contested. Lesions and inactivation can affect connected circuits and may impair sensory processing, orienting, or response precision as well as attention. Human and macaque frontal anatomy also differs, complicating direct translation. Finally, the evolutionary reconstruction of pre-foveal primate function is indirect. These limitations do not invalidate the model; they define where alternative explanations and stronger experiments are needed.
Study instruction:
- "Write a balanced evaluation with at least three supporting findings and three unresolved inferential limitations."
Key point:
- The proposal is supported by converging evidence but is not a final account of every PF signal.
- Task design, species differences, and causal inference remain important constraints.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Tsujimoto and Postle: Reconsidering the mnemonic scotoma
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Amiez and Petrides: Anatomical organization of primate eye fields
53. Q: What experiments would most directly test whether caudal PF cortex selects goals according to current value?
A: Show answer
A strong test would manipulate outcome value independently of stimulus salience and spatial location. For example, animals could learn that two visually matched objects predict different outcomes, then undergo a selective change in biological need or outcome devaluation. Researchers could measure whether caudal PF activity and covert-search performance shift toward the currently valuable object while controlling gaze, sensory discriminability, and motor demands. Reversible perturbation of orbital-to-caudal pathways could test whether value information is necessary for the shift. The design should distinguish altered valuation from impaired visual detection or saccade execution.
Study instruction:
- "Design a factorial experiment varying learned outcome value, physical salience, and current need, and specify behavioural and neural dependent measures."
Key point:
- Value must be manipulated independently of visual salience and motor demands.
- Pathway perturbation can test whether orbital input is necessary for value-guided search.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Armstrong and Moore: FEF microstimulation and visual cortical responses
54. Q: How should researchers distinguish attention deficits from basic visual or oculomotor deficits after PF disruption?
A: Show answer
Researchers should use matched control conditions that isolate each component. Visible-target saccades assess basic eye-movement accuracy without a memory delay. Covert-search tasks with enforced fixation test target selection without eye movements. Simple detection tasks assess whether the stimulus can be detected at all, while feature discrimination tasks test sensory quality. Delayed-response tasks add memory and prospective-goal demands. Eye position, reaction time, accuracy, endpoint distribution, and corrective movements should be measured separately. A deficit in one measure should not be labelled an attention or memory deficit until plausible sensory and motor explanations have been tested.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a control matrix pairing each proposed impairment with a task that isolates it and an outcome measure that can detect it."
Key point:
- No single behavioural deficit uniquely identifies an attentional mechanism.
- Separate sensory detection, covert selection, memory, and motor precision.
Source links:
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Dias and Segraves: FEF inactivation and saccades
- Funahashi et al.: Prefrontal lesions and oculomotor delayed response
- Tsujimoto and Postle: Reconsidering the mnemonic scotoma
