55. Q: What is the significance of the chapter's distinction between finding a goal and achieving it?
A: Show answer
The distinction prevents the FEF's saccade-related activity from being mistaken for the whole motor process that obtains a reward. Searching for a food item, shifting attention toward it, and bringing it to the mouth are different stages. Caudal PF cortex is proposed to help find and select the goal; premotor and motor systems transform the selected target into movements that achieve it. This division is useful because a region may issue or bias orienting signals without implementing the full action sequence. It also explains why eye movements can be considered attentional acts in this framework, even though they are movements in a physiological sense.
Study instruction:
- "Take a real-world action such as picking up a cup and divide it into target valuation, visual search, orienting, reach planning, grasp, and outcome."
Key point:
- Goal search and attention are distinct from the motor mechanisms that obtain the outcome.
- The same behaviour can involve multiple interacting control systems.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Milner and Goodale: Two visual systems re-viewed
- Astafiev et al.: Attending, looking, and pointing
56. Q: How can the chapter's framework be applied to human visual attention research?
A: Show answer
The framework predicts that human FEF regions should participate in covert attention and in selecting visually relevant goals, not only in generating saccades. Human imaging can compare attending, looking, and pointing to the same location to separate attentional preparation from effector-specific motor planning. Stimulation studies can test whether prefrontal perturbation changes processing in feature-selective posterior areas. Translation must remain cautious because the location and organization of frontal eye fields differ across species, and human imaging measures population-level hemodynamic signals rather than single-neuron codes. The model is therefore a guide for designing human experiments, not a claim that every macaque circuit maps exactly onto a human region.
Study instruction:
- "Propose a human experiment with separate attend, look, and point conditions and explain how it controls for eye movements."
Key point:
- The model predicts an attentional role for human FEF beyond saccade production.
- Cross-species anatomical and measurement differences limit direct equivalence.
Source links:
- Astafiev et al.: Attending, looking, and pointing
- Amiez and Petrides: Anatomical organization of primate eye fields
- Morishima et al.: Task-specific prefrontal signals to visual cortex
57. Q: What integrated account best summarizes the chapter's evidence and proposal?
A: Show answer
The chapter's integrated account is that orbital PF cortex represents and updates the value of possible outcomes, while caudal PF cortex uses that information to search visual scenes and orient attention toward the most valuable learned goals. Connections with dorsal and ventral visual streams supply spatial and object information; feedback from caudal PF can enhance relevant sensory representations. The FEF contributes to overt orienting through direct and indirect oculomotor pathways and to covert attention when gaze remains fixed. Delay-period activity can reflect attention, prospective goal coding, memory, or task rules, so it should not be treated as proof of a unitary spatial-memory function. Motor systems then achieve the selected goal.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the complete circuit from current need to outcome value, visual goal search, attentional enhancement, orienting, and action execution."
Key point:
- Value-guided search and attention are the proposed core functions of caudal PF cortex.
- The chapter favours a distributed goal-selection account over a memory-only or motor-only account.
Source links:
- Oxford Academic: The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex
- Petrides and Pandya: Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and connections
- Lebedev et al.: Attended versus remembered locations
- Wardak et al.: FEF contribution to covert visual attention
- Zhou and Desimone: Feature-based attention in FEF and V4

Table 6
Dorsal Prefrontal Cortex: Context, Goal Generation, and Action Planning
1. Q: What is the central proposed function of the dorsal prefrontal cortex in this chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that the dorsal prefrontal (PF) cortex generates goals appropriate to the current context and the desired outcome, then prospectively represents those goals until an attempt can be made to achieve them. The context is not limited to the location of a visual cue. It can include the order, timing, duration, distance, number, size, and speed of recent events. The mid-lateral PF cortex, especially area 46, is the principal focus because its connections combine information about events, outcomes, and action possibilities. Its role is therefore framed as context-sensitive goal generation and planning, rather than as a store for spatial information alone.
Study instruction:
- "State the chapter's proposal in one sentence, then identify the contextual features and connections that make the proposal possible."
Key point:
- Dorsal PF cortex uses recent-event context to generate current and future goals.
- Prospective goal coding helps preserve the selected goal until action is possible.
2. Q: What does the chapter mean by the current context for choosing a goal?
A: Show answer
The current context is the information that determines which goal is appropriate at a particular choice point. It combines stimuli available at the time of choice with memory for relevant recent events. In delayed matching-to-sample, for example, the subject sees a sample and later chooses among comparison stimuli. The comparison stimuli alone do not determine the correct response; the remembered sample changes their meaning. In more complex sequences, the context must also distinguish which of several recent events is relevant now. The chapter argues that the dorsal PF cortex integrates such contextual information so that a goal reflects the right event, at the right point in a sequence, rather than a stale cue or an average of previous trials.
Study instruction:
- "For a delayed matching-to-sample example, separate information currently visible from information carried forward from the sample and explain how both determine the response."
Key point:
- Current context combines present stimuli with relevant recent-event information.
- The same visible options can imply different goals under different remembered contexts.
3. Q: Why might anthropoid primates have benefited from an expanded dorsal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter links the evolution of the dorsal PF cortex in anthropoid primates to the demands of daytime foraging. Long foraging excursions consume energy and expose animals to predation, so efficient selection and sequencing of reaches can be advantageous. Anthropoids also have high-acuity foveal vision and trichromatic colour vision, making the location, identity, timing, and order of visual events useful for choosing what to reach for next. The proposed advantage is not simply better seeing or remembering. It is the ability to use recent visual events to select a goal, organize a sequence of goals, and revise that sequence as new events occur. The evolutionary account is presented as a functional proposal, not as a directly demonstrated single cause of cortical expansion.
Study instruction:
- "Connect the proposed ecological pressure, visual capacities, and goal-sequencing function without treating the evolutionary explanation as proven causation."
Key point:
- The proposed adaptation concerns efficient visually guided foraging and sequential reaching.
- The chapter presents an evolutionary interpretation supported by converging functional arguments.
4. Q: How does the chapter define the anatomical territory of the mid-lateral and dorsal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
In macaques, the mid-lateral PF cortex occupies the rostral two-thirds of the principal sulcus and extends onto the convexity cortex dorsal and ventral to it. The authors distinguish this region from the caudal third, which they call postero-lateral PF cortex. In humans, the corresponding divisions are described within the middle frontal gyrus, between the superior and inferior frontal sulci. The chapter uses dorsal PF cortex for cortex in both banks of the principal sulcus and the dorsal convexity cortex of area 9, while excluding medial parts of area 9. These terms are intended to reduce ambiguity in the literature, where area 46 and dorsolateral prefrontal cortex have been used inconsistently.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch the macaque principal sulcus and label the rostral mid-lateral and caudal postero-lateral divisions; note the human anatomical description separately."
Key point:
- The authors use connection-informed regional terms to avoid loose use of area 46 and dorsolateral PF cortex.
- Anatomical labels must be interpreted in relation to the landmarks and definitions used by each study.
5. Q: Why does the chapter treat cortical connections as an anatomical fingerprint?
A: Show answer
The pattern of connections helps explain what information a cortical area can receive, integrate, and send onward. The mid-lateral PF cortex is notable because it connects with posterior parietal areas, premotor regions, other PF regions including orbital PF cortex, and structures linked indirectly with the hippocampal system. This combination gives it access to spatial and temporal context, object and outcome information, and mechanisms for implementing actions. The chapter argues that no other cortical area has the same pattern. Its proposed function follows from this distinctive convergence, not from one isolated connection or one neural response. Connectional anatomy therefore provides a framework for interpreting why a lesion in one region can have effects that a lesion elsewhere does not.
Study instruction:
- "Create a connection map with each input or output labelled by the kind of information or action it may contribute."
Key point:
- The mid-lateral PF cortex is positioned to integrate several information streams.
- A region's function is interpreted partly through its distinctive pattern of connections.
6. Q: What information does the posterior parietal cortex contribute to the dorsal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The posterior parietal cortex supplies more than spatial coordinates. Its neurons and associated human imaging findings are linked to visuospatial information, time intervals, recency, temporal and spatial order, quantity, size, speed, length, and distance. Because the mid-lateral PF cortex receives strong projections from caudal parietal areas, these signals can inform which recent event is relevant and what goal should follow. This connection helps explain performance on delayed-response and n-back tasks, where the correct response depends on selecting an item according to its position or order in a sequence. The chapter therefore interprets parietal input as information for selecting and controlling visually guided action, not merely as a spatial memory feed.
Study instruction:
- "List at least five non-location features associated with posterior parietal processing and explain how each could change a goal choice."
Key point:
- Parietal input carries spatial and temporal metrics, including order and distance.
- The dorsal PF cortex uses this input to establish action-relevant context.
7. Q: How do multimodal, object-related, and somatosensory connections broaden the inputs to area 46?
A: Show answer
The mid-lateral PF cortex connects with area TPO, a superior temporal polysensory region whose cells respond to visual, auditory, and somatosensory stimuli. It also receives input from the perirhinal cortex, associated in the chapter with object identification, and from secondary somatosensory area S2 and inferior parietal area PFG. These connections mean that its contextual representations need not be restricted to visual location. They can incorporate object identity and bodily or tactile information. The chapter notes that somatosensory responses distinguish mid-lateral PF cortex from caudal and postero-lateral PF regions, whose cells are described as having mainly visual and attentional properties. The functional contribution of each input is not equally established, so the connections support an integration hypothesis rather than proving every possible computation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the information associated with area TPO, perirhinal cortex, S2, and PFG, and distinguish demonstrated connections from proposed functional implications."
Key point:
- Area 46 receives multimodal, object-related, and somatosensory information.
- These inputs broaden contextual integration beyond a purely visual-spatial account.
8. Q: What is the significance of dorsal PF connections with premotor cortex and preSMA?
A: Show answer
The mid-lateral PF cortex connects with dorsal and ventral premotor cortex, pre-supplementary motor area, and rostral cingulate motor area. The chapter emphasizes that these projections preferentially involve representations of the hand and arm rather than the foot and leg. Such a pattern is consistent with a role in selecting and organizing reaching, manipulation, and feeding movements, rather than locomotion. The connections provide a route by which a selected goal can influence the motor systems that achieve it. They also support the proposal that the dorsal PF cortex can generate an ordered series of goals, while premotor areas help translate those goals into movement sequences. This does not mean that area 46 directly executes the movement; it contributes to goal specification and coordination with action-related regions.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a hypothetical reach from contextual cue to selected goal to premotor implementation, identifying which stage each connection supports."
Key point:
- Dorsal PF output is strongly linked to hand- and arm-related action systems.
- Goal generation and movement execution are related but distinguishable functions.
9. Q: How does orbital PF cortex contribute to goal generation by the dorsal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The orbital PF cortex (OFC) supplies information about specific outcomes and their value in relation to current needs. The chapter describes outcome information as arriving first in granular OFC and then reaching mid-lateral PF cortex through intrinsic PF connections. This allows area 46 to combine event order and spatial context from parietal inputs with information about what a potential goal is expected to produce. The distinction matters because a location or cue alone may not be enough to determine the best goal; the expected outcome also matters. In the strategy task discussed later, OFC strategy signals appear earlier than corresponding signals in mid-lateral PF cortex, while the latter region develops a goal signal. The proposed division is that OFC contributes outcome and strategy information, while dorsal PF cortex uses it to generate the current goal.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why event-order information and predicted-outcome information are complementary rather than interchangeable inputs."
Key point:
- OFC contributes specific outcome and value information to dorsal PF goal selection.
- Area 46 integrates outcome information with recent-event context.
10. Q: What does the classical delayed response task test?
A: Show answer
In the classical delayed response task, a monkey observes an experimenter place food in one of two wells. A delay follows, during which an opaque screen can prevent the monkey from seeing the relevant location. The screen is then raised and the monkey chooses a well by displacing a cover. A correct choice is rewarded. The appropriate goal depends on the earlier visual event, not simply on the stimuli present at the moment of reaching. The task therefore requires the subject to use a recent cue to select a later goal after intervening time and other trial events. The chapter focuses on this task because performance depends critically on the mid-lateral PF cortex and because it exposes the difficulty of using the most recent relevant event in the face of interference.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the cue, delay, choice, and feedback phases; identify which event determines the correct goal and what information is unavailable during the delay."
Key point:
- The task tests goal selection based on a preceding cue after a delay.
- The relevant problem is linking the earlier event to the later action.
11. Q: What happens after lesions of the mid-lateral PF cortex on the classical delayed response task?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports a profound and persistent impairment. Monkeys trained before surgery perform at approximately chance level after permanent mid-lateral PF lesions, making as many frank errors as correct choices. Monkeys first exposed to the task after such lesions may fail to learn it even when the delay is only one second. The chapter contrasts this with the milder effects of caudal or postero-lateral PF lesions on the oculomotor delayed response task. The severity of the classical task deficit is important evidence that area 46 is necessary for using a prior event to generate a later reach goal under the task's conditions. It does not, by itself, establish whether the impaired process is retrospective memory, order coding, rule use, or prospective goal coding; the chapter evaluates these alternatives separately.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the reported behavioural result from the theoretical explanation proposed to account for it."
Key point:
- Mid-lateral PF lesions can reduce classical delayed-response performance to chance.
- A lesion effect establishes necessity under task conditions, not a unique cognitive mechanism.
12. Q: Why can classical and oculomotor delayed-response tasks produce different lesion effects?
A: Show answer
The tasks differ in both the required response and the opportunity to use attention during the delay. The oculomotor version requires a saccade and may leave the goal location visible in peripheral vision, allowing covert attention to remain directed toward it. In the classical Wisconsin General Testing Apparatus procedure, an opaque screen descends during the delay, making covert visual attention to the baited well more difficult. The classical task also requires a reach that displaces a cover, so errors are recorded as frank wrong choices rather than as small accuracy deviations. These methodological differences can make deficits appear more or less severe. The chapter cautions against treating the two tasks as interchangeable measures of one undifferentiated spatial-memory ability.
Study instruction:
- "Compare response type, visibility during the delay, and error measurement in the two task versions before interpreting lesion results."
Key point:
- Task design changes the strategies available to the subject and the errors researchers can observe.
- Different delayed-response variants should not be assumed to measure identical processes.
13. Q: What evidence indicates that the delay period is critical for the mid-lateral PF contribution?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes conditional visuomotor tasks with spatial cues and spatial goals but no delay period. Monkeys with mid-lateral and postero-lateral PF lesions could learn such a task normally. In contrast, inactivation of mid-lateral PF cortex during the early delay period in delayed-response trials reduced performance to chance, while stimulation of postero-lateral cortex had a smaller effect. Lesion findings on delayed alternation also show a stronger effect for damage involving the central principal-sulcus region than for more posterior damage. Together, these results indicate that the impairment is not simply a general inability to map a visual cue onto a spatial response. The intervening delay and the need to preserve or use the cue-derived goal across that interval are central to the deficit.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a no-delay control condition and explain why normal performance on it narrows the interpretation of a delayed-task deficit."
Key point:
- Area 46 is especially important when a cue must guide a later choice across a delay.
- A delay manipulation helps separate general visuomotor mapping from context maintenance and goal selection.
14. Q: What are the competing interpretations of delay-period activity in the mid-lateral PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Delay-period activity has often been interpreted as retrospective spatial working memory because it can encode the location of a cue after the cue disappears. The chapter notes, however, that delay activity occurs in many PF and non-PF regions and does not always represent remembered location. Some activity reflects attended locations, and some may represent the current goal or planned action. In experiments where a cue remains visible, many cells show equal or greater activity than when it disappears, which weakens a simple interpretation as storage of an absent cue. The chapter therefore distinguishes retrospective coding of a past cue from prospective coding of a future goal, while acknowledging that both kinds of cells exist. Neural activity during a delay must be interpreted through the task's control conditions, not by timing alone.
Study instruction:
- "Define retrospective, attentional, and prospective interpretations, then identify what experimental control would help distinguish them."
Key point:
- Delay-period firing is not automatically evidence of retrospective working memory.
- The represented information must be inferred from task comparisons and response demands.
15. Q: What is prospective coding, and how does it differ from retrospective coding?
A: Show answer
Retrospective coding represents a past event, such as the location where a cue appeared. Prospective coding represents a future goal or action that the subject intends to carry out. In the chapter's account, a monkey can encode the goal location as soon as the cue makes that goal identifiable, rather than continuing to maintain only the cue as a remembered sensory event. This goal representation can persist until movement is permitted. Prospective coding is therefore action-oriented: it translates contextual information into a selected future target. The distinction is not that retrospective information is absent from the PF cortex; recordings show cells for previous and current goals. The proposal is that prospective representations predominate in many relevant conditions and help the subject resist interference from other recent events.
Study instruction:
- "For one delayed-response trial, label the cue representation and the future-goal representation separately, including when each becomes useful."
Key point:
- Retrospective coding represents what happened; prospective coding represents what is to be done.
- Prospective coding links context to a future action goal.
16. Q: How can interference between successive trials impair delayed-response performance?
A: Show answer
Across a session, monkeys encounter repeated cues and choose among the same limited set of locations. Earlier cues and goals can therefore compete with the current trial's relevant event. A subject that does not distinguish the most recent cue from earlier cues may choose a location based on stale information or on the average reinforcement history. The chapter discusses evidence that lesioned monkeys can perform well when the current correct location happens to match the previous two trials but fall to chance when it differs. This pattern is not simple perseveration, which would predict below-chance responding. Instead, the animals appear unable to base the choice reliably on the single event that currently matters. Interference is thus a problem of selecting and using the relevant event within a sequence, not merely forgetting all spatial information.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a three-trial cue sequence and predict how an animal that relies on older trial history could respond on the final trial."
Key point:
- Repeated cues and goals create competition among memories across trials.
- The current goal must be selected from the most relevant event, not an average of prior outcomes.
17. Q: What does the A-not-B task illustrate about the use of recent events?
A: Show answer
In the A-not-B task, an infant first retrieves an object hidden at location A and later sees it hidden at location B. Some infants nevertheless reach back to A, even when the object at B can be seen under a transparent cover. The chapter interprets this as a tendency to repeat a recently reinforced choice rather than use the latest hiding event to generate the current goal. With development, children become better at using the most recent event. The example illustrates a general conflict between prior goal or choice history and a new event that should determine the next action. It provides an intuitive comparison for the delayed-response impairment: the challenge may involve giving the latest relevant event control over the current goal, not simply retaining a location in memory.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why reaching to A after the object is moved to B is not adequately described as a failure to see the new event."
Key point:
- A-not-B demonstrates competition between a recent reinforced choice and a newer cue.
- Successful goal selection requires updating behaviour when context changes.
18. Q: Why is the n-back task relevant to the chapter's account of dorsal PF function?
A: Show answer
In an n-back task, the subject sees a sequence of items and must identify the item that appeared a specified number of events earlier. The correct response depends on temporal order, not just on the presence or identity of an item. In the three-back version, for example, the relevant item is the one three events before the current point. The chapter reports that mid-lateral PF activation appears when order is relevant, whereas a simpler spatial-memory task without an order requirement activates caudal PF cortex but not mid-lateral PF cortex. This supports the idea that area 46 contributes to identifying which event in a sequence currently governs the response. N-back performance can therefore reflect order processing and interference resolution, not merely a generic capacity to store more items.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a simple location-memory task with a two-back task and identify the extra temporal-order computation required by the latter."
Key point:
- N-back tasks make event order essential to choosing the relevant item.
- Mid-lateral PF involvement may reflect order and interference demands rather than storage alone.
19. Q: What do high- and low-interference n-back conditions reveal?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a three-back face task in which high interference was created by repeatedly using the same set of faces, while low interference used novel faces as distractors. Subjects performed worse when distractors came from the same set as target faces. In the same study, greater mid-lateral PF activation was associated with better performance under high interference. This pattern links area 46 to coping with competition among similar memory representations. The finding does not prove that the region stores the faces themselves. Rather, it is consistent with a role in selecting or maintaining the task-relevant representation when distractors are difficult to distinguish from targets. The manipulation is valuable because it changes interference while keeping the broad task structure comparable.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how reusing target-like distractors increases interference and explain why performance and activation should both be examined."
Key point:
- Similar distractors create stronger competition than novel distractors.
- Mid-lateral PF activation is associated with better performance when interference is high.
20. Q: What do ordered-object tasks show about the role of trial-to-trial interference?
A: Show answer
In ordered-object tasks, a monkey selects among several objects and must avoid choosing objects already selected. Studies using novel objects on each trial found relatively normal performance after some dorsal PF lesions, whereas studies reusing the same objects or pictures across trials found substantial impairment after lesions involving mid-lateral PF or dorsal convexity regions. Reusing stimuli makes it harder to identify which occurrence or choice is currently relevant. The chapter interprets this contrast as evidence that trial-to-trial interference is a major contributor to the deficit. The number of objects also matters: impairment increased when the set expanded from three to five. The result suggests that the dorsal PF cortex helps organize choices when the same representational items recur and their recent order must be tracked.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast novel-item and repeated-item designs, holding the choice structure as constant as possible, and predict where interference should be greater."
Key point:
- Repeated stimuli across trials create interference that novel stimuli can reduce.
- The impairment is sensitive to how much recent history must be distinguished.
21. Q: What are the three explanations the chapter considers for delayed-response impairment?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three related, not mutually exclusive, accounts. The order-coding account proposes that the subject cannot reliably determine which relevant event occurred most recently. The rule-coding account proposes that the subject cannot learn or apply the rule that links the latest cue or previous choice to the current goal. The prospective-coding account proposes that the subject cannot encode the current goal in advance to protect it from competing memories. The evidence reviewed supports elements of all three, and the authors state that they do not know which one accounts for the impairment or whether all contribute. This framing is important because it avoids treating a complex behavioural deficit as proof of one narrowly defined memory mechanism.
Study instruction:
- "Make a three-column comparison of order coding, rule coding, and prospective coding, with one predicted behavioural signature for each."
Key point:
- The three accounts address event selection, rule application, and future-goal protection.
- The chapter leaves open the possibility that the mechanisms operate together.
22. Q: What evidence supports a specific role for the mid-lateral PF cortex in judging temporal order?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews lesion, imaging, and single-cell findings. Monkeys with dorsal or mid-lateral PF damage were impaired when they had to choose between middle items in a sequence according to which appeared earlier or later, even when novel objects reduced trial-to-trial interference. Human imaging found mid-lateral PF activation during judgments about which of two stimuli appeared earlier. In monkey recordings, a substantial proportion of delay-active cells encoded the sequence of visual stimuli or the order of spatial targets. Another study found greater activity for the second than the first picture, which could provide a signal for recency. Together, these findings support a role in representing temporal order, while leaving open how order signals are computed and how they interact with rules and goals.
Study instruction:
- "Separate evidence from lesions, imaging, and neuronal recordings, and state what each method contributes to the order-coding hypothesis."
Key point:
- Order information is represented in mid-lateral PF activity and is behaviourally important.
- Converging methods strengthen the hypothesis but do not specify a single neural algorithm.
23. Q: How might the dorsal PF cortex contribute to learning and maintaining task rules?
A: Show answer
A task rule specifies how current information should be transformed into a choice. In delayed response, the rule is that the most recent relevant visual event determines the current goal. In matching tasks, the rule may be to match by colour or by shape. The chapter reports that PF neurons can encode conditional visuomotor, matching, and nonmatching rules, and that these representations can be prospective. It also describes monkeys with mid-lateral plus postero-lateral PF lesions that could relearn and switch between colour and shape matching rules, but their performance fell to chance after an unusually long intertrial interval. This suggests difficulty maintaining the currently relevant rule across a delay. Rule coding is therefore not just knowing a rule in the abstract; it includes keeping the appropriate rule active when the task context temporarily changes or becomes less immediate.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the rule, cue, and required response in a matching task, then explain how a delay could test rule maintenance."
Key point:
- PF cortex can represent rules that organize how cues determine choices.
- A rule may be learned yet fail when it must be maintained across an interval.
24. Q: What do lose-shift and win-shift strategies mean in delayed alternation?
A: Show answer
In a delayed alternation task, the subject typically chooses the location that was not rewarded on the previous trial. The win-shift rule therefore requires rejecting a recently rewarded location and selecting the alternative. A correction procedure after an error can create a simpler lose-shift opportunity: if a choice fails, reject that location on the next correction trial. The chapter reports that monkeys with dorsal PF lesions took many more trials than normal animals to acquire lose-shift and failed to learn win-shift within the testing limit in the cited experiment. Win-shift is especially demanding because it opposes the natural tendency to repeat a rewarded response. These results support the possibility that dorsal PF damage disrupts the use of recent choice and outcome information to implement an arbitrary rule.
Study instruction:
- "Write the action rule for a regular trial and a correction trial separately; explain why reinforcement history favours win-stay but the task requires win-shift."
Key point:
- Lose-shift uses nonreward to reject a choice; win-shift rejects a previously rewarded choice.
- Dorsal PF damage can impair acquisition of rules that require using recent history in a nonhabitual way.
25. Q: How do separate neural populations for previous and current goals support prospective coding?
A: Show answer
Recordings described in the chapter identified one population of dorsal PF cells encoding the goal from the previous trial and another encoding the current goal. Only a small number of cells showed hybrid coding of both more generally. This separation suggests that the cortex can distinguish retrospective information about what was done from prospective information about what is to be done. If the two representations were not differentiated, the previous goal could intrude on the current choice. The authors propose that a lesion might impair this separation or the ability to establish the prospective goal representation, thereby allowing previous goals to interfere. The finding does not mean that every neuron has one exclusive function in all tasks, but it provides a cellular basis for distinguishing past and future goal signals.
Study instruction:
- "Draw separate timelines for previous-goal and current-goal activity and explain why keeping the representations distinct could reduce perseveration."
Key point:
- Dorsal PF populations can encode previous and current goals separately.
- Prospective representation may prevent an earlier goal from controlling the next choice.
26. Q: What do human matching and recall conditions reveal about prospective coding?
A: Show answer
In the study reviewed, subjects first viewed a sequence of spatial locations and then completed either a matching task or a recall task after a delay. In matching, they judged whether a later sequence matched the original, so they needed to remember the cues but could not plan a sequence of pointing movements during the delay. In recall, they knew they would have to point to the locations in order, allowing them to prepare future actions. Mid-lateral PF delay-period activation was significant in recall but not in matching, even though both conditions required retrospective memory. Caudal PF and posterior parietal regions showed activation in both conditions. This contrast supports the interpretation that mid-lateral PF activation is especially associated with preparing forthcoming goals or actions, rather than with all forms of spatial retention.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why matching and recall are a useful contrast: both require remembering, but only recall permits advance action preparation."
Key point:
- Prospective action preparation distinguishes recall from matching in the cited design.
- Mid-lateral PF delay activity was greater when future goals could be prepared.
27. Q: How does the two-delay-period experiment strengthen the prospective-coding interpretation?
A: Show answer
The follow-up design included two delay periods, but only the second allowed subjects to prepare future goals. The chapter reports significant mid-lateral PF activation during the second delay, not the first. A control condition presented a new sequence during the second delay, requiring subjects to prepare that sequence instead of the original one; activation was also observed in that control. This pattern is consistent with activity reflecting the sequence that is currently being prepared, not simply the memory of the original cues. The chapter acknowledges that imaging sensitivity can complicate claims about absent activation, and other work detected some retrospective delay activity in more rostral dorsal PF regions. The strongest conclusion is therefore a predominance of prospective activation in area 46 under the tested conditions, not an absolute absence of retrospective coding.
Study instruction:
- "Identify what differs between the first and second delays and why the replacement-sequence control is important."
Key point:
- Activation followed the opportunity to prepare forthcoming goals.
- The evidence favours prospective coding without excluding retrospective activity.
28. Q: How does prospective coding protect memory from distraction?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses an accumulator-network idea to explain how a selected goal can resist competing representations. Recent events provide evidence to competing goal representations; the relevant event should push the appropriate goal toward a decision threshold. Prospective PF activity can bias this competition, much like a thumb on the scale, so the current goal remains dominant over less recent cues. In human experiments, subjects remembered sequences of locations while distractors were sometimes inserted. Greater mid-lateral PF delay-period activation was associated with more accurate recall on distractor trials. When distractors were similar to the memoranda, activation was greater than when distractors were from a different category. These findings support a protective role for active maintenance, although the chapter treats the accumulator account as an explanatory model rather than a directly observed neural mechanism.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the accumulator analogy and identify the evidence linking greater delay activity with better performance under distraction."
Key point:
- Prospective activity may bias competition in favour of the current goal.
- Greater mid-lateral PF activation is associated with distractor-resistant performance.
29. Q: What is meant by free selection or willed action in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Free-selection tasks ask subjects to generate a series of actions without each movement being specified by an external cue. For example, subjects may choose among joystick directions or finger movements and vary their choices across trials. The chapter reports repeated mid-lateral PF activation when people generate such variable sequences, whereas activation is not observed in the same way when an arrow externally instructs the movement. Because each new choice depends partly on what has just been selected, subjects must use recent action history to generate the next goal. The authors interpret this as another expression of the dorsal PF function: generating goals from a context that includes recent events and previous choices. It is not simply the execution of arbitrary movements.
Study instruction:
- "Compare self-generated movement choices with externally instructed movements and identify which condition requires using recent choice history."
Key point:
- Free selection requires internally generating goals across successive choices.
- Mid-lateral PF activation is linked to variable goal generation, not movement alone.
30. Q: Why is the absence of mid-lateral PF activation on the first trial of a free-selection series informative?
A: Show answer
The chapter reasons that the first trial has no preceding choice in the series to provide a recent-event context. If mid-lateral PF activation supports generating a goal based on the relationship to previous goals, it should be weak or absent before the first choice and emerge on later trials. An imaging study found activation across a series but no significant activation on the first trial, while activity could be detected on a single middle trial. This control argues against the result being caused merely by insufficient statistical power. The finding supports the claim that area 46 is engaged when a current goal must be selected in relation to prior events. It does not imply that the cortex is inactive at the start of every task, only that this particular sequence-dependent signal lacks its defining context on trial one.
Study instruction:
- "State the prediction for trial one and a middle trial, then explain why detecting activation on a middle trial is a useful sensitivity check."
Key point:
- Sequence-dependent goal generation has no prior choice context on the first trial.
- Activation on later trials is consistent with using recent action history.
31. Q: What do lesion and stimulation findings show about free selection?
A: Show answer
Patients with large PF lesions produced more stereotyped and less random movement sequences than control subjects. Imaging studies found greater mid-lateral PF activation when subjects generated more random number sequences. Repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation over the relevant region made generated sequences more stereotyped. These findings complement activation studies by suggesting that the region is not merely correlated with free selection but contributes causally to the ability to vary choices. The evidence comes from different methods with different spatial and temporal precision, so it should be interpreted as converging support rather than as a complete account of how randomness is computed. The chapter's central point is that generating a non-repetitive sequence requires reference to recent actions and the selection of a new goal in context.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast correlational imaging evidence with lesion and rTMS evidence, and state what causal inference each method permits."
Key point:
- PF damage or disruption can make self-generated action sequences more stereotyped.
- Multiple methods support a necessary contribution to varied goal selection.
32. Q: How does the chapter distinguish generating the next goal from planning a whole sequence?
A: Show answer
Generating the next goal means selecting one action at a time based on the current context, which may include the immediately preceding event or choice. Planning a whole sequence means representing several future goals before beginning execution. The chapter reviews evidence for both. In some tasks, neurons encode the specific sequence just before a series of eye movements; in others, delay-period activity reflects categories or patterns of reaching sequences. In maze tasks, cells can encode subordinate goals needed to reach a final target. These findings suggest that dorsal PF regions can contribute to both trial-by-trial updating and more extended prospective organization. The distinction matters because observing a sequence of successful movements does not by itself show that the entire sequence was planned in advance.
Study instruction:
- "For a multi-step task, mark which decisions could be made online and which would need to be represented before movement begins."
Key point:
- Sequential behaviour can arise from online goal updating or advance sequence planning.
- Neural coding studies are needed to distinguish representations of individual goals from whole sequences.
33. Q: What does sequence-category coding add to the account of goal planning?
A: Show answer
In the reaching-sequence study, monkeys learned multiple four-movement sequences and prepared to repeat them after a delay. Some delay-active neurons responded similarly to sequences sharing a transition pattern, even when the individual movements differed. Examples included double-repeat patterns such as AABB and alternating patterns such as ABAB, contrasted with repetition such as AAAA. Roughly half of the delay-active cells showed category-specific activity, with more such cells dorsal than ventral to the principal sulcus. This suggests that dorsal PF activity can represent an abstract structure shared by different concrete movement sequences. The cortex may therefore encode not only which movement comes next but also the higher-order organization of a sequence.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two sequences with different elements but the same transition pattern and explain what a category-selective neuron would represent."
Key point:
- Some dorsal PF neurons encode abstract sequence patterns rather than only specific movements.
- Sequence structure can be represented independently of the exact action elements.
34. Q: How does the visual-maze task demonstrate subordinate-goal representation?
A: Show answer
In the visual-maze task, a monkey moves a cursor through a maze using a handle to reach a final target. Obstacles can change, requiring the monkey to find a new route. Reaching the final target may require a series of subordinate goals, such as intermediate cursor positions. The chapter reports that different subpopulations of mid-lateral PF cells can encode these subordinate goals, the final goal, or both. By changing the mapping between handle movement and cursor movement, researchers could distinguish a representation of the desired cursor goal from a representation of the limb movement used to achieve it. Most cells represented the goal rather than the specific movement. This supports the idea that PF cortex organizes action around intended outcomes and intermediate targets.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a maze route with one final goal and at least two subordinate goals; distinguish cursor targets from the hand movements used to reach them."
Key point:
- Complex goals can be decomposed into ordered subordinate goals.
- Dorsal PF cells often encode intended goals rather than a fixed motor command.
35. Q: What does the 25-door search task reveal about planning and organization?
A: Show answer
In the 25-door search task, a monkey faces a grid of opaque doors, each hiding a peanut. The efficient strategy is to open each door once and avoid returning to doors already searched. Normal monkeys develop an orderly pattern, whereas monkeys with mid-lateral and postero-lateral PF lesions search in a disorganized way and revisit doors. The task tests the generation of an efficient sequence of reaches to unseen targets. The chapter notes that the impairment could initially be attributed to forgetting which doors were opened, but human studies found reduced regularity even in the starting choice of a search sequence. That observation suggests a planning or organizational deficit beyond simple memory for previous selections. The task also illustrates how a structured strategy can reduce memory demands.
Study instruction:
- "Describe an efficient search path and explain how regularity can reduce the need to remember every previously visited location."
Key point:
- Dorsal PF damage is associated with disorganized search and repeated reaches.
- Planning a regular sequence can reduce memory load while improving efficiency.
36. Q: How can researchers distinguish a planning deficit from a simple memory deficit in spatial search?
A: Show answer
One approach is to examine whether the subject begins with a regular strategy before any previous choices need to be remembered. In the computerized human search studies described, patients with frontal lesions not only revisited previously selected boxes but also showed less regularity in their starting choices. Because the first choice cannot be explained by forgetting an earlier choice in that sequence, the irregularity points toward impaired organization or planning. Researchers can also measure consistency, path length, revisits, and strategy use separately from raw accuracy. A subject may remember individual locations yet fail to arrange choices into an efficient sequence. Conversely, an irregular search path can increase memory demands and make later forgetting more likely, so the two explanations may interact.
Study instruction:
- "Design separate measures for initial strategy, path regularity, revisit rate, and recall of previously visited locations."
Key point:
- Early choices can reveal planning problems before memory for prior choices becomes relevant.
- Memory and planning should be measured separately rather than inferred from one error score.
37. Q: What role does lateral area 9 play in timing and interval reproduction?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes neurons in lateral area 9 that become active after a specified interval has elapsed and others that become active when a monkey reproduces that interval without external timing cues. Lesions of dorsal PF cortex impaired tasks requiring monkeys to distinguish or report different elapsed intervals, and inactivation of lateral area 9 caused errors in reporting time intervals. Some earlier lesion findings suggest that the dorsal convexity, rather than mid-lateral and postero-lateral PF cortex alone, was important for these timing effects. The chapter therefore distinguishes lateral area 9 contributions to interval timing from the better-known area 46 role in delayed-response tasks. Timing is one contextual feature that can help determine when a goal should be selected or an action should occur.
Study instruction:
- "Differentiate a task that requires remembering an interval from one that requires reproducing it, and note the evidence implicating lateral area 9."
Key point:
- Lateral area 9 contains activity related to elapsed and reproduced time intervals.
- Dorsal PF contributions to timing are not confined to area 46.
38. Q: How does the dorsal PF cortex represent distance information?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that neurons in the mid-lateral PF cortex can encode absolute and relative distance. In a discrimination task, monkeys saw two stimuli positioned at different distances from a reference point and had to choose the one farther away. More cells encoded relative distance than absolute distance, and many also represented conjunctions between stimulus features and relative distance. Lesions of dorsal PF cortex impaired a task in which monkeys had to reproduce one of two movement distances and report which distance had been performed. The chapter cautions that such impairment could reflect difficulty judging movement distance, movement duration, or both. Distance is therefore treated as one of several metrics that can define the current context and guide the choice of a goal.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between absolute and relative distance and identify why a movement-distance task may also involve timing."
Key point:
- Dorsal PF activity can represent both absolute and relative distance.
- Distance judgments may be intertwined with movement duration and other contextual features.
39. Q: What is conjunctive coding in the dorsal PF cortex?
A: Show answer
Conjunctive coding occurs when a neuron represents a combination of features rather than one feature in isolation. The chapter gives examples in which cells encode the conjunction of stimulus colour and duration, colour and relative distance, order and duration, or order and distance. Other cells reflect combinations of a spatial cue and the arm required to reach a goal. This differs from a simple representation of duration or location alone. Conjunctive coding allows the system to represent an event as a structured combination of what occurred, when it occurred, where it occurred, and what action or outcome was associated with it. The authors argue that the dorsal PF cortex acts as a convergence point for signals that jointly determine a goal.
Study instruction:
- "Create two-feature examples such as colour-by-duration and order-by-distance, and state what information would be lost if only one feature were represented."
Key point:
- Conjunctive cells encode relationships among multiple event features.
- Integrated representations can provide a richer context for goal generation.
40. Q: Why is temporal order especially important in the conjunctive representations described?
A: Show answer
In studies of duration and distance discrimination, monkeys were not always required to report stimulus order, yet many mid-lateral PF neurons encoded conjunctions involving order and the other task features. For example, cells represented whether the first or second stimulus had lasted longer, or combined order with relative distance. The chapter notes that order-based signals appeared at an intermediate time relative to the later signal encoding the selected goal. This timing suggests that order conjunctions may contribute to the process by which a goal is generated. The finding implies that event order is not merely an optional feature added when a task explicitly asks about sequence; it may be a fundamental organizing dimension in the contextual computations of area 46.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why order coding in a task that does not explicitly ask about order is evidence that order may be integrated into broader representations."
Key point:
- Order can be encoded even when it is not the overt feature being reported.
- Order-related conjunctions may help bridge event context and goal selection.
41. Q: How do orbital and mid-lateral PF cortex differ in representing a stay-or-shift strategy?
A: Show answer
In the strategy task, a visual cue instructed the monkey either to stay with the previous goal or to shift to the alternative. The chapter reports that OFC cells encoded the strategy earlier than mid-lateral PF cells. As the strategy signal developed in mid-lateral PF cortex, a goal signal appeared there as well. The two regions also differed on error trials: OFC continued to encode the correct strategy, whereas the mid-lateral PF strategy signal was weak or absent. The authors interpret this as evidence that OFC supplies a strategy signal and dorsal PF cortex uses it, together with the previous goal, to generate the current goal on correct trials. Later OFC activity can encode the chosen goal at feedback regardless of whether the choice was correct.
Study instruction:
- "Map the timing of strategy and goal signals in OFC and area 46, and compare their behaviour on correct and error trials."
Key point:
- OFC strategy signals precede the corresponding strategy and goal signals in mid-lateral PF cortex.
- The two regions contribute differently to strategy representation, goal generation, and feedback.
42. Q: How does the dorsal PF cortex integrate context, outcomes, and action possibilities?
A: Show answer
The chapter's synthesis is that the posterior parietal cortex contributes spatial and temporal features, including order, duration, and distance; OFC contributes specific outcomes and value; other PF regions and intrinsic connections contribute object, rule, and strategy information; and premotor regions provide routes for achieving selected goals. Mid-lateral PF cortex combines these inputs into representations that can specify a current goal or a sequence of goals. Its conjunctive neurons provide one form of evidence for this integration because they encode combinations that no single input stream alone fully specifies. The system is therefore described as a convergence point within a broader network, not as an isolated executive module. Goal selection depends on context and predicted consequences, while action-related connections make the selected goal implementable.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a network diagram linking parietal context, OFC outcome, intrinsic PF integration, and premotor implementation."
Key point:
- Goal generation depends on combining contextual and outcome information.
- The dorsal PF cortex operates as part of a connected network rather than in isolation.
43. Q: Why do posterior parietal lesions not produce the same delayed-response deficit as mid-lateral PF lesions?
A: Show answer
If posterior parietal input alone were sufficient for delayed-response performance, lesions of posterior parietal cortex should produce a similar impairment. The chapter reports that they do not produce the same deficit on delayed response or delayed alternation tasks. The authors propose that posterior parietal cortex supplies important event-order and spatial information but lacks the specific outcome information that mid-lateral PF cortex receives from OFC. Area 46 can therefore combine event context with the expected outcome and current value of a potential goal. The posterior parietal cortex contributes part of the required information but not the full combination needed to generate a goal based on current context and predicted outcome. This is a connectional explanation for why an input region is not functionally interchangeable with its recipient.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between supplying necessary input and possessing the complete network integration required for a task."
Key point:
- Posterior parietal cortex supplies important context but not all goal-relevant outcome information.
- Area 46's additional OFC connections help explain its distinctive necessity.
44. Q: How does the chapter qualify the traditional spatial working-memory theory?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews the influential view that area 46 is necessary for retrospective spatial working memory, based largely on delayed-response tasks and delay-period activity. It does not deny that some mid-lateral PF neurons encode remembered locations. Instead, it argues that many apparent memory signals can reflect attention or prospective goal coding, and that the task deficits may depend especially on event order, rule use, and interference. Human imaging findings in n-back tasks are interpreted as potentially reflecting order processing rather than a broad nonspatial working-memory function. The authors therefore shift emphasis from storing a cue to using recent events to generate a goal and maintaining that goal prospectively. They reserve a broader discussion of working-memory theory for a later chapter, so the present chapter offers a reinterpretation rather than a final dismissal.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize what the chapter accepts and what it reinterprets in the spatial working-memory account."
Key point:
- Retrospective spatial coding exists but may not explain the core delayed-response deficit by itself.
- The authors emphasize order, rules, prospective goals, and interference.
45. Q: Why is prospective coding not simply another name for working memory?
A: Show answer
Prospective coding refers to the content and purpose of a representation: it specifies a future goal or action. Working memory is a broader term often used for temporary maintenance of information, including past cues. A prospective goal may need to be maintained over a delay, but its function is to guide a forthcoming response and protect that goal from interference. The chapter argues that a task requiring subjects to remember a sequence without knowing what response will be needed differs from one that permits preparation of a future sequence of movements. Mid-lateral PF activation was greater in the latter condition. The distinction is therefore between remembering information for later comparison and preparing an action goal for later execution, even though both may involve sustained activity.
Study instruction:
- "Classify examples as retrospective retention, prospective goal preparation, or both, and justify the classification by the task's response requirements."
Key point:
- Prospective coding is defined by future-goal content, not simply by delay-period duration.
- Memory maintenance and action preparation can overlap but are not identical.
46. Q: What experimental limitations should be considered when interpreting the chapter's evidence?
A: Show answer
Several qualifications recur. Different delayed-response variants permit different strategies, including covert attention, and they record different kinds of errors. Delay-period firing can reflect remembered cues, attended locations, or future goals, so activity timing alone is insufficient. Large lesions may affect several regions, making localization uncertain; stimulation and inactivation provide different forms of causal evidence but also have spatial and temporal limitations. Imaging signals are indirect and may fail to detect weaker activity, so absence of activation should not be treated as proof that no neurons represent a feature. Some behavioural impairments could reflect more than one process, such as distance and duration judgments. The authors explicitly leave open whether order, rule, and prospective coding all contribute to delayed-response deficits.
Study instruction:
- "For any study cited in the chapter, record task variant, lesion or imaging method, control condition, response measure, and alternative interpretations."
Key point:
- Task design and measurement strongly shape what a result can establish.
- The chapter's proposal is integrative and acknowledges unresolved mechanisms.
47. Q: How do coordinate frames and target visibility relate to the proposed role in reaching sequences?
A: Show answer
The chapter connects orderly reaching to the primate ability to compute reach targets and hand position in a fixation-centred coordinate frame. It proposes that this mechanism became important for reaching to targets in an organized sequence, particularly when targets are not visible. For visible targets, parietal and premotor mechanisms may be sufficient, which could explain why PF lesions do not necessarily disrupt all visually guided reaching. The 25-door task is relevant because the monkey reaches to opaque doors and must organize successive actions without seeing the food targets. This account links the dorsal PF cortex to the planning and sequencing demands of anthropoid reaching, rather than to basic limb movement or locomotion.
Study instruction:
- "Compare reaching to visible and unseen targets and identify when sequence organization and fixation-centred spatial coding become especially important."
Key point:
- The proposed PF contribution is strongest for organizing reaches to unseen targets in sequences.
- Basic visually guided movement can rely on parietal and premotor systems without the same PF demand.
48. Q: How might dorsal PF function support efficient foraging in changing environments?
A: Show answer
Efficient foraging requires more than locating food. An animal may need to remember which locations or targets were recently sampled, estimate when resources will become available, and plan an efficient sequence of reaches. The chapter suggests that anthropoids face resource volatility linked to timing, weather, and synchrony among trees of the same species. Predicting food availability therefore requires combining object identity with the order and duration of events. The dorsal PF cortex is proposed to represent such conjunctions and use them to select current and future goals. Its ability to update goals as new events occur would allow a foraging plan to change when the environment changes. The evolutionary interpretation is plausible within the chapter's framework but remains a proposal about adaptive advantage.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a foraging scenario in which object identity, elapsed time, event order, and target location jointly determine the next reach."
Key point:
- Foraging decisions depend on dynamic combinations of identity, timing, order, and location.
- Dorsal PF function is proposed to improve the efficiency and flexibility of sequential reaching.
49. Q: How should a researcher interpret a deficit that appears only when the same cues recur across trials?
A: Show answer
A deficit that increases when identical or similar cues recur suggests a role in resolving interference, tracking order, or updating the current goal. It is less consistent with a simple inability to perceive the cue or execute the response, especially if performance is preserved with novel stimuli or without a delay. The researcher should test whether the subject can discriminate the stimuli, learn the basic mapping, remember the most recent event, apply the rule after a delay, and prepare a future goal. Trial spacing and distractor similarity can be manipulated to separate these possibilities. The chapter's evidence shows that area 46 impairment is especially evident when subjects must distinguish the current relevant event from a history of repeated events.
Study instruction:
- "Propose at least two control conditions that vary cue repetition and delay independently, and state what each would test."
Key point:
- Repetition-sensitive deficits point toward interference and sequence-context demands.
- Separate perception, rule learning, memory, and goal preparation with targeted controls.
50. Q: What is the relationship between order coding, rule coding, and prospective coding?
A: Show answer
These mechanisms can be understood as successive or interacting stages in goal generation. Order coding identifies which event in a sequence is relevant now. Rule coding specifies how that event should be translated into a choice, such as matching the latest cue or shifting away from the previous goal. Prospective coding establishes and maintains the resulting goal until action is permitted. A failure at any stage can produce similar overt errors, such as choosing the wrong location after a delay. The chapter does not claim that these are independent modules or that one must always precede the others in a fixed sequence. Rather, they are complementary explanations for how recent events become a current action goal and how that goal is protected from interference.
Study instruction:
- "Use one delayed-response example to trace the event-order, rule-application, and prospective-maintenance stages in sequence."
Key point:
- Order identifies the relevant event; rules map it to a goal; prospection maintains the goal.
- Similar behavioural errors can arise from different points in this process.
51. Q: What does the chapter imply about abstract versus concrete goals?
A: Show answer
The dorsal PF cortex is proposed to represent concrete goals, such as a particular spatial location, and abstract goals, such as a rule or sequence pattern that can be instantiated by different movements. Evidence for abstract coding includes neurons that respond to transition patterns such as alternation or double repetition even when the specific movements differ. In maze tasks, cells can represent subordinate goals and final goals, and many encode the intended cursor target rather than a specific limb movement. These findings suggest that goal representations can operate at multiple levels of abstraction. A higher-level goal can organize lower-level actions, while the exact movement used to achieve it may depend on current sensorimotor conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Give one concrete spatial goal and one abstract sequence goal, then show how different movements could implement the same abstract goal."
Key point:
- Dorsal PF representations can specify both particular targets and abstract action structures.
- Goal identity can be distinct from the motor movement used to achieve it.
52. Q: How does the chapter link sequential goal planning to premotor organization?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that dorsal PF cortex generates an ordered series of goals and that its connections with premotor regions enable those goals to be achieved. Evidence from preSMA includes cells encoding the rank order of movements and cells representing a later movement while the monkey waits to initiate the first. Sequence studies in PF cortex show delay activity for specific sequences or sequence categories, while maze studies reveal subordinate-goal representations. Together, these findings suggest a division in which PF regions help specify what sequence of goals is appropriate and premotor systems contribute to organizing and executing the movements. The chapter does not reduce sequence planning to a single anatomical pathway; rather, it emphasizes coordinated activity across connected frontal motor and prefrontal regions.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish sequence representation from movement execution and identify evidence for both in the PF and premotor systems."
Key point:
- Dorsal PF cortex contributes to the structure and order of goals.
- Premotor connections help translate sequential goals into coordinated action.
53. Q: What is the significance of encoding conjunctions of previous choices and their outcomes?
A: Show answer
A current goal may depend on what was chosen previously and whether that choice produced a reward. The chapter describes cells in mid-lateral PF cortex that encode a previous choice together with its outcome, as well as cells representing combinations of strategy and goal. Such signals can contribute to updating the context from which the next goal is generated. They resemble, in some respects, outcome-related coding described in OFC, but the mid-lateral PF cortex uses these signals in relation to sequential goal selection. This is important for tasks where the correct next action is not simply to repeat a rewarded response. The representation of choice and outcome together can inform whether to stay, shift, or select a different goal under a rule.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a two-trial example and show how the previous choice and its outcome could jointly determine the next goal."
Key point:
- Choice-outcome conjunctions provide information for updating subsequent goals.
- Sequential choice depends on both what happened and the result it produced.
54. Q: How can the chapter's model be used to design a rigorous experiment on goal generation?
A: Show answer
A rigorous design should separate the information needed to identify the relevant event, the rule that maps it to a response, and the prospective preparation of the goal. Researchers could vary event order while holding stimulus identity constant, vary rule cues while keeping the event sequence fixed, and manipulate whether a future action can be prepared during the delay. Interference can be varied through repeated versus novel distractors, and response requirements can distinguish matching from recall or reaching. Neural recordings, imaging, lesions, or temporary inactivation can then be interpreted against these controls. The chapter's main methodological lesson is that a single delayed-response score cannot uniquely identify the cognitive process that failed. Strong inference requires task contrasts that isolate the proposed computations.
Study instruction:
- "Design a factorial experiment with independent manipulations of event order, rule cue, delay, and distractor similarity; state the predicted result for each account."
Key point:
- Task contrasts should isolate order, rule, and prospective-goal demands.
- Behavioural performance alone is insufficient to identify the underlying mechanism.
55. Q: What are the chapter's main conclusions about why area 46 is especially well placed to generate goals?
A: Show answer
Area 46 receives parietal information about the location, duration, distance, number, and order of recent events; it receives outcome information through connections with OFC; it is interconnected with other PF regions that contribute object, rule, and strategy signals; and it connects with premotor systems that can implement hand- and arm-related goals. Its neurons can represent conjunctions of event features and distinguish previous from current goals. This combination allows it to generate context-sensitive goals and maintain them prospectively until action is possible. Posterior parietal cortex alone lacks some of the outcome information, while premotor cortex is positioned more directly for movement control. The proposed function therefore follows from the particular integration performed by area 46 within a larger network.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the four major connectional contributions—parietal, orbital, intrinsic PF, and premotor—and link each to one step in goal-directed behaviour."
Key point:
- Area 46 combines context, outcome, and action-related information.
- Its distinctive connectivity explains its proposed role in goal generation and prospective coding.
Rows: 55
Table 7
Ventral Prefrontal Cortex: Context-Dependent Goal Generation and Abstract Control
1. Q: What is the central function proposed for the ventral prefrontal cortex in Chapter 7?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that the ventral prefrontal (PF) cortex generates goals appropriate to a current behavioural context and a desired outcome, with the outcome evaluated in relation to current biological needs. The context can be specified by visual or acoustic signs, and the goal may be a concrete object, place, or action, or an abstract class or set of possible goals. This is the authors' integrative account of the region's role, grounded in its connections and in lesion, recording, and imaging studies.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter's brief and expanded proposals, then identify the evidence categories used to support each part.
Key point:
- The ventral PF cortex links sensory context with outcome-sensitive goal selection.
- Its proposed outputs include concrete goals, abstract categories, rules, and strategies.
2. Q: What does the chapter mean by a visual or auditory sign?
A: Show answer
A sign is a non-spatial sight or sound that acts as a cue to a behavioural context; it need not correspond to an entire object. Examples include colour, shape, texture, glossiness, translucence, a tone, a sequence of sounds, or an animal call. Such features can signal what resource or action may be appropriate. The chapter distinguishes signs from the order, timing, and location of sensory events, which were emphasized in its discussion of dorsal PF functions.
Study instruction:
- List five visual signs and three auditory signs, and explain what behavioural context each could signal.
Key point:
- A sign is a contextual cue, not necessarily a whole object.
- Visual and auditory signs can guide goal selection when interpreted in context.
3. Q: Where is the ventral PF cortex located in macaque monkeys and humans?
A: Show answer
In macaque monkeys, the ventral PF cortex includes the lateral convexity below the principal sulcus and extends around the lateral extreme of the hemisphere to the lateral orbital sulcus. The homologous human region lies in the inferior frontal gyrus. The chapter identifies macaque areas 45 and 12/47 and human areas 45 and 47 as part of this territory. The designation 12/47 reflects the proposed homology between human area 47 and monkey area 12.
Study instruction:
- Draw a lateral view of macaque and human frontal cortex and label the principal sulcus, inferior frontal gyrus, areas 45 and 12/47 or 47.
Key point:
- The chapter maps ventral PF cortex to the macaque lateral convexity and human inferior frontal gyrus.
- Area labels are species-specific and should not be treated as numerically identical.
4. Q: Why are the connections of the ventral PF cortex central to the chapter's explanation of its function?
A: Show answer
The authors argue that the region can combine information that is available separately in other cortical areas. Temporal areas provide visual and auditory cue information; premotor connections provide access to action-related representations; and the orbital PF cortex and amygdala provide information related to outcomes and current needs. Converging these inputs allows the ventral PF cortex to associate a contextual sign with a possible action or object and the outcome that makes it appropriate. The explanation is therefore connection-based rather than based solely on anatomical location.
Study instruction:
- Construct a three-part diagram showing context, action or goal, and outcome inputs, and label the pathways described in the chapter.
Key point:
- The proposed function depends on convergent access to context, action, and outcome information.
- Connectivity provides the chapter's main explanation for regional specialization.
5. Q: How does the ventral PF cortex receive information about visual objects and their features?
A: Show answer
It has reciprocal connections with the inferior temporal cortex and connections with the perirhinal cortex. Inferior temporal regions contribute to discrimination of colour, form, and visual texture, while the perirhinal cortex represents more extensive conjunctions of object features. The chapter describes ventral PF input as arising from middle- and high-order visual processing rather than the most caudal low-order visual areas. This arrangement gives the region access to meaningful combinations of features that can be used to specify context and guide goal selection.
Study instruction:
- Compare the roles assigned to inferior temporal and perirhinal cortex, then trace how their information can reach ventral PF cortex.
Key point:
- Ventral PF cortex receives processed visual information rather than only elementary visual features.
- Inferior temporal and perirhinal inputs contribute different levels of feature conjunction.
6. Q: What role do the superior temporal cortex and auditory pathways play in ventral PF function?
A: Show answer
The superior temporal cortex is involved in recognizing sounds and sequences of sounds, including calls from other animals, and it has reciprocal connections with ventral PF cortex. These connections can provide auditory signs that specify the current context. The chapter's account of conditional auditory-visual learning supports the functional importance of this route: disconnecting PF cortex from superior temporal cortex prevented monkeys from performing above chance on a task in which tones instructed choices among visual stimuli. The authors therefore regard auditory context as an integral part of ventral PF goal generation.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a tone could serve as an instruction in a conditional task and identify the pathway required to use it.
Key point:
- Superior temporal input supplies auditory context to ventral PF cortex.
- Auditory signs can guide visual choices when the relevant connections are intact.
7. Q: How does somatosensory information reach the ventral PF cortex, and what might it contribute?
A: Show answer
