- "For two foods with different tastes and current desirability, distinguish the sensory identity representation from the abstract value comparison."
Key point:
- Outcome identity and outcome value are related but distinct representational problems.
- Specific sensory outcomes and abstract common-currency value both contribute to choice.
17. Q: Why does the chapter distinguish specific outcome representations from common-currency valuation?
A: Show answer
Specific outcome representations preserve information about which food or fluid was obtained and its sensory properties. This detail is important when learning which particular choice produced which consequence. Common-currency valuation expresses the relative desirability of different outcomes along a more general dimension, allowing comparisons across otherwise dissimilar options. The chapter argues that these functions complement each other rather than compete. An animal may need to identify a specific food to update an association, but it may need an abstract value estimate to choose between a food, a location, or another opportunity. Current needs can change the value of a specific outcome without changing its physical identity.
Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison of specific outcome identity and common-currency value, then give one decision that requires each type of information."
Key point:
- Specific identity supports outcome learning; common currency supports comparison.
- Motivational value can change even when the sensory identity of an outcome stays constant.
18. Q: Why must context, goal, and outcome information be integrated when learning conditional visuomotor associations?
A: Show answer
In a conditional visuomotor task, a cue specifies which action or spatial target should be selected, and the selected action produces an outcome. With experience, the contexts, goals, and outcomes can each become familiar and may acquire similar long-term values. The animal therefore cannot rely only on a context–outcome association or a goal–outcome association. It must learn the conjunction linking the particular context, the goal selected in that context, and the outcome produced. The chapter argues that this three-way integration is a central reason the granular PF cortex is necessary for rapid conditional learning. Its temporal, premotor, and orbitofrontal connections provide access to the different components of the conjunction.
Study instruction:
- "For a cue-to-joystick task, write out the context, chosen movement goal, and resulting outcome for at least three trials and explain why pairwise links may be insufficient."
Key point:
- Conditional learning requires context–goal–outcome conjunctions.
- The PF cortex is positioned to integrate cue, action-target, and outcome information.
19. Q: What is an event in the chapter’s theory of prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
An event is defined as the conjunction of a context, a goal, an action, and an outcome occurring at a particular time and place. Context describes the situation or cues available; goal identifies the object or location targeted; action is what the animal does; and outcome is the cost or benefit that follows. The event is therefore richer than a stimulus–response pair or a generic reward signal. It preserves the relation among the circumstances, selected target, behaviour, and consequence. The chapter proposes that PF systems can use such single events to guide later goal selection, either through short-term prospective memory or through longer-term event representations.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a recent decision as an event by recording its context, target, action, consequence, time, and place."
Key point:
- An event is a structured context–goal–action–outcome conjunction.
- The event concept is broader than a reward alone or a stimulus–response association.
20. Q: How does event-based learning differ from ancestral reinforcement learning?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts a newer PF-dependent mechanism with an older reinforcement-learning system. The ancestral system adjusts associations among stimuli, responses, and outcomes gradually by accumulating feedback across multiple experiences, often in a weighted average. The event-based mechanism can use a single context–goal–action–outcome event to select a later goal, allowing faster learning when circumstances change. The two mechanisms are not mutually exclusive: the authors argue that the granular PF cortex augments the older system, while many routine behaviours can still be guided effectively by accumulated reinforcement. This distinction is about the basis and speed of updating, not about whether rewards are involved at all.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a single-event update with a gradual average over repeated feedback, and identify a situation in which each learning mode would be useful."
Key point:
- Reinforcement learning accumulates experience; event-based learning can use one specific episode.
- The proposed PF mechanism complements rather than abolishes older learning systems.
21. Q: Why can using a single event reduce errors in changing environments?
A: Show answer
A single event can provide a direct basis for selecting a goal without waiting for many repeated feedback trials. In volatile environments, repeated trial-and-error learning can be costly because an unproductive choice may waste time, resources, or expose an animal to danger. If the animal can remember the context, target, action, and outcome from one relevant event, it can avoid repeating an ineffective choice or rapidly select a more promising one. The chapter proposes that this capacity is especially useful when resources change over time or when opportunities are limited. It also emphasizes that error reduction can arise from both rapid learning and abstract strategies that transfer knowledge across similar problems.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a changing-resource scenario and compare the number and consequences of errors expected under cumulative learning and single-event goal selection."
Key point:
- Single-event use can accelerate adjustment when feedback opportunities are limited.
- The adaptive benefit is fewer costly or risky choices, not simply faster performance for its own sake.
22. Q: What is a discrimination learning set, and how is trial-two performance used to measure it?
A: Show answer
In a visual discrimination task, an animal chooses between two stimuli, one of which is rewarded. A learning set develops as the animal gains experience with many such problems and learns how to use the structure of the task. In the serial version, the same stimulus pair is presented on consecutive trials, allowing the animal to use the outcome of the first choice to guide the second. The chapter reports that rhesus monkeys can eventually perform near 90 percent correct on trial two after extensive experience with many problems. Trial two is informative because the first encounter with a new pair is initially at chance, while the next encounter tests whether the preceding event has been used.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why trial one and trial two answer different questions in a discrimination learning-set experiment."
Key point:
- A learning set is an acquired ability to solve new problems more efficiently.
- Trial-two performance tests use of information from the immediately preceding event.
23. Q: Why does serial presentation support learning sets better than concurrent presentation?
A: Show answer
In serial learning, a particular pair of stimuli reappears on the next trial, so the animal can use the immediately preceding choice and outcome to generate and maintain the next goal. In concurrent learning, many different pairs intervene before a given pair returns. The animal cannot simply hold every pair-specific goal in short-term memory until its next appearance. The chapter reports that monkeys lose much of their high trial-two advantage under concurrent presentation, despite the task structure and general strategy remaining similar. This result supports the authors’ view that learning-set performance depends on using a recent event and maintaining its selected goal across the relevant intertrial interval.
Study instruction:
- "Design a simple serial and concurrent schedule for four stimulus pairs and identify which schedule permits immediate prospective maintenance of the next goal."
Key point:
- Serial presentation preserves the immediate event-to-next-goal relation.
- Concurrent presentation disrupts the short-term prospective use that supports a strong learning set.
24. Q: How does the win-stay, lose-shift strategy support discrimination learning?
A: Show answer
Win-stay, lose-shift uses the outcome of the previous choice to guide the next choice: after a rewarded selection, the animal stays with that option; after an unrewarded selection, it shifts to the alternative. The chapter treats this as one component of a discrimination learning set. Another component is learning the general structure of the task, namely that one member of a pair has a higher value than the other. Together, task-structure learning and strategy use help the animal interpret the preceding event and select a likely goal on the next trial. The strategy does not guarantee success in every new problem, but it can reduce errors before the specific stimulus values are fully learned.
Study instruction:
- "Work through four illustrative win and loss sequences and calculate the choice predicted by the strategy after each outcome."
Key point:
- Win-stay, lose-shift links the previous choice and its outcome to the next goal.
- Strategy learning and learning the structure of the task are related but distinct contributions.
25. Q: What lesion evidence supports a role for granular prefrontal cortex in discrimination learning sets?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes studies in which disconnecting inferior temporal visual input from frontal cortex caused monkeys to lose the rapid trial-two advantage they had acquired through serial discrimination training. Their performance returned toward the slower level seen in concurrent learning or in animals without a strong learning set. Earlier work with large frontal lesions also found weaker trial-two performance than in intact monkeys. The authors interpret these findings as evidence that the interaction between inferior temporal cortex and granular PF cortex is needed for the robust use of single events in learning sets. The evidence does not imply that all discrimination learning disappears after frontal damage; slower cumulative learning can remain.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the crossed-disconnection design and distinguish the loss of a learning-set advantage from the complete loss of discrimination learning."
Key point:
- The PF–inferior temporal interaction supports the rapid learning-set advantage.
- Lesioned animals may retain slower forms of learning even when event-based performance is impaired.
26. Q: What is a reversal learning set, and how does it resemble a discrimination learning set?
A: Show answer
In reversal learning, the rewarded member of a familiar stimulus pair changes, and the animal must switch its choice. With experience across many reversals, monkeys can learn to adjust after relatively few errors, developing a reversal set. The chapter argues that this advantage, like a discrimination learning set, depends on using recent events to generate a new goal. Serial presentation allows the relevant pair and its changed contingency to guide the next choice, whereas intervening concurrent problems disrupt the ability to maintain the appropriate goal. Disconnecting PF cortex from inferior temporal cortex or damaging orbital PF regions reduces the rapid reversal-set advantage, although slower improvement with experience may remain.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast initial reversal learning with reversal-set performance after extensive experience, and explain the role of the preceding error or outcome."
Key point:
- A reversal set is rapid adaptation to changed contingencies across repeated reversal problems.
- Its advantage depends on event use and prefrontal interactions, not simply on cumulative reinforcement.
27. Q: How does prospective coding help performance on delayed response and delayed alternation tasks?
A: Show answer
Prospective coding means representing the goal that should be achieved in the future and maintaining it in short-term memory. In delayed response, once the animal sees which food well has been baited, it can maintain that location as the current goal during the delay. In delayed alternation, the animal can use the preceding trial to establish the next location goal and maintain it across the between-trial interval. This approach can reduce interference from earlier locations or choices. The chapter notes that mid-lateral PF lesions can produce severe and persistent impairments, but it leaves open whether the deficit reflects failure to relearn the rule, failure to maintain prospective goals, or both.
Study instruction:
- "For each task, map the event that establishes the goal, the delay interval, and the source of potential interference."
Key point:
- Prospective coding maintains a future target rather than merely retaining a past cue.
- The chapter acknowledges uncertainty about the precise source of delayed-task lesion deficits.
28. Q: What does the object-in-place scenes task reveal about event memory and one-trial learning?
A: Show answer
In the object-in-place scenes task, each background scene contains choice objects in fixed locations, and the correct choice depends on the scene. Monkeys can learn the scene–choice relationship rapidly, while lesions involving the PF cortex produce substantial impairments. The chapter highlights particularly strong effects after broad PF lesions and after disconnection of frontal cortex from inferior temporal cortex; polar PF lesions also impair trial-two performance. Because many other trials intervene before a scene reappears, the animal cannot maintain all scene-specific goals in short-term memory until the next presentation. The authors therefore interpret the task as relying on long-term memory for past events, with the scene acting as context for retrieving the relevant event and generating a goal.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the interleaved structure of the task argues against holding every scene-specific choice in short-term memory."
Key point:
- The background scene supplies context for retrieving a past event and selecting a goal.
- The chapter distinguishes long-term event use here from short-term prospective maintenance in serial learning sets.
29. Q: How is one-trial learning tested in conditional visuomotor tasks?
A: Show answer
A conditional visuomotor task maps each arbitrary visual cue to a particular movement or spatial goal. The chapter describes three-choice versions in which monkeys first encounter a cue, then encounter another trial, and later see the original cue again. Performance on the later presentation can be compared with performance on a novel cue, allowing the experimenters to test whether one prior event improved the choice. A correction procedure ensures that the monkey has experienced the correct mapping at least once. Better performance on the repeated cue indicates learning from the prior conjunction of context, selected goal, and outcome, rather than merely relying on a general strategy or repeated exposure across many trials.
Study instruction:
- "Create a three-choice trial sequence with an initial cue, an intervening different cue, and a repeated or novel test cue; state what comparison measures one-trial learning."
Key point:
- The repeated-cue test isolates benefit from one prior context–goal–outcome event.
- A correction procedure controls the animal’s actual experience with the correct mapping.
30. Q: How do repeat-stay, change-shift, and lose-shift strategies reduce errors?
A: Show answer
These strategies use information from the previous trial to constrain the next goal. Repeat-stay means that when the cue repeats, the animal stays with the same goal. Change-shift means that when the cue changes, the animal shifts to another goal. Lose-shift means that after an error, the animal changes its choice. In a three-choice task, change-shift can eliminate one option and reduce the decision to a two-choice problem, improving performance without solving the specific cue–goal mapping completely. The chapter emphasizes that these strategies transfer across problems and therefore reduce errors before all individual mappings have been learned. Their application depends on remembering the previous cue and previous goal.
Study instruction:
- "For a three-choice sequence, label each trial as repeat or change and calculate how the strategy narrows the available choices."
Key point:
- Abstract strategies reduce uncertainty by constraining the next goal.
- Strategy-based transfer improves performance but does not replace learning each specific mapping.
31. Q: What do comparisons of prefrontal and hippocampal lesions show about their cooperation in conditional learning?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that hippocampal or fornix lesions impair one-trial conditional visuomotor learning and increase the number of errors, but the impairment is relatively mild compared with PF lesions. These lesions do not abolish the repeat-stay, change-shift, and lose-shift strategies. Animals can still learn the mappings over roughly 50 to 100 trials. By contrast, lesions involving ventral and orbital PF cortex eliminate the rapid learning advantage and disrupt the strategies. The authors infer that hippocampal systems contribute event memory while the granular PF cortex is especially important for integrating that information into rapid goal generation and applying abstract strategies. Neither system is presented as wholly independent of the other.
Study instruction:
- "Make a comparison matrix for PF lesions and hippocampal or fornix lesions, separating one-trial learning, strategy use, and slower acquisition."
Key point:
- The hippocampus contributes to event memory, while PF regions are critical for rapid goal use and strategy implementation.
- The systems cooperate, but their lesion effects are not identical.
32. Q: What is credit assignment, and how does it relate to single-event learning?
A: Show answer
Credit assignment is the problem of determining which choice or action caused a particular outcome when several actions or events have occurred. The chapter describes experiments in which normal monkeys could use a memory of a specific choice to associate it with the outcome that followed. Monkeys with lesions of granular orbitofrontal cortex learned these associations more slowly and cumulatively across trials, relying more on a time-weighted average of past events. The authors interpret this as evidence that prefrontal outcome systems can assign an outcome to a particular choice using a single event. This is important because inaccurate credit assignment can strengthen the wrong association and lead to repeated poor choices.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a sequence with two object choices and different outcomes, then identify which event should receive credit for each consequence."
Key point:
- Credit assignment links a particular outcome to the choice or action that caused it.
- The chapter associates rapid single-event credit assignment with granular orbitofrontal function.
33. Q: What are temporally extended events, and how can an intervening cue support their learning?
A: Show answer
A temporally extended event occurs when the choice and its outcome are separated in time. The chapter describes experiments in which a cue appeared during the delay and signalled that the outcome was imminent. Normal monkeys learned more quickly when this cue bridged the interval than when the delay was empty. Monkeys with a disconnection between PF cortex and inferior temporal cortex did not gain the same benefit. The authors interpret the cue as helping maintain the relation between the earlier choice and the later outcome, allowing the components to be treated as one event despite temporal separation. This requires integration across time and, because the outcome may occur elsewhere, across space as well.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the timeline for choice, delay cue, and outcome, then explain how the cue can preserve the choice–outcome relationship."
Key point:
- An intervening sign can bind separated parts of an event into a useful association.
- PF–inferior temporal interaction supports learning when events extend across time.
34. Q: Why does the chapter use event memory rather than episodic memory?
A: Show answer
The authors deliberately use event memory because their theory does not require explicit recollection or conscious re-experiencing of a past episode. Episodic memory is often defined as recollection of events in their temporal and spatial context, including a sense of reliving the event. The chapter notes that experiments have not established that monkeys recollect events in the human sense. Its event concept is narrower and functional: a conjunction of context, goal, action, and outcome at a particular time and place. Sometimes an event can guide a future goal immediately, without a separate act of remembering. The terminology therefore avoids making unsupported claims about subjective awareness.
Study instruction:
- "Define event memory using the four components and contrast it with explicit episodic recollection without assuming consciousness in animal tasks."
Key point:
- Event memory is a functional conjunction used to guide choice.
- The chapter does not claim that the demonstrated monkey behaviours prove human-like episodic recollection.
35. Q: How does the chapter distinguish attentive control from automatic control of behaviour?
A: Show answer
The authors use attentive control for goal generation based on current contexts and single events, a process that depends on granular PF cortex. Automatic control relies more heavily on established associations accumulated through reinforcement and can produce fast, reliable routine behaviour. Automatic behaviour is useful when resources and contingencies are stable, because it reduces the need to devote attention to every action. Attentive control becomes important when familiar behaviour fails, when circumstances change, or when a new goal must be generated from a particular event. The distinction is functional: the chapter does not use attentive control to imply conscious awareness in monkeys. It contrasts mechanisms for selecting goals rather than making a claim about subjective experience.
Study instruction:
- "Classify examples of routine, well-practised behaviour and novel event-based choices, explaining which control mode is most likely to dominate."
Key point:
- Automatic control supports efficient routine performance; attentive control supports flexible goal generation.
- In this chapter, attentive control does not imply conscious awareness.
36. Q: How does a dual-task paradigm provide an operational test of attentive control?
A: Show answer
A dual-task paradigm asks whether performing a secondary task interferes with a primary task. If the primary behaviour becomes less accurate or more stereotyped when attention is occupied elsewhere, it is considered vulnerable to attentive demands. The chapter gives the example of producing a random sequence of digits while also performing a memory task: the digit sequence becomes less random. The logic is that an attentively controlled task depends on limited processing resources and can lose the competition when another task demands those resources. This provides an operational measure of attentiveness without relying on verbal reports of awareness. The degree of interference can vary with task difficulty and the extent to which each task requires controlled processing.
Study instruction:
- "Design a dual-task experiment with a primary motor or cognitive task and a secondary memory task; specify the performance measure and control condition."
Key point:
- Interference from a secondary task can reveal dependence on attentive processing.
- The measure is behavioural and operational, not a direct test of subjective consciousness.
37. Q: What does the accumulator-network account contribute to the explanation of attentive and automatic behaviour?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses accumulator networks as a theoretical model of competition among goal-generation systems. Stronger or more established associations may accumulate evidence and reach a decision threshold more quickly, allowing automatic behaviour to control action. Attentive behaviour may depend on a larger set of weaker associations and therefore take longer to reach threshold. A competing automatic response can win unless it is suppressed or the attentive representation is strengthened. PF systems are proposed to bias this competition toward the goal that fits the current context. This model offers a way to explain how a slower but more flexible choice can prevail over a fast habitual response. It is an explanatory framework, not a direct observation of a single literal race in every task.
Study instruction:
- "Draw two competing accumulators for a habitual response and a context-sensitive goal, and show how biasing either pathway changes the selected action."
Key point:
- Accumulator models explain how competing goal systems may control behaviour.
- The model is a theoretical account of competition, not proof that all choices use identical neural dynamics.
38. Q: What happens to prefrontal activation as a motor sequence becomes automatic?
A: Show answer
Imaging studies reviewed in the chapter show that PF activation can decline as people practise a motor sequence and performance becomes more automatic. In one study, activation fell toward baseline after extended practice; in another, dual-task evidence indicated that the sequence had become automatic while PF activation decreased. At the same time, activity can increase in structures such as the putamen, SMA, posterior parietal cortex, and cerebellum. The authors interpret this pattern as a shift in the systems contributing to control. However, reduced blood-oxygen-level-dependent activation does not establish that PF neurons become completely inactive. Single-cell recordings have found PF activity during familiar tasks, so imaging sensitivity and the kind of signal measured must be considered.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the claims supported by declining fMRI activation with those that would require direct neuronal recordings."
Key point:
- Automaticity is associated with reduced engagement of some PF signals and increased engagement of other circuits.
- Reduced BOLD activation must not be interpreted as total absence of PF neural activity.
39. Q: How do rostral and caudal striatal territories relate to attentive and automatic control?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes a functional distinction between rostral striatal territories receiving input from granular PF cortex and more caudal territories receiving input from premotor and primary motor regions. Studies of learning and overtraining suggest that rostral striatum is important during new, attentive learning, whereas more caudal striatal regions contribute increasingly to automatic performance. As a task becomes practised, activity may shift from rostral toward more middle or caudal striatal territories. Inactivation studies also found that rostral striatal disruption impaired new learning, while more caudal disruption impaired established automatic sequences. The authors caution against treating the whole basal ganglia as a single habit system; different territories participate in different aspects of behaviour.
Study instruction:
- "Map the cortical inputs to rostral and caudal striatum and summarize the contrasting effects of inactivation during new learning and overtrained performance."
Key point:
- Different striatal territories contribute to different stages or modes of behavioural control.
- The chapter rejects the simplification that all basal ganglia functions are habitual.
40. Q: Why does the chapter assign the cerebellum a particular role in timing automatic actions?
A: Show answer
Cerebellar loops involving the PF cortex and motor regions become more engaged as movement sequences become automatic. Lesion and inactivation findings reviewed in the chapter indicate that animals can still learn new sequences and perform some PF-sensitive tasks after dentate or interpositus damage, yet show deficits in reaction-time improvement, predictable timing, or eye–hand coordination during overtrained sequences. These results suggest that the cerebellum contributes to the temporal organization and coordination of automatic actions more than to choosing which goal should be pursued. The distinction is important because timing and goal selection are related but separable components of successful behaviour.
Study instruction:
- "For a predictable target-arrival task, identify which outcome measures would test goal selection and which would test timing or coordination."
Key point:
- Cerebellar contributions highlighted here concern timing and coordination.
- Normal goal selection does not guarantee normal temporal precision of an automatic action.
41. Q: What may trigger a shift from automatic to attentive control?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that a shift may occur when familiar behaviour no longer produces consistent outcomes. Reward-prediction errors or other signed and unsigned error signals could indicate that an established association is no longer reliable. Such signals may arise from midbrain dopaminergic neurons or the amygdala and could help recruit PF-dependent attentive control. Human imaging studies reviewed in the chapter found greater granular PF and preSMA activation when participants attended to their actions or intentions than when they performed familiar sequences automatically. The trigger account is a hypothesis about how control modes may be engaged; it should not be treated as a fully established single pathway or as evidence that every unexpected outcome causes a conscious shift.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a hypothetical unexpected outcome through prediction-error detection, reassessment of the current goal, and possible recruitment of attentive control."
Key point:
- Unexpected or unreliable outcomes may signal the need to reconsider an automatic response.
- The chapter presents candidate error signals and pathways rather than one definitive trigger mechanism.
42. Q: What six functional themes emerge from the chapter’s behavioural and physiological fingerprints?
A: Show answer
The chapter groups evidence into six themes: integration, interference, flexibility, prospection, sequences, and valuation. Integration combines contexts, goals, and outcomes across modalities and events. Interference concerns selecting the relevant information among competing memories or distractors. Flexibility permits goals to change when contexts or contingencies change. Prospection involves representing future goals in short-term memory. Sequences organize subordinate goals and actions over time. Valuation represents the consequences and current desirability of outcomes. These themes are not independent boxes: for example, prospective goals can reduce interference, and valuation helps determine which rule or goal should guide behaviour. The authors use the themes to connect many lesion-sensitive tasks and neural coding findings to one broader proposal.
Study instruction:
- "For each of the six themes, select one task described in the chapter and identify the behaviour or cell activity that illustrates it."
Key point:
- The six themes organize diverse PF findings into a coherent functional account.
- Their interactions are central; the themes should not be treated as unrelated functions.
43. Q: What is prospective memory in the terminology used by the authors?
A: Show answer
Prospective memory means encoding and maintaining goals in short-term memory so that they can be achieved later. It differs from simply retaining a cue or recalling a past stimulus. A monkey may maintain a location to visit after a delay, an object to choose on the next trial, or the next element of a planned sequence. The representation is prospective because it specifies what should happen in the future. The chapter links this ability to event-based goal generation: a previous event can establish a future goal, and PF activity can maintain that goal until action is possible. Prospective memory is one mechanism for protecting the relevant goal from interference by other recent events.
Study instruction:
- "For a delayed response task and a multi-step action sequence, identify the future goal that must be maintained and the interval during which it is held."
Key point:
- Prospective memory maintains future goals, not merely past cues.
- It supports action across delays and helps protect a selected goal from interference.
44. Q: How should goal, outcome, current context, and current need be distinguished in applying the theory?
A: Show answer
A goal is an object or location that serves as the target of an action. An outcome is the consequence of the action, described in terms of costs and benefits and represented through sensory properties and value. Current context includes stimuli available to sensory receptors and relevant recent events. Current needs refer to the animal’s biological state, which can change the value of outcomes; for example, consuming a particular food can reduce the motivation to obtain more of it. Confusing these terms can obscure the theory. A food item can be a goal before it is obtained and an outcome after it is consumed, while its current value depends on need and context.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a food-choice example and label the cue context, target food, action, resulting outcome, and current motivational state separately."
Key point:
- Goal and outcome are not interchangeable: one is the action target, the other is its consequence.
- Current needs change outcome value and can therefore change which goal is appropriate.
45. Q: What is the chapter’s evolutionary explanation for the expansion of granular prefrontal areas in primates?
A: Show answer
The authors propose that granular PF areas evolved in relation to the foraging challenges faced by primates and later anthropoid primates. Early primates benefited from improved ability to locate and evaluate objects in a fine-branch niche, while later anthropoids faced more extensive foraging, volatile resources, competition, and predation risk. Foveal and trichromatic vision improved the detection of subtle visual signs at a distance and within a foraging patch. New prefrontal areas could integrate these cues with spatial, temporal, auditory, outcome, and goal information, allowing rapid learning from limited experience. The authors interpret this as an adaptive advantage because fewer unproductive or dangerous choices could be made. This is an evolutionary proposal linking anatomy, ecology, and cognition, not a directly observed historical experiment.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the proposed sequence from ecological challenge to sensory adaptation, PF specialization, and reduced foraging errors."
Key point:
- The evolutionary account links prefrontal expansion to more efficient choices in demanding foraging environments.
- The adaptive interpretation is the authors’ explanatory proposal and should be identified as such.
46. Q: Does the chapter claim that only primates can learn from a single event?
A: Show answer
No. The authors explicitly acknowledge specialized forms of one-trial learning in other animals, including taste-aversion learning and imprinting, as well as cases in which rodents use a single prior event to guide a choice. Their claim is narrower and comparative: the granular PF cortex may support a more general-purpose mechanism that applies single events across a wider range of foraging and behavioural problems. This distinction prevents an overstatement that other species lack all rapid learning. The proposed primate advantage concerns the breadth and flexibility of event-based goal generation, its integration with abstract rules and strategies, and its access to complex sensory and outcome information.
Study instruction:
- "Compare one specialized single-trial learning mechanism with the broader event-based capacities proposed for granular PF cortex."
Key point:
- Single-event learning is not unique to primates across all domains.
- The proposed primate distinction is generality and integration across a wider range of problems.
47. Q: How does the theory explain flexible behaviour when the first action fails to achieve a goal?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that PF systems can represent a goal independently of the particular action used to reach it. If an initial action fails, the animal can maintain the desired object or location and select a different action. This separation between goal and means is a key property of the goal hierarchy. It allows flexibility without losing sight of the intended outcome. In a foraging setting, an animal might continue to pursue a particular resource while changing its route or grasping movement. The theory therefore distinguishes flexible action selection from simply repeating a stimulus–response association that was reinforced in the past.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a task in which an initial movement fails but the target remains relevant; identify what should remain constant and what should change."
Key point:
- A stable goal representation can support changing means of action.
- Flexible control requires separating the desired target from the movement used to attain it.
48. Q: Why does the chapter argue that integration can reduce interference rather than merely increase the amount of information represented?
A: Show answer
The PF cortex receives a large amount of sensory and event information, but effective choice requires selecting what is relevant rather than treating every input equally. Top-down attention can bias processing toward features, places, or events that matter for the current goal and needs. The dorsal PF cortex is discussed in relation to order and temporal context, while caudal PF regions contribute to attention and search. Integration allows these signals to be combined, but attentional control determines which representations should guide behaviour in the presence of distractors. The authors therefore link integration with interference resolution: the system must represent a rich context and still select the relevant part of it.
Study instruction:
- "Create a scene with several competing objects and events; identify the relevant cue and explain how top-down attention could reduce distraction."
Key point:
- Integration provides a broad context; attention biases selection within that context.
- More information is useful only when relevant representations can guide the current goal.
49. Q: What evidence links PF activity to abstract categories and task rules within the broader hierarchy account?
A: Show answer
Earlier chapters describe PF cells that encode categories, matching or nonmatching rules, and strategies that apply across different stimulus items. Chapter 8 incorporates these findings into the goal hierarchy: a rule can specify the operation to apply, while a strategy can constrain the set of goals that should be considered. Such representations are not tied to a particular familiar object or movement. They allow transfer to novel stimuli and help reduce errors before every exemplar has been learned individually. The chapter also notes that category coding, abstraction, and integration can be viewed as overlapping descriptions of higher-level processing rather than necessarily separate, mutually exclusive functions.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a rule and a strategy from the chapter and explain how each could guide a response to a novel stimulus."
Key point:
- Abstract rules and strategies generalize beyond the specific items used during training.
- They contribute to goal generation by organizing which goals are relevant or excluded.
50. Q: How should the evidence from lesion, disconnection, recording, and imaging studies be combined?
A: Show answer
Each method answers a different question. Lesions test whether intact tissue is necessary for normal performance, while disconnection studies test whether communication between regions is required. Single-cell recordings reveal what information neural activity represents during a task, but coding alone does not prove that the cells are necessary. Imaging identifies patterns of regional engagement but has limited sensitivity to some neural activity and cannot by itself establish causal necessity. The chapter combines these approaches to support its account of integration and event-based goal generation. A careful interpretation should distinguish anatomical connectivity, correlational neural coding, causal lesion effects, and theoretical explanations rather than treating them as interchangeable evidence.
Study instruction:
- "For one claim in the chapter, identify the supporting anatomical, recording, lesion, and imaging evidence and state what each method can and cannot establish."
Key point:
- Converging methods strengthen an interpretation when their limitations are kept distinct.
- Neural representation, regional activation, and causal necessity are different evidential claims.
51. Q: What are the principal limitations and open questions in the chapter’s proposal?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents a broad synthesis, but several issues remain open. Some circuit-level integration mechanisms, including contributions from basal ganglia loops and upward spiralling, are described as controversial. Lesion effects can be difficult to interpret when damage is extensive or when animals must relearn task rules after surgery. Reduced imaging activation does not establish that PF neurons are silent during automatic behaviour. The proposed distinction between attentive and automatic control is functional and does not imply awareness in monkeys. Finally, the evolutionary account connects ecological pressures with neural specializations but is an explanatory hypothesis rather than direct evidence of historical causation. These limitations do not invalidate the proposal; they define where further tests and more selective experiments are needed.
Study instruction:
- "Select two claims from the chapter and list the strongest evidence, alternative explanation, and experiment that could discriminate between them."
Key point:
- The theory is a testable synthesis with acknowledged anatomical, methodological, and interpretive uncertainties.
- Careful qualification is essential when moving from animal experiments to broad claims about cognition or evolution.
52. Q: What practical framework can be used to analyze a new task using the chapter’s theory?
A: Show answer
A useful analysis begins by specifying the current context, including sensory cues and relevant recent events. Next identify the goal or goals available, distinguishing concrete targets from abstract rules or strategies. Then identify the predicted outcomes and how their value depends on current needs. Determine whether the task can be solved through a familiar cumulative association or whether it requires using a single event to generate a new goal. Ask whether the goal must be maintained prospectively, whether distractors create interference, and whether behaviour is attentive or automatic. Finally, map the relevant sensory, hippocampal, amygdala, PF, premotor, striatal, and cerebellar pathways while separating evidence from interpretation.
Study instruction:
- "Apply this framework to a novel cue-guided reaching task and complete a diagram showing context, goal, action, outcome, memory demand, and candidate neural pathways."
Key point:
- The theory can be applied by analyzing context, goal, outcome, event use, memory, and control mode in sequence.
- A good task analysis separates behavioural requirements from the neural mechanisms proposed to support them.
Rows: 52
Table 9
Human Prefrontal Cortex: Evolution, Goal Generation, and Higher Cognition
1. Q: What is the chapter's central account of the human prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that the granular prefrontal cortex (PF cortex) retains an ancestral primate function: generating goals appropriate to a current context and desired outcome, while taking current needs into account. It extends this account to human cognition by proposing that larger and more interconnected PF systems support more abstract representations, instruction-based learning, imitation, prospective maintenance of goals, and mental simulation. These capacities allow people to reduce errors beyond what physical trial-and-error learning can achieve. The authors do not claim that every complex human ability is fully explained by this framework. They use the context, goal, and outcome hierarchies as a starting point, then propose re-representation as a possible mechanism for explaining higher-order awareness, reasoning, self-evaluation, and social judgement.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the chapter's argument from ancestral goal generation through human elaborations, and distinguish its core proposal from its more tentative explanations.
Key point:
- Human PF functions are presented as elaborations of an ancestral goal-generation system.
- Error reduction is the unifying theme, not a claim that every human cognitive ability has been solved.
2. Q: How does the chapter connect human prefrontal function with the ancestral primate function described in the preceding chapter?
A: Show answer
The proposed ancestral function is to generate a goal from a context in light of an expected outcome and current biological needs. In monkeys, this can involve selecting an object, place, or action based on a cue, learned association, or abstract rule. The human chapter argues that the same broad architecture can support words, imagined objects, action plans, remembered events, and social goals. The representational content and time horizon become more complex, but the basic operation remains a mapping from context to an appropriate goal. Human capacities for instruction, imitation, and imagining alternative futures add routes for selecting goals without having to discover every consequence through overt action. This is an evolutionary continuity argument, not proof that all human cognition is reducible to foraging decisions.
Study instruction:
- Draw a context-to-goal diagram and add examples from animal cue-guided choice, verbal retrieval, planning, and social judgement.
Key point:
- The chapter emphasizes continuity in the core operation and elaboration in representational complexity.
- Do not equate the proposed common mechanism with identical cognitive abilities across species.
3. Q: What does the fossil evidence suggest about changes in the human frontal lobes?
A: Show answer
Fossil endocasts provide indirect evidence about the overall shape of the brain, not a direct map of PF cytoarchitecture or function. The chapter reviews evidence that hominid frontal lobes became wider and more rounded at the front. Australopithecine endocasts differ in shape, with some resembling the more pointed frontal form of modern great apes and others showing a more rounded contour. The Australopithecus sediba material is discussed as suggesting a transition toward the rounded form seen in Homo. Measurements comparing australopithecines with later Homo specimens also suggest an increase in relative frontal width. The slope of the inner frontal brain case in archaic human skulls is reported as broadly similar to that in early modern humans, leading the authors to suggest that frontal shape may have reached a modern configuration hundreds of thousands of years ago. These are inferences from skull shape, not direct evidence of particular cognitive abilities.
Study instruction:
- Compare the fossil examples discussed in the chapter and list what endocasts can establish versus what they cannot reveal about cortical organization.
Key point:
- Fossils support changes in frontal-lobe shape and relative size, not direct claims about PF function.
- Anatomical shape should not be treated as a direct measure of intelligence or a specific cognitive skill.
4. Q: Why is it difficult to reconstruct prefrontal evolution from fossil skulls alone?
A: Show answer
Fossil skulls preserve impressions of the brain's external surface only imperfectly. In the hominid material discussed, the dura mater prevents clear sulcal impressions on the inner skull, so researchers cannot reliably identify the boundaries of individual cortical areas. Endocasts can support comparisons of overall size and contour, such as frontal widening or rounding, but they do not reveal granular versus dysgranular cortex, dendritic structure, connectivity, or neuronal computations. The chapter therefore treats fossil evidence as suggestive and combines it with comparative studies of living primates, cytoarchitecture, gene expression, and connectivity. Even this combined evidence has limitations, because different methods measure different anatomical properties and cannot be assumed to produce interchangeable estimates.
Study instruction:
- Create an evidence table with separate columns for fossil shape, cortical microstructure, connectivity, and functional imaging, noting the inference each method can legitimately support.
Key point:
- Endocasts are useful for broad morphology but cannot identify detailed cortical organization.
- Evolutionary conclusions require converging evidence from different methods.
5. Q: What evidence does the chapter use to argue that the granular PF cortex expanded disproportionately in humans?
A: Show answer
The chapter compares estimates of granular PF surface area across primates. Brodmann's estimates place the granular PF cortex at roughly 11 percent of the neocortex in macaques, 17 percent in chimpanzees, and 28 percent in humans. Other analyses estimate the proportion of the frontal lobe occupied by granular PF cortex and likewise suggest a substantial human expansion. The authors note, however, that researchers disagree about the boundaries used to define the frontal cortex and granular PF cortex. Semendeferi and colleagues' estimate of cortex rostral to the precentral sulcus is not equivalent to a measure of granular PF cortex, because some of that territory may be dysgranular. The chapter's conclusion is therefore that human granular PF cortex is greatly expanded, while acknowledging uncertainty in the exact magnitude.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a percentage of the frontal lobe and a percentage of the neocortex are different denominators, and why cytoarchitectonic boundaries matter.
Key point:
- Comparative measurements support disproportionate PF expansion, but estimates depend on anatomical definitions.
- Do not quote one percentage as an uncontested measurement.
6. Q: What is the significance of the expansion of the polar prefrontal cortex, especially area 10?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights the polar PF cortex as a region that expanded substantially in humans relative to other primates. Area 10 is small in anthropoid monkeys but is described as one of the largest cytoarchitectonic areas in the human frontal lobe. Comparative measurements suggest that its proportion of the brain is roughly twice as large in humans as in chimpanzees. The chapter discusses a counterargument based on a regression of area 10 volume against total brain volume: the human value lies only slightly above the predicted line. The authors respond that the regression slope is greater than one, so larger brains devote a greater proportion to area 10; fitting the line therefore does not imply equivalent functional capacity. They propose that area 10 may contribute a higher level in goal hierarchies, including the maintenance of primary goals while subsidiary goals are processed.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between absolute size, relative size, and allometric scaling, then summarize the chapter's argument about area 10.
Key point:
- Area 10 expansion is linked to possible higher-order goal processing.
- The evolutionary and functional interpretation remains dependent on comparative anatomical evidence.
7. Q: How might dendritic spines and neuropil support greater integration in the human PF cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews findings that human area 10 contains more neuropil between layer 3 cell bodies than the corresponding areas in apes, and that human PF pyramidal cells have substantially more dendritic spines than macaque cells. Dendritic spines are important sites for synaptic input. A greater number of spines can therefore provide an individual neuron with more opportunities to receive and integrate information from multiple sources. The authors relate this anatomy to the PF cortex's role in combining context, goals, and outcomes, and in forming increasingly abstract representations. The evidence supports increased integrative potential; it does not mean that each neuron necessarily uses every input simultaneously or that spine number alone determines cognitive performance.
Study instruction:
- Trace the proposed relationship from dendritic spines to synaptic input, neuronal integration, and cross-domain representation, and identify what this anatomical evidence does not prove.
Key point:
- More spines may increase a neuron's capacity to integrate inputs.
- Structural capacity is not by itself a direct measure of a particular cognitive operation.
8. Q: What do the findings on short and long intrinsic white-matter fibres imply about human PF organization?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes long association fibres running through the core of the white matter from shorter fibres connecting neighbouring regions within PF gyri. Comparative measurements suggest that the human volume containing long association fibres is broadly what would be expected for a brain of human size, whereas short fibres are more extensive than expected. The authors interpret this as a possible increase in local interconnection among PF areas. Such connections could help one PF region share or combine information with another, supporting mappings across cognitive domains and additional levels of hierarchical processing. The evidence concerns anatomical connectivity, however; it does not directly demonstrate the content of information transmitted or establish which specific task depends on a given fibre system.
Study instruction:
- Sketch a PF network showing local links between neighbouring areas and longer association pathways, then explain the distinct integration roles each might support.
Key point:
- The chapter proposes unusually extensive short-range interconnections in the human PF cortex.
- Connectivity data suggest capacity for integration but do not specify the computation by themselves.
9. Q: What can diffusion tensor imaging reveal about PF connectivity, and what are its limitations?
A: Show answer
Diffusion tensor imaging (DTI) uses the directional properties of water diffusion along axons to estimate large-scale white-matter pathways in the living human brain. The chapter reports that DTI studies find a broad pattern of human PF connections resembling patterns established by tracer studies in monkeys: dorsal PF links with posterior parietal cortex, ventral PF with inferior temporal cortex, and orbital or medial regions with the amygdala and related structures. DTI is valuable because it permits non-invasive study of human connectivity. Its limitations are substantial: it cannot match the sensitivity of microscopic tracer methods, cannot reliably identify exact projection origins and terminations, and cannot resolve all crossing or fine-scale pathways. The chapter therefore uses DTI for broad comparative patterns, not as a substitute for cellular anatomical tracing.
Study instruction:
- Compare DTI with axonal tracer methods in terms of invasiveness, spatial resolution, and what each method can establish about a connection.
Key point:
- DTI supports broad connectivity comparisons between humans and monkeys.
- It does not provide the same precise projection detail as microscopic tracing.
10. Q: What does the comparison of Broca's area in humans and other primates suggest about language specialization?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Broca's area as including areas 44 and 45, positioned rostral to ventral premotor cortex. It reviews evidence that area 44 is more strongly connected with inferior parietal regions, whereas area 45 has stronger connections with temporal cortex. The overall arrangement has a counterpart in monkeys, but human area 45 has more widespread connections with middle temporal cortex than chimpanzee area 45, and the pathway is larger and more left-lateralized in humans. Developmental findings also suggest increasing strength of left-sided connections in children. The chapter relates this connectivity pattern to left-hemisphere specialization for language and verbal processing, while noting that comparative evidence is not conclusive. It also cautions against assuming that every function associated with language is unique to a single cortical region.
Study instruction:
- Map the connections of areas 44 and 45 and explain how the human left-sided temporal pathway differs from the pattern reported in chimpanzees.
Key point:
- Human Broca's area shows connectivity elaborations associated with language specialization.
- The chapter presents the comparative case as broadly consistent, not definitive.
11. Q: Why does the chapter treat the lateral polar PF cortex as a possible evolutionary specialization rather than an established human-only area?
A: Show answer
Some comparative anatomical and connectivity findings suggest that a lateral portion of area 10 may be present in apes and humans but absent in monkeys. The chapter reviews topological and cytoarchitectonic arguments, as well as human resting-state correlations between lateral polar PF cortex and inferior parietal regions that were not found in the corresponding monkey analysis. The absence of a matching connection in monkey tracer studies is consistent with a specialization that arose after the monkey-ape divergence. However, the proposed homology and area boundary are not settled, and the authors explicitly call for more evidence. Functionally, this region could provide an additional rostral level in a lateral hierarchy, supporting more complex contexts and the maintenance of primary goals while secondary goals are processed.
Study instruction:
- Separate the anatomical evidence, connectivity evidence, and proposed cognitive role for lateral area 10; mark which conclusions are tentative.
Key point:
- Lateral polar PF specialization is plausible but not conclusively established.
- Its proposed role concerns higher-order context and goal organization.
12. Q: What is the proposed significance of the dorsal paracingulate cortex?
A: Show answer
The dorsal paracingulate cortex is described as a region between the cingulate sulcus and paracingulate sulcus in humans who possess the latter sulcus. The chapter distinguishes it from the area 32 found in monkeys, arguing that monkey area 32 is more comparable to the human pregenual medial PF cortex. DTI findings suggest that the human dorsal paracingulate cortex lacks some of the amygdala and hypothalamic connections characteristic of pregenual medial PF regions, while showing connections with dorsal PF cortex. This pattern is presented as evidence that the dorsal paracingulate cortex may have evolved in the ape-human lineage. The authors propose that it adds a rostral level to medial hierarchical processing, potentially supporting reflection on intentions, self-evaluation, and other people's mental states. The evidence remains suggestive, not definitive.
Study instruction:
- Compare dorsal paracingulate and pregenual medial PF connectivity, then list the functional interpretations that remain hypotheses.
Key point:
- The dorsal paracingulate cortex may be a later evolutionary elaboration of medial PF hierarchy.
- Its exact homology and evolutionary distribution remain uncertain.
13. Q: How does the chapter distinguish a perceptual decision from a choice of goal or an action?
A: Show answer
A perceptual decision identifies what is happening in the sensory world, such as whether a field of moving dots is moving left or right. A goal choice selects the target that should be pursued given that perceptual context, while an action is the movement used to achieve the selected goal, such as a saccade or button press. The chapter argues that PF activations during perceptual decision tasks need not mean that the PF cortex merely performs perception. The same decision can guide different report actions, and PF activity that is common across response modes may reflect the goal rather than the motor response. The authors interpret the PF cortex as integrating perceptual information about context with the selection of an appropriate goal.
Study instruction:
- For a moving-dot task, label the sensory judgement, selected target, and report movement separately; explain why changing the response effector helps isolate the decision-related component.
Key point:
- Perception, goal selection, and action are related but analytically distinct stages.
- PF activity may encode the selected goal rather than the movement used to report it.
14. Q: Why does the chapter caution against treating an imaging voxel as if it were a single neuron?
A: Show answer
A functional imaging voxel contains many thousands of cells and their synaptic inputs. The blood-oxygen-level-dependent signal is influenced substantially by synaptic activity and local processing, not simply by the firing of one homogeneous neuronal population. Within a voxel, some cells may encode perceptual information, others a goal, and still others a response action. These populations can be intermingled. The chapter uses monkey single-cell findings to illustrate that PF neurons can reflect an animal's report of an illusory movement independently of the saccade used to report it, while other nearby neurons encode saccades to visible goals. An imaging study cannot separate these intermingled populations with the same precision. Therefore, an activation should not be assigned one simple psychological label without considering task design and other evidence.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between a voxel-level BOLD result and a single-cell recording, and identify at least two neuronal codes that could coexist in one voxel.
Key point:
- Imaging activation is a population-level signal, not a direct readout of one neuronal function.
- Task contrasts and converging methods are needed for functional interpretation.
15. Q: How does sensory imagery fit the chapter's account of PF context generation?
A: Show answer
When people imagine faces, houses, or other objects without seeing them, activation occurs in ventral and mid-lateral PF regions as well as in content-specific temporal regions. The chapter interprets imagery as the attentive generation of an internal representation that functions like a context. Connectivity analyses reviewed in the chapter suggest that PF interactions with temporal cortex vary with the content being imagined, whereas posterior parietal interactions do not show the same pattern. A dual-task study further suggests that generating an image requires attention: performing a demanding random-number task disrupts image generation, although an image that has already been generated can be maintained with less interference. The proposed role is therefore not simply storage of a picture, but attentively constructing a representation that can guide subsequent thought or action.
Study instruction:
- Contrast seeing an object, generating an image of it, and maintaining the image after generation; identify which stage is most attention-demanding in the evidence reviewed.
Key point:
- PF cortex is linked to the attentive construction of internal context representations.
- Generating an image and maintaining an already generated image are not equivalent operations.
16. Q: What does the chapter mean by reflective sensory awareness, and how is it distinguished from accurate stimulus discrimination?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews studies in which people could identify a masked stimulus correctly but differed in whether they reported that they had actually seen it or had merely guessed. Mid-lateral PF activation was greater when subjects reported seeing the stimulus, even when objective accuracy was matched. Disruptive stimulation of PF cortex altered the frequency of reports of seeing without reducing the accuracy of stimulus identification. Patients with frontal lesions also reported seeing masked digits less often, particularly when lesions included left polar PF cortex. The authors interpret these findings as evidence for reflective awareness or confidence about one's perception, rather than basic sensory discrimination itself. They suggest that a higher PF level may re-represent a perceptual judgement and support a judgement about the experience of seeing. This interpretation is related to, but does not settle, broader theories of consciousness.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish objective accuracy from subjective awareness in a backward-masking experiment and explain what a selective change in awareness reports would imply.
Key point:
- PF involvement may concern awareness of having perceived, not basic identification accuracy.
- The chapter's re-representation account is a proposed explanation, not a complete theory of consciousness.
17. Q: How does verb generation illustrate the mapping of a context to a goal?
A: Show answer
In verb-generation tasks, a noun such as "cake" provides a context and the participant generates an appropriate verb such as "eat", "cut", or "make". The response is not fixed in advance, so the participant must actively select a goal item that fits the cue. The chapter reports greater PF activation during generation than during simple repetition of the noun, with a peak in ventral PF cortex. When repeated exposure makes responses stereotyped, PF activation falls toward the control level. This pattern is interpreted as reflecting attentive goal generation rather than passive exposure to words. The same logic applies to completing an unconstrained sentence: the less the context determines a single answer, the more the participant must generate a suitable goal from alternatives.
Study instruction:
- Design a small verb-generation comparison using constrained and unconstrained nouns, and predict how response variability and practice should affect the need for attentive generation.
Key point:
- A cue word can act as context for generating a response word as goal.
- Novel, flexible generation recruits PF processing more than a practised stereotyped response.
18. Q: Why does imagining an action recruit PF cortex when executing a simple movement may not?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts the execution of simple finger movements with imagining those same movements. In the cited studies, simple execution did not reliably activate PF cortex, whereas motor imagery did. Imagining an action requires a person to construct and maintain an internal representation of what is to be done, rather than simply carrying out a prepared movement. PF activity also depends on attention to the upcoming goal: when attention is diverted to a concurrent visual search task, PF influence on premotor regions decreases. Premotor and posterior parietal areas may contribute movement preparation, but the authors argue that PF cortex helps generate the goal representation that guides the imagined action. Thus motor imagery is treated as an attentively generated goal representation, not as a direct proxy for movement execution.
Study instruction:
- Compare neural demands in movement execution, motor imagery, and motor imagery under divided attention; state why these conditions should not be treated as equivalent.
Key point:
- Imagining an action requires an internally generated representation of a goal.
- Attention to the goal helps explain PF recruitment during motor imagery.
19. Q: What do Tower of London and Tower of Hanoi tasks reveal about PF contributions to planning?
A: Show answer
These tasks require participants to arrange objects into a target configuration using a sequence of moves, often while minimizing the number of steps. The chapter reports activation in mid-lateral PF cortex during planning and greater polar PF activation as the required sequence becomes longer or more demanding. More difficult problems may require counterintuitive intermediate moves, such as moving an object temporarily away from its final position while preserving the overall goal. Patients with frontal lesions can make errors on such problems. The authors interpret polar PF involvement as potentially supporting a higher level of goal organization: the final goal must remain active while subsidiary goals are generated and executed. They also caution that these tasks mix planning with problem solving, so activation cannot be attributed to planning alone.
Study instruction:
- Solve a multi-step rearrangement problem and label the final goal, intermediate goals, and any move that temporarily appears to move away from the target.
Key point:
- Complex planning requires maintaining a final goal while generating subsidiary goals.
- Planning tasks often also tax problem solving, which complicates interpretation of imaging activations.
20. Q: What is a task set, and how does the PF cortex help maintain it?
A: Show answer
A task set is the rule or cognitive operation that currently guides behaviour, such as remembering letters rather than locations, reversing their order, or making a semantic rather than phonological judgement. The chapter reviews experiments in which a cue or self-generated decision establishes the rule before a delay period. During that delay, rostral ventral PF activity reflects which task is to be performed, even when the task was selected without a verbal instruction. This activity is interpreted as maintaining the current rule so that the appropriate task-specific processing can be engaged when the relevant stimulus arrives. Correlations between rostral PF activation and task-specific regions vary with the rule, suggesting that the maintained task set helps configure downstream processing.
Study instruction:
- Write two contrasting task rules and specify what information must be held during the delay and which downstream process each rule should recruit.
Key point:
- Task set is the currently applicable rule, not merely the sensory cue that announced it.
- PF maintenance of the rule can configure task-specific processing.
21. Q: What do task-switching lesion findings show about maintaining and changing task sets?
A: Show answer
In the task-switching study reviewed, people with large lateral frontal lesions made more errors when the instruction changed from one trial to the next but fewer errors than controls when the instruction repeated. The authors interpret this pattern as a failure to update and maintain the currently relevant task set: after a switch, the patient may default to the previously used rule. The lesion group also showed weaker covariance between delay-period PF activity and activity in task-specific parietal or temporal regions, consistent with disrupted coordination between the maintained rule and the processing system it should guide. The result is not simply a generic inability to remember a word or visual cue; it concerns applying the correct rule at the right time.
Study instruction:
- Construct a stay-versus-switch trial sequence and predict the error pattern if a participant can retrieve both rules but fails to update which one currently governs behaviour.
Key point:
- Rule knowledge and current rule implementation can dissociate.
- PF lesions can impair updating and coordinating task sets, especially on switch trials.
22. Q: How does the chapter distinguish maintaining a future task in prospective memory from remembering instructions in long-term memory?
A: Show answer
A person may be able to repeat instructions when asked but still fail to carry them out at the appropriate time. The chapter describes patients with rostral PF lesions who could state the instructions for several simple tasks yet attempted fewer tasks and switched between them less often than healthy participants. This pattern suggests that retrieval of the instruction from long-term memory can remain intact while maintaining it as an active intention during ongoing behaviour is impaired. The authors relate this to goal neglect: the goal is known but is not kept sufficiently available to guide action. Prospective memory therefore involves preserving a future intention in a form that can be acted upon when the appropriate opportunity arises, not merely retaining a verbal description of what should be done.
Study instruction:
- Create a prospective-memory example involving two errands and identify separately what would test instruction recall and what would test successful intention maintenance.
Key point:
- Knowing an instruction is different from keeping it active for later execution.
- Goal neglect can arise when an intention is not maintained or implemented at the appropriate time.
23. Q: What is the proposed role of the polar PF cortex in multitasking and branching behaviour?
A: Show answer
In branching tasks, participants perform a primary task while temporarily suspending it to carry out a secondary task, then return to the primary task. The chapter reports greater polar PF activation in a branching condition than in a condition where participants simply alternate between tasks. The difference may reflect the need to preserve the primary task while attending to the secondary one. Patients with polar PF damage attempted fewer tasks and switched between them less often in studies requiring several simple tasks to be completed. The authors therefore suggest that the polar PF cortex supports prospective maintenance of more than one task or goal, including the ability to suspend and resume an intention. The interpretation is broader than ordinary rapid task switching and concerns the maintenance of pending goals.
Study instruction:
- Compare alternating and branching schedules and mark when a task is completed, suspended, or pending; explain which condition requires preserving an uncompleted goal.
Key point:
- Branching requires retaining a pending primary goal while another task is performed.
- Polar PF involvement is associated with prospective multitasking, not simply faster switching.
24. Q: Why might the lateral polar PF cortex represent unchosen alternatives as well as the selected goal?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews studies in which activity in lateral polar PF cortex tracked the relative advantage or reward value of an alternative that a participant did not choose. In a task with several alternatives, activity reflected the value of the best unchosen option. The authors interpret this as evidence that the region may maintain potential goals while the person pursues one of them. Comparing the chosen option with alternatives can support decisions about whether to persist or switch. This operation is important for mental trial-and-error: a person can evaluate possible outcomes without physically carrying out every alternative. The evidence does not imply that every unchosen option is represented equally or that activity alone proves a complete simulation of future consequences.
Study instruction:
- Given a choice among several options, record the chosen option, the best unchosen option, and the information needed to decide whether switching would be advantageous.
Key point:
- The polar PF cortex may help maintain and evaluate potential goals that are not currently selected.
- Evaluating alternatives mentally can reduce reliance on costly physical trial-and-error.
25. Q: What does monitoring one's own intention to act reveal about medial and lateral PF organization?
A: Show answer
In intention-monitoring experiments, participants report when they first become aware of an intention to move, rather than simply reporting when the movement occurs. The chapter reports activation in mid-lateral PF cortex, pre-supplementary motor area, and posterior parietal cortex during intention reports. The degree of PF activation was related to how early the participant reported awareness of the intention, and analyses suggested an influence from PF cortex to preSMA that was not matched by an equivalent influence from posterior parietal cortex. Other introspective tasks recruit dorsomedial PF and dorsal paracingulate regions when people judge their own goals, emotions, or feelings. The authors place these findings within a medial hierarchy in which increasingly rostral regions represent increasingly reflective aspects of one's own mental states.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish reporting movement onset from reporting awareness of intention, and identify the regions associated with each in the chapter's account.
Key point:
- Monitoring an intention is different from monitoring the movement itself.
- Rostral medial PF activity is associated with reflection on one's own mental states.
26. Q: How might the ability to monitor one's own intentions support understanding other people's intentions?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses the proposal that representing one's own intentions provides a basis for representing the intentions of others. Studies of mental-state judgement commonly find activation in dorsal paracingulate and neighbouring medial PF regions. Similar regions can be engaged when participants decide whether personality traits apply to themselves or to another person. The authors interpret the pattern as a possible hierarchical extension from monitoring one's own actions and intentions toward representing more abstract mental states, including those of other people. The discussion of expert flint knapping adds a practical example: experts, who may better infer the toolmaker's purpose, show medial PF activation when observing complex tool manufacture, whereas novices show a different pattern. These findings are suggestive and do not establish that one region alone performs theory of mind.
Study instruction:
- Compare self-intention, other-intention, and imitation tasks; identify the inference each task requires beyond simply observing an action.
Key point:
- Social understanding may build on capacities for monitoring one's own intentions.
- Medial PF activation is associated with mental-state reasoning but is not a complete explanation of social cognition.
27. Q: What does monitoring internal bodily states contribute to the chapter's outcome hierarchy?
A: Show answer
Outcomes are not limited to external rewards such as food or money. The chapter reviews activations in anterior cingulate regions when participants attend to hunger, emotion-inducing stimuli, or heart rate. These signals can arise from receptors monitoring the stomach, vascular system, or tissue damage. The authors relate this to the PF outcome hierarchy because the value of a goal depends on the organism's current needs and internal state. A choice that is beneficial under one physiological condition may be less useful under another.
