Monitoring internal signals therefore helps determine which outcomes matter and whether a selected goal remains appropriate. The chapter's emphasis is on the contribution of internal-state information to goal selection, not on a claim that all bodily awareness is generated in PF cortex.
Study instruction:
- Choose an example involving hunger, pain, or arousal and explain how a change in internal state could alter the value of the same external goal.
Key point:
- Current needs influence the value of possible outcomes.
- Internal-state monitoring helps keep goal selection aligned with the organism's condition.
28. Q: Why does the chapter reject the idea that anterior cingulate activation is only an error or conflict detector?
A: Show answer
Earlier theories linked anterior cingulate activation to detecting errors or monitoring conflict after negative feedback. The chapter highlights an experiment that balanced the information provided by positive and negative feedback. When both types of outcome were equally informative, anterior cingulate activation did not differ simply according to whether feedback was positive or negative. The authors therefore favour a broader account in which the region monitors outcomes generally. Outcome monitoring includes evaluating what happened relative to the task's requirements, not merely registering failure. Errors can be important outcomes, but they are one subset of the information that guides subsequent behaviour. The chapter also notes that the meaning of an outcome depends on what participants believe the task is testing, which can recruit more rostral medial regions involved in self-evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a design that equates the information in positive and negative feedback is more diagnostic than a design where only errors provide informative feedback.
Key point:
- Anterior cingulate activity is not adequately described as error detection alone.
- Outcome monitoring includes positive and negative information that can guide future choices.
29. Q: How does the chapter distinguish monitoring an outcome from evaluating oneself?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an experiment in which participants were told either that a memory task measured intelligence or that it simply tested task parameters. Both groups showed anterior cingulate activation when they detected errors. Only the group that believed the task assessed intelligence also showed dorsal paracingulate activation when monitoring outcomes and rating their own performance. The authors interpret the additional activation as reflecting self-evaluation: the participant is not only registering what happened but judging what it says about them. This distinction fits the proposed medial hierarchy, where more caudal regions monitor actions and outcomes, while more rostral regions support increasingly abstract representations of the self. It also links to metacognition, because the person forms a judgement about their own performance or knowledge.
Study instruction:
- Compare a neutral performance-monitoring task with one framed as a test of personal ability, and identify which response measures would distinguish outcome detection from self-evaluation.
Key point:
- Monitoring an error and evaluating what it means about oneself are different operations.
- Self-evaluation is associated with more rostral medial PF processing in the chapter's model.
30. Q: How does verbal paired-associate learning illustrate learning from a single event?
A: Show answer
In a verbal paired-associate task, a participant encounters a pair such as "cabbage-pen" and later uses the first word as a cue to retrieve the second. The chapter reports ventral PF activation during initial learning and greater activation when established associations are altered than when entirely new pairs are introduced. The authors interpret the activity as supporting retrieval of a goal item from a context cue, sometimes on the basis of a single prior presentation. Because the pair may have appeared only once, successful retrieval depends on memory for a specific episode rather than repeated reinforcement. Frontal lesions can impair learning paired associates, supporting a contribution of frontal systems to the mapping. The chapter does not claim that the PF cortex is the sole storage site for all paired-associate memories.
Study instruction:
- Create a set of cue-response pairs and test immediate retrieval after one exposure; distinguish learning a new pair from updating a previously learned mapping.
Key point:
- A cue can retrieve a goal representation learned from one episode.
- PF involvement concerns the mapping and retrieval process, not necessarily permanent storage of the association.
31. Q: What role does the PF cortex play in learning word meanings and mapping words to referents?
A: Show answer
The chapter treats word learning as a form of conditional association: a spoken or written word is linked to an object, concept, or referent. Imaging studies reviewed show PF activation when people learn new sound-picture or face-name associations, particularly during initial learning or when mappings are unfamiliar. With repeated familiarity, retrieval may rely more heavily on temporal-lobe representations. The chapter also notes that humans can learn some word-referent associations after a single presentation, a capacity often called fast mapping. This is consistent with the broader proposal that granular PF systems support rapid context-to-goal mapping. In language use, a speaker also selects words to influence a listener's mental representation, linking word generation to intended communicative outcomes.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a novel spoken word can become a cue for a referent, and distinguish initial mapping, later retrieval, and using a word to communicate an intended meaning.
Key point:
- PF cortex contributes to rapid learning and use of novel word-referent mappings.
- The chapter does not equate PF activation with permanent storage of lexical knowledge.
32. Q: How does the chapter distinguish semantic memory from episodic memory?
A: Show answer
Episodic memory concerns personally experienced events situated in a particular time and place, often accompanied by a sense of recollection. Semantic memory concerns general knowledge about objects, categories, and facts about the world, such as knowing that palm trees and pyramids are associated with Egypt. The chapter uses this distinction to explain why different tasks recruit PF systems in different ways. Retrieving a particular event may involve medial PF and hippocampal-related networks, whereas applying factual knowledge or retrieving a difficult semantic association often involves left ventral PF cortex alongside temporal regions. The distinction is conceptual rather than a claim that the systems never interact: facts can be learned during episodes, and an event can later contribute to general knowledge.
Study instruction:
- Classify examples such as remembering a particular visit to Egypt and knowing that pyramids are found there, and explain what makes one episodic and the other semantic.
Key point:
- Episodic memory is tied to particular events; semantic memory is general knowledge.
- PF contributions differ according to whether a task retrieves an event, applies a fact, or maps a cue to a goal.
33. Q: What does the evidence suggest about the ventral PF cortex and semantic knowledge?
A: Show answer
The chapter reviews tasks requiring participants to use factual relationships among objects, such as selecting a tool that shares a functional association with another tool. These tasks activate left ventral PF cortex as well as middle and inferior temporal regions. A patient with a ventral PF lesion performed relatively well on an easier version, while patients with rostral temporal damage performed poorly, suggesting that temporal regions are important for storing semantic knowledge. However, temporary disruption of caudal left ventral PF cortex with repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation increased errors on difficult semantic association trials but not on a rhyming control task. The authors infer that ventral PF cortex contributes to attentive retrieval or selection of semantic knowledge, especially when associations are difficult, while the temporal lobe has a major role in semantic representation.
Study instruction:
- Contrast the lesion, stimulation, and imaging evidence, and explain why preserved performance after a chronic lesion does not necessarily show that a region is never involved.
Key point:
- Temporal regions appear important for semantic knowledge representation.
- Ventral PF cortex contributes to controlled retrieval or selection when semantic associations are demanding.
34. Q: Why is learning facts from instruction important to the chapter's account of human error reduction?
A: Show answer
Humans can acquire information by being told it, reading it, or observing another person, rather than having to discover every relationship through personal trial and error. The chapter describes studies in which participants learned facts from sentences, with greater left ventral PF activation for unfamiliar facts than for familiar ones. This suggests that instruction-based learning recruits PF mechanisms when new information must be integrated and later used. If a person learns that two objects are associated through a verbal explanation, they can apply that fact without personally testing every possible combination. Instruction can therefore prevent errors and reduce the time, cost, and risk associated with physical experimentation. The chapter presents this as a distinctive elaboration of primate learning, while recognizing that other memory systems also contribute.
Study instruction:
- Compare how a person could learn a factual relationship through direct trial and error versus a verbal explanation, and identify which errors the instruction can prevent.
Key point:
- Instruction enables acquisition without personally experiencing every outcome.
- PF cortex is proposed to help integrate and apply newly instructed information.
35. Q: What is the chapter's distinction between event retrieval and source memory?
A: Show answer
Event retrieval involves remembering a personally experienced episode. Source memory is more specific: the participant must retrieve the context in which an item was encountered, such as which list contained a word, which person presented an object, or what colour background accompanied it. In source-memory tasks, the item itself can cue the retrieval of its original context. This reverses the more familiar mapping in which a context cues a goal. The chapter reports ventral PF activation when people retrieve contextual details, especially when there is high interference between possible sources. The authors interpret this as the PF cortex helping map a remembered item back to the context of its occurrence.
Study instruction:
- Design a source-memory test with two lists or two presenters and explain how it differs from a simple item-recognition test.
Key point:
- Source memory requires recovery of contextual details, not just recognition of an item.
- It can involve a goal-to-context mapping rather than a context-to-goal mapping.
36. Q: How does the chapter relate autobiographical memory to medial PF function, and what are the evidential limitations?
A: Show answer
Retrieving autobiographical events activates medial PF regions, including medial area 9, alongside hippocampal and retrosplenial regions. The chapter places these activations within a medial hierarchy that progresses from monitoring actions and intentions toward more abstract representations of the self and one's personal history. However, whether medial PF activity is necessary for episodic retrieval is unresolved. Some patients with unilateral frontal lesions retain the ability to recall past events, although their narratives may be disorganized. A patient with a large bilateral medial frontal lesion had a marked autobiographical-memory impairment, but the lesion also damaged the fornix, complicating attribution. The authors therefore distinguish a possible role in generating goals from event memories from a stronger and unproven claim that medial PF cortex is the storage site or indispensable retrieval mechanism for episodic memories.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate the unilateral and bilateral lesion evidence and identify the anatomical confound that prevents a simple causal conclusion.
Key point:
- Medial PF activation accompanies autobiographical retrieval.
- Necessity for episodic recollection remains uncertain because lesion evidence is limited and sometimes confounded.
37. Q: How does imagining future events extend the role of episodic memory?
A: Show answer
People can combine elements of past experience to construct scenes and possible future events. The chapter reports that imagining future scenarios activates regions that overlap substantially with those involved in retrieving autobiographical memories. People with amnesia for past autobiographical events can also have difficulty imagining future events. The authors relate this overlap to mental time travel: memory supplies elements that can be recombined to represent possible situations that have not occurred. The PF cortex may help generate goals within these imagined contexts and compare potential outcomes. In this way, a person can simulate alternative courses of action before acting, rather than relying exclusively on physical trial and error. The chapter treats this as an important human elaboration, while recognizing that future imagination depends on a broader network including hippocampal and retrosplenial regions.
Study instruction:
- Construct a hypothetical future scene from remembered elements and identify which parts are retrieved, recombined, and used to evaluate a possible goal.
Key point:
- Future simulation draws on mechanisms related to autobiographical memory.
- Imagined contexts allow people to evaluate goals before acting.
38. Q: How does the chapter explain the relationship between reasoning tasks and abstract rules?
A: Show answer
Tasks such as Raven's progressive matrices require participants to infer a rule from relationships among several items and apply it to a new item. The context is the arrangement of the matrix, while the goal is the missing element that satisfies the inferred relation. More difficult problems may require the participant to identify a rule that applies across different transformations, rather than relying on surface similarity. The chapter reports activation in mid-lateral and ventral PF regions, with more rostral activation on some more difficult problems. The authors relate this to the PF cortex's established role in applying abstract rules: a rule allows a person to map a complex context to a suitable goal. They acknowledge that general intelligence and the precise mechanisms of fluid reasoning remain debated.
Study instruction:
- Solve a simple matrix problem and state the rule explicitly before selecting the missing item; then explain how the same rule transfers to a new set of stimuli.
Key point:
- Reasoning requires extracting and applying relations that go beyond individual stimulus features.
- PF rule-guided goal generation is the chapter's proposed bridge to fluid reasoning.
39. Q: Why does analogical reasoning require re-representing relationships?
A: Show answer
In an analogy such as "shoe is to foot as glove is to hand", the participant must identify the relationship within each pair and compare those relationships. The relevant representation is not just the objects or the first relation, but the relation between two relations. The chapter reports that explicit comparison of analogical relations recruits a more rostral lateral PF region near the boundary of area 46 and polar PF cortex, whereas supplying a missing word can recruit ventral PF systems involved in semantic retrieval. The authors propose that higher-order reasoning may depend on re-representation: a higher level represents the relationship that lower levels have already extracted. This account is offered as a constrained hypothesis tied to activation locations and hierarchical organization, not as a complete explanation of all reasoning.
Study instruction:
- Analyse an analogy in three stages: identify each pair's relation, compare the relations, and state what additional representation is required for that comparison.
Key point:
- Analogical reasoning requires comparing relations, not merely retrieving related words.
- Re-representation is the chapter's proposed mechanism for this higher-order comparison.
40. Q: How does the chapter relate moral and social rules to PF goal generation?
A: Show answer
Moral rules specify what a person ought or ought not to do, while social rules specify what behaviour is appropriate in a particular context. Both are abstract because they apply across many different actions and situations. The chapter proposes that social judgement involves identifying the present situation, generating a permissible or desirable goal, and maintaining that goal while acting. Imaging studies reviewed show medial PF and dorsal paracingulate activation when people consider deliberate violations of social norms or judge the mental states of others. The authors suggest that socialization gradually internalizes externally imposed consequences, so that people can regulate themselves by applying norms and evaluating their own conduct. They explicitly caution that social behaviour is more complex than the limited tasks reviewed and that the context-goal-outcome account does not explain every aspect of human social life.
Study instruction:
- Choose a social dilemma and identify the context, relevant norm, possible goals, expected outcomes, and the self-monitoring process required to follow the norm.
Key point:
- Moral and social rules can be treated as abstract constraints on goal selection.
- The chapter presents this as a useful framework, not a complete theory of social behaviour.
41. Q: What does the ultimatum game show about knowing a social rule versus implementing it?
A: Show answer
In the ultimatum game, one person proposes how to divide a sum and the other can accept or reject the offer as a whole. Imaging studies reviewed in the chapter report PF and insular activation when participants respond to unfair offers. Disruptive stimulation of right mid-lateral PF cortex increased acceptance of unfair offers even though participants continued to judge those offers as unfair. Patients with ventromedial and polar PF lesions could describe what others would regard as appropriate but behaved differently themselves. These findings suggest a distinction between knowing a norm and using it to guide a current choice. The chapter relates this to goal neglect: a person may retain the rule in long-term knowledge but fail to maintain or implement it when making a decision.
Study instruction:
- Separate a participant's verbal judgement that an offer is unfair from their actual accept-or-reject decision, and explain what a dissociation between them implies.
Key point:
- Norm knowledge does not guarantee norm-consistent behaviour.
- PF systems may be needed to maintain and apply a rule during a live choice.
42. Q: What is re-representation, and how does it help explain higher-order PF functions?
A: Show answer
Re-representation is the proposed process by which a higher level of a processing hierarchy represents information or operations already represented at a lower level. A first-order representation might encode a sensory feature or an action-outcome relation; a higher level can represent the judgement about that information, and a still higher level can represent the relation between such judgements. The chapter uses this idea to interpret reflective sensory awareness, self-evaluation, analogical reasoning, and moral self-regulation. It also proposes a caudal-to-rostral gradient: more rostral PF regions may re-represent information processed more caudally. The authors acknowledge a major risk: if re-representation is used loosely, it could be invoked to explain almost anything. They therefore argue that it must be tied to specific activation locations and the known organization of PF hierarchies.
Study instruction:
- Create a three-level example showing a sensory representation, a judgement about that representation, and a further judgement about the first judgement; explain how this differs from simple repetition.
Key point:
- Re-representation means representing an existing representation or relation at a higher level.
- The hypothesis must be constrained by anatomy and task-specific evidence to remain testable.
43. Q: How do instruction, imitation, and mental trial-and-error contribute to the chapter's account of error reduction?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts older learning systems that improve behaviour by accumulating feedback across repeated physical trials with primate PF mechanisms that can use a single event to generate and maintain a goal. Human learning adds further ways to avoid errors. Instruction allows a person to use knowledge acquired by someone else; imitation allows a learner to reproduce an effective action, especially when the model's intention is understood; and mental trial-and-error allows alternative plans and their likely outcomes to be evaluated in imagination. These methods can reduce the need for risky or wasteful physical experimentation. The authors propose that combining mental simulation with re-representation can produce insight, because people can compare alternative scenarios and abstract relations across domains. The claim is an evolutionary and functional proposal rather than a demonstration that humans can eliminate all errors.
Study instruction:
- For a practical problem, devise one solution learned by instruction, one by imitation, and one by mental simulation; compare the errors and risks each method could prevent.
Key point:
- Human PF elaborations support learning and planning before overt action.
- Error reduction can occur through knowledge transfer and imagined alternatives, not only repeated feedback.
Rows: 43
Table 10
Evaluating Theories of the Primate Prefrontal Cortex
1. Q: What is the central purpose of Chapter 10, “Conclusions,” in The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex?
A: Show answer
Chapter 10 evaluates the authors’ account of primate prefrontal (PF) cortex against alternative theories and against five tests introduced in the book: evolutionary history, connectional anatomy, functional specificity, explanatory generality, and falsifiability. It is not simply a recap of earlier chapters. It asks whether the proposed account explains why granular PF cortex evolved in primates, how its connections support its proposed functions, how it differs from other neural systems, whether it can accommodate the broad empirical literature, and what observations could prove it wrong. The chapter also considers how a primate learning advantage may have contributed to the human capacity for insight through re-representation, instruction, imitation, and mental trial-and-error.
Study instruction:
- Be able to state the five tests and explain why a theory of PF function must satisfy all of them rather than relying on a single kind of evidence.
Key point:
- Chapter 10 evaluates a unified evolutionary, anatomical, and functional theory.
- A useful theory must explain evidence and generate possible disconfirming tests.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- The Neurobiology of the Prefrontal Cortex: book overview
2. Q: What five aims does the book use to organize its theory of the prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
The authors aim to explain (1) what granular PF cortex allows primates to do more efficiently than their ancestors and other mammals, and what later expansion added in anthropoids; (2) how its axonal connections make those advantages possible; (3) what fundamental function the granular PF cortex performs and how that function differs from other brain systems; (4) how the proposal accounts for activations observed in human PF cortex during complex cognitive tasks; and (5) how the proposal differs from existing theories and what observations could refute it. These aims link evolutionary explanation to circuit-level mechanism, cognitive function, human evidence, and scientific testability. A proposal that addresses only executive performance or imaging activation would leave several of the aims unanswered.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the five aims from memory and map each to the kind of evidence needed to address it.
Key point:
- The theory connects evolutionary history, connectivity, function, human cognition, and falsifiability.
- No single experimental method can answer all five aims.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
3. Q: What is the authors’ central proposal about the fundamental function of granular prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
The authors propose that granular PF cortex provides primates with an enhanced learning and goal-generation system that uses information about current contexts and individual events to guide choices more rapidly and with fewer errors than ancestral, slower learning mechanisms alone. Its function is not described as a single generic act of “thinking.” Rather, different PF regions contribute distinct kinds of information and goal-related operations through their particular connections. Across regions, the granular PF cortex helps generate or select goals using sensory cues, internal states, learned outcomes, context, and abstract rules. The proposal is evolutionary: these abilities are argued to have improved foraging decisions in changing environments. It is the authors’ explanatory framework, not a claim that every aspect of cognition is exclusively prefrontal or that other animals lack learning and flexible behaviour.
Study instruction:
- Explain the proposal in two levels: the common learning advantage across granular PF cortex and the region-specific operations that contribute to it.
Key point:
- The proposed core contribution is faster, more accurate context-sensitive learning and goal generation.
- The proposal does not claim that non-primates lack all comparable abilities.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 8: Prefrontal cortex as a whole
4. Q: How does the authors’ evolutionary account distinguish early primates from later anthropoid primates?
A: Show answer
The account proposes a staged expansion. Early primates adapted to moving and foraging on fine branches, where visually cluttered surroundings made locating and evaluating food consequential. The caudal PF cortex is linked to learned, top-down search and attention, while orbital PF cortex is linked to learning the value of object choices from individual experiences and updating value in relation to current needs. Later, in anthropoid evolution, additional granular regions—including dorsal and ventral PF cortex and probably polar PF cortex—are associated with more complex foraging demands, larger bodies and ranges, competition, predation risk, and variable food availability. The authors connect these later additions with generating goals from recent events, visual or auditory contexts, and more abstract rules. These are evolutionary interpretations assembled from comparative evidence; they should be described as the authors’ hypothesis rather than as directly observed ancestral behaviour.
Study instruction:
- Draw a two-stage timeline and associate each proposed PF-region expansion with the ecological problem and behavioural advantage described in the chapter.
Key point:
- The proposed evolutionary sequence links new PF regions to changing foraging demands.
- The ecological narrative is an explanatory hypothesis supported indirectly by comparative evidence.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 2: Evolution of the primate prefrontal cortex
5. Q: What does the history test require of a theory of prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
The history test asks whether a theory is consistent with the evolutionary development of the PF cortex and explains why the relevant structures appeared in particular lineages. A theory should not treat the human PF cortex as if it emerged fully formed or assume that all mammals possess identical frontal organization. It should distinguish older agranular frontal regions from granular regions that the authors argue evolved in primates, and it should account for the later expansion of granular PF areas in anthropoids. The test also asks whether proposed functions make ecological and evolutionary sense. In the authors’ account, changes in visual foraging, food distribution, competition, and predation provide plausible selective pressures for improved search, evaluation, and goal generation. Evolutionary plausibility does not by itself prove a neural mechanism, so it must be combined with anatomical and experimental tests.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a candidate PF theory by asking what it predicts about species differences, ancestral structures, and the order in which cortical specializations evolved.
Key point:
- A theory should explain the evolutionary distribution of the structures it invokes.
- Evolutionary fit is necessary but not sufficient evidence for a functional theory.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 2: Evolution of the primate prefrontal cortex
6. Q: What is the anatomy test, and why is it central to the authors’ argument?
A: Show answer
The anatomy test asks whether a proposed PF function is made plausible by the region’s actual axonal connections. The authors argue that the PF cortex cannot be understood as one undifferentiated executive centre because its subregions receive and send different patterns of information. Medial PF connections with hippocampal, amygdalar, and medial premotor systems are related to internal signals, action choices, rules, and effort-sensitive valuation. Orbital PF connections with sensory cortices and the amygdala support conjunctions of information about specific outcomes and current biological value. Caudal PF connections with visual systems and oculomotor structures support learned, goal-directed attention. Dorsal and ventral PF regions draw on different combinations of contextual, sensory, outcome, and action-related information. A theory that labels all of these functions “control” without specifying the relevant inputs, outputs, and computations does not pass this test adequately.
Study instruction:
- For each major PF region, list its principal information sources, its output pathways, and the proposed behavioural operation those connections enable.
Key point:
- Functional claims should be constrained by connectional anatomy.
- Regional specialization is a core part of the proposed explanation.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 8: Prefrontal cortex as a whole
7. Q: What does the specificity test ask, and how does it differ from simply showing that the prefrontal cortex is active during a task?
A: Show answer
The specificity test asks what the PF cortex contributes that other brain areas do not contribute in the same way. Showing that PF activity increases during a difficult task establishes association, not a unique function. A stronger explanation identifies the information represented or operation performed by the PF region, explains how its connections support that operation, and predicts what should change when the region or its inputs are disrupted. The authors’ account distinguishes PF contributions such as generating goals from learned contexts, assigning credit to outcomes based on single events, and maintaining goal representations that resist interference from the functions of sensory, motor, parietal, limbic, and reinforcement-learning systems. Specificity need not mean that a function is performed by one isolated cortical patch; it means that the proposed contribution is defined precisely enough to distinguish it from the contributions of connected systems.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a broad claim such as “the PFC supports executive function” as a testable statement specifying the information, operation, and predicted impairment.
Key point:
- Activation alone does not establish functional specificity.
- A useful theory identifies the PF contribution relative to other neural systems.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Wood & Grafman: Human prefrontal cortex—processing and representational perspectives
8. Q: What is meant by the generality test for a prefrontal theory?
A: Show answer
The generality test asks whether a proposal can account for the broad range of findings about the PF cortex and its different subdivisions, rather than explaining only a narrow task or a favoured experimental paradigm. The chapter acknowledges that the literature includes working memory, planning, sequences, conditional learning, inhibition, abstraction, fast learning, monitoring, attention, episodic control, and complex problem solving. A theory may legitimately emphasize one core operation, but it must show how that operation and its regional implementations relate to the wider evidence. Generality does not mean that every PF region performs the same task or that one mechanism explains every cognitive phenomenon without qualification. It means the theory has sufficient scope to organize diverse results while preserving meaningful differences among regions and methods. The authors also acknowledge gaps in evidence for some medial and agranular areas, which limit the strength of a comprehensive claim.
Study instruction:
- Select findings from at least four different task families and assess whether the same theory explains them without erasing important distinctions.
Key point:
- Generality means broad explanatory coverage, not a vague claim that the PFC does everything.
- Missing evidence is a limitation, not proof that the theory is correct.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Friedman & Robbins: The role of prefrontal cortex in cognitive control and executive function
9. Q: What is the falsifiability test, and why must a theory expose itself to possible refutation?
A: Show answer
The falsifiability test asks whether a theory is stated precisely enough to generate observations that could count against it. A proposal that can explain every possible result after the fact is not a strong scientific account. The authors therefore discuss tasks and comparative predictions that could challenge their claims—for example, whether rodents can acquire particular learning sets or conditional mappings as quickly and accurately as monkeys under genuinely comparable conditions, and whether the relevant frontal lesions selectively impair rapid learning. They also identify broad classes of findings that would require revision, such as evidence that other mammals routinely show the same single-event learning advantages under well-controlled conditions. Falsifiability does not require a theory to be easy to disprove; it requires its claims to be sufficiently operationalized that evidence can discriminate between the proposal and alternatives.
Study instruction:
- For each major claim in the chapter, write one feasible result that would support it and one result that would force revision or rejection.
Key point:
- A scientific theory must specify what evidence could show it to be wrong.
- Testability is stronger than post hoc compatibility with findings.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Wood & Grafman: Human prefrontal cortex—processing and representational perspectives
10. Q: How does the chapter evaluate theories based on working memory?
A: Show answer
Working-memory accounts emphasize the temporary maintenance and use of information needed for ongoing cognition. They help explain why PF activity is observed when subjects retain information across delays, protect relevant representations from distraction, or use maintained information to guide later behaviour. The chapter treats working memory as an important body of evidence but questions whether it alone captures the full function of PF cortex. The authors’ proposal is broader: PF regions use information about goals, contexts, outcomes, and rules to generate or select behaviour, and some of these operations extend beyond holding items in mind. A theory of working memory must therefore specify what is maintained, how it is used, how regional anatomy supports it, and how it relates to learning from individual events and goal generation. Working memory is not synonymous with the whole of PF function.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish maintenance of information from using that information to select or generate a goal, and identify experiments that separate the two demands.
Key point:
- Working memory explains important PF phenomena but may not explain the entire functional range.
- Theoretical accounts should specify representational content and its use.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Baddeley: Working Memory—Theories, Models, and Controversies
11. Q: What is a cross-temporal contingency, and why is it relevant to prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
A cross-temporal contingency exists when information or an action at one point in time changes the significance or appropriate response at a later point. Many goal-directed behaviours require an animal or person to bridge a delay between a cue, an intermediate action, and an outcome. The PF cortex is implicated because it can maintain relevant information, connect temporally separated events, and allow current behaviour to be guided by a future consequence rather than only by an immediately present stimulus. The chapter discusses cross-temporal contingency as one of several theoretical approaches to monkey PF function, but it does not treat temporal bridging as a complete explanation of every PF specialization. A comprehensive account must also explain the content of the maintained information, the regional pathways involved, and how the system learns which events are causally or behaviourally relevant.
Study instruction:
- Design a simple delayed task and mark the cue, delay, action, and outcome; identify where a cross-temporal dependency occurs and what information must persist.
Key point:
- PF-dependent behaviour can link events separated in time.
- Temporal bridging alone does not specify all PF computations.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Friedman & Robbins: Cognitive control and executive function
12. Q: How do planning and sequence theories explain prefrontal activity, and what do they leave to be explained?
A: Show answer
Planning and sequence theories emphasize the organization of actions across multiple steps and the maintenance of an intended sequence until it is completed. They account for the need to represent future states, order actions, monitor progress, and adjust behaviour when circumstances change. The chapter includes planning and sequences among the theories evaluated using monkey evidence. Their limitation, if treated as complete explanations, is that “planning” can describe a behavioural outcome without specifying the neural information and computations that make planning possible. A stronger theory must explain how goals are selected, how context and outcomes shape them, why different PF regions contribute differently, and what distinguishes PF planning from sequence-related processing elsewhere in the brain. The authors’ account places planning within a wider system of context-sensitive goal generation and error reduction.
Study instruction:
- Compare a fixed motor sequence with a plan that must be revised after a changed cue or outcome; identify which demands require flexible goal selection.
Key point:
- Planning theories capture temporally organized behaviour.
- A mechanistic account must specify the representations and circuits that support flexible planning.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Badre & Desrochers: Hierarchical cognitive control and the frontal lobes
13. Q: What are temporally extended events, and how might prefrontal cortex help represent them?
A: Show answer
Temporally extended events are meaningful episodes whose identity depends on relationships among multiple components unfolding over time, rather than on one isolated stimulus or response. Examples include completing a multi-step task, pursuing a goal through intermediate actions, or interpreting a sequence of events as one structured episode. PF theories in this area propose that frontal systems help organize information across time so that behaviour remains coherent despite delays and intervening events. The chapter includes these accounts in its review of monkey-based theories. Their explanatory value depends on whether they specify how events are segmented, what information is retained, and how the current stage is linked to the overarching goal. The authors’ broader proposal can accommodate temporal structure while emphasizing that goals are generated from current context and learned relationships, not merely from sequence position.
Study instruction:
- Describe a multi-step everyday activity as an event structure and identify the cues that signal transitions between sub-events.
Key point:
- PF theories must explain how behaviour remains organized across extended episodes.
- Temporal structure is one component of a wider context-to-goal relationship.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Duncan: The Structure of Cognition—Attentional Episodes in Mind and Brain
14. Q: What does conditional learning contribute to theories of prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
Conditional learning requires an organism to select a response according to a cue or context, rather than applying one fixed response to every occurrence of a stimulus. The chapter considers conditional learning as a major category of monkey evidence. It is relevant because flexible behaviour depends on learning which action is appropriate under which conditions, and because a novel stimulus–response mapping may sometimes be acquired rapidly. The authors connect the ventral PF cortex with generating goals from visual or auditory contexts and applying abstract rules or strategies to new problems. A theory must distinguish simple stimulus–response association from learning a conditional mapping that depends on contextual information, and it should predict how learning speed changes when the relevant PF system is disrupted. The authors use rapid conditional learning as one potential test of their account of granular PF cortex.
Study instruction:
- Construct a two-cue/two-action example and explain why the cue determines the response; then specify how to test learning of a new mapping.
Key point:
- Conditional learning links context to the appropriate action.
- Fast acquisition of novel mappings is a proposed marker of granular PF contribution.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 7: Generating goals based on visual and auditory contexts
15. Q: How should behavioural inhibition and response inhibition be distinguished when evaluating prefrontal theories?
A: Show answer
Behavioural inhibition is a broad description of suppressing or withholding an action that would otherwise occur. Response inhibition is a more specific experimental or cognitive construct concerning stopping or preventing a particular response. Inhibitory control may involve several processes, including detecting conflict, maintaining a goal that conflicts with a prepotent response, changing a response rule, or stopping an action already initiated. The chapter lists inhibition-related theories among the approaches assessed against monkey data, but it cautions against treating one task label as a complete explanation of PF function. A theory should specify which form of inhibition is required, what information must be represented, and whether the relevant effect is specific to a PF region or depends on a wider network. The authors’ broader framework interprets some inhibitory effects as part of maintaining and generating context-appropriate goals that reduce errors.
Study instruction:
- Compare a task requiring withholding a response with one requiring switching to a new response, and state the distinct control demands.
Key point:
- “Inhibition” covers separable behavioural and cognitive operations.
- A task’s performance label does not by itself identify the underlying PF mechanism.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Friedman & Robbins: The role of prefrontal cortex in cognitive control and executive function
16. Q: Why are abstractions, categories, rules, and strategies important in the authors’ account?
A: Show answer
Abstractions allow behaviour to be guided by relationships that are not tied to one particular stimulus or one previously practised response. A category groups different instances according to a relevant shared property; a rule specifies how to respond under a defined condition; and a strategy organizes choices across a series of trials or situations. The chapter discusses these abilities as part of the evidence for PF function, particularly the capacity to transfer prior experience to a new problem. The authors associate ventral PF cortex with applying abstract rules and strategies based on visual or auditory context, while the wider granular PF system supports goal generation from context and recent events. The important distinction is between memorizing a specific response and acquiring a reusable structure that can guide behaviour when surface features change.
Study instruction:
- Invent a simple rule that applies to several different stimuli and explain how a transfer test would distinguish rule learning from rote memorization.
Key point:
- Abstract rules support transfer beyond the exact training examples.
- The proposed PF advantage includes generating goals from reusable contextual structures.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 7: Generating goals based on visual and auditory contexts
17. Q: What does “fast learning” mean in the chapter, and why is it more informative than simply measuring final accuracy?
A: Show answer
Fast learning concerns how quickly an organism acquires a useful mapping, discrimination, or strategy from limited experience. Final accuracy alone can conceal important differences: two groups may eventually perform equally well even though one group required many more trials and made many more errors. The authors argue that granular PF cortex gives primates an advantage in learning from single events or a small number of experiences, thereby reducing the costs of errors when conditions are changing. They distinguish this proposed advantage from the claim that other mammals cannot learn the same relationships at all. A rigorous test should measure trial-by-trial acquisition, control prior experience and motivation, and compare species under equivalent task demands. It should also distinguish rapid learning from a pre-existing bias or an easier stimulus–response arrangement.
Study instruction:
- Sketch learning curves for two hypothetical groups with equal asymptotic accuracy but different learning rates; explain why the number of early errors matters.
Key point:
- Learning speed and eventual performance are different outcome measures.
- The proposed primate advantage is often quantitative, not an all-or-none capacity difference.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 8: Prefrontal cortex as a whole
18. Q: How does hierarchical processing relate to the organization of prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
Hierarchical processing involves representing goals and subgoals at different levels, with higher-level context constraining which lower-level action or rule is appropriate. For example, a broad objective may determine a sequence of subgoals, while the current subgoal determines the immediate response. The chapter includes hierarchical processing among theories based on monkey evidence and later considers hierarchical or multi-level accounts in the wider theoretical landscape. These models help explain why more anterior or rostral PF regions may participate in maintaining abstract rules or higher-order goals, while more caudal regions are closer to concrete stimulus and response information. The authors’ own proposal also requires integration across different contextual and goal hierarchies. However, a hierarchy should not be inferred merely from an anterior-to-posterior activation gradient; it needs explicit behavioural manipulations and circuit-level evidence.
Study instruction:
- Represent a complex activity as a hierarchy of goal, subgoal, and action; identify what changes when the top-level goal is switched.
Key point:
- Hierarchical models explain how abstract goals constrain concrete actions.
- A neural hierarchy requires testable predictions, not just anatomical labels.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Badre & Desrochers: Hierarchical cognitive control and the frontal lobes
19. Q: What does integration mean in theories of prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
Integration refers to combining information from different sources or processing stages so that behaviour can be guided by a coherent representation of the current situation. Depending on the theory, this may involve sensory information, memory, goals, outcomes, internal states, or rules. The authors argue that the granular PF cortex is positioned to integrate information because its subregions receive diverse inputs and contribute to generating goals appropriate to current needs and context. They do not treat integration as an unspecified capacity to combine anything with anything. The relevant information and the proposed connections must be identified. Integration also does not mean that every PF region performs an identical operation: the pattern of inputs and outputs differs across medial, orbital, caudal, dorsal, ventral, and polar regions.
Study instruction:
- For a concrete decision, list the sensory, memory, motivational, and rule-based inputs required, then map them to the PF regions discussed in the book.
Key point:
- Integration is meaningful only when its inputs, outputs, and behavioural purpose are specified.
- Connectional differences support differentiated forms of integration.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 8: Prefrontal cortex as a whole
20. Q: What is adaptive coding, and how does it relate to general problem solving?
A: Show answer
Adaptive-coding accounts propose that PF neurons can represent different kinds of information depending on the current task demands, rather than having one permanently fixed stimulus meaning. This flexibility can support general problem solving because the system can prioritize the information currently needed to achieve a goal. The chapter considers adaptive coding and general problem solving among theories based on monkey evidence, and it later discusses domain-general accounts of human PF function. The authors see merit in broad, cross-domain explanations but argue that such generality should be grounded in a more fundamental account of how primate PF systems evolved to solve ecological problems and support rapid learning. Adaptive coding is therefore relevant, but it does not by itself specify why different PF subregions have distinct connectional patterns or how learning from individual events is achieved.
Study instruction:
- Compare the same stimulus used in two tasks with different goals; identify what a flexible neural code would need to represent in each task.
Key point:
- Adaptive coding emphasizes flexible representation according to current demands.
- Domain-general flexibility still requires an anatomical and evolutionary explanation.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Duncan: The multiple-demand system of the primate brain
21. Q: How does the chapter use animal learning theory to frame the prefrontal contribution?
A: Show answer
The authors contrast the proposed granular PF advantage with ancestral reinforcement-learning mechanisms that adjust behaviour gradually through accumulated experience. Reinforcement learning is valuable because outcomes strengthen or weaken behavioural tendencies over time, but gradual updating can be slow when environments change and may produce many errors before the best response is acquired. The proposed PF contribution augments rather than replaces this older system: it allows primates to use contextual information and single events to guide choices more quickly, including assigning an outcome to a particular choice or action. This is not a claim that reinforcement learning is absent from primates or that all learning can be reduced to one PF process. The theory instead describes an interaction between slower experience-averaging mechanisms and more rapid, context-sensitive PF learning and goal generation.
Study instruction:
- Contrast gradual reward-based updating with learning from a single informative event, and identify circumstances in which each strategy would be advantageous.
Key point:
- The PF system is proposed to augment ancestral reinforcement learning.
- Faster event-based updating can reduce costly errors when conditions change.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Soltani & Koechlin: Computational models of adaptive behavior and prefrontal cortex
22. Q: How do human theories of monitoring differ from theories based mainly on maintenance of information?
A: Show answer
Monitoring theories emphasize evaluating ongoing or completed behaviour by comparing expected events, intended goals, and actual outcomes. They address how a system detects errors, tracks progress, and adjusts future behaviour. Maintenance theories, by contrast, emphasize keeping information or goals active so they remain available for later use. The processes are related: monitoring requires a reference goal or expectation, while maintenance can preserve the information needed for comparison. The chapter treats monitoring and active maintenance as separate but related approaches in its review of human-subject theories. The authors’ wider proposal links goal maintenance and reduced interference to the ability to keep current goals available while remaining flexible about the means used to achieve them. Neither monitoring nor maintenance alone explains all the region-specific functions described in the book.
Study instruction:
- For an error-monitoring task, identify the maintained goal, the expected outcome, the observed outcome, and the adjustment that follows a mismatch.
Key point:
- Maintenance preserves relevant information; monitoring evaluates performance against it.
- Effective control often requires both operations to interact.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Friedman & Robbins: Cognitive control and executive function
23. Q: What is active maintenance of items in memory, and what are its limitations as a complete theory of the prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
Active maintenance is the sustained availability of information over a delay so it can guide subsequent decisions or actions. It is often studied using delayed-response tasks, in which a subject must remember a cue after it disappears. Such findings support an important role for PF circuits in keeping task-relevant information available. However, the chapter’s review makes clear that memory maintenance does not by itself explain all PF functions, including conditional learning, rapid acquisition of new mappings, abstract strategy use, and goal generation from context. A complete account must also explain what is maintained, how the representation is selected, how interference is resisted, and how maintained content is translated into behaviour. The authors’ theory includes goal maintenance as part of a broader mechanism rather than treating it as the sole fundamental function.
Study instruction:
- Compare a simple delayed-response task with a task requiring the subject to learn a new cue–action rule; identify which additional processes the second task requires.
Key point:
- Active maintenance is a key PF-related operation but not a full account of PF function.
- The content and behavioural use of maintained information matter.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Baddeley: Working Memory—Theories, Models, and Controversies
24. Q: What is executive control and the maintenance of goals as a theory of prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
This approach proposes that PF systems help keep goals and the means for achieving them active, allowing goal-relevant information to guide processing and behaviour even when habitual or immediately salient responses compete. It explains why PF disruption can lead to distractibility, inconsistent task performance, or actions that no longer serve the intended objective. The chapter includes goal maintenance among the theories based on human evidence. The authors’ account overlaps with this approach but seeks to specify how goals are generated from current contexts and recent events, how distinct PF regions contribute, and how the evolutionary history of granular PF cortex relates to the capacity. Goal maintenance is therefore an important explanatory component, but a full theory must also account for goal formation, learning, regional anatomy, and flexible changes in strategy.
Study instruction:
- Identify a task in which the correct goal conflicts with a habitual response; explain what must be maintained and what information triggers a goal update.
Key point:
- Goal maintenance helps protect intended behaviour from distraction and habit.
- Goal generation and goal maintenance are related but distinct theoretical problems.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Friedman & Robbins: The role of prefrontal cortex in cognitive control and executive function
25. Q: What is episodic control, and why does it matter for understanding complex behaviour?
A: Show answer
Episodic-control accounts emphasize the use of information about particular past events to guide current decisions, rather than relying only on a gradually accumulated average of prior outcomes. An episode can provide a specific relationship among a cue, an action, and an outcome, which may be especially useful when the environment is variable or when a single event is highly informative. The chapter reviews episodic control among human-subject theories and connects the broader importance of single events to the authors’ account of primate learning. The critical question is how event information is encoded, retrieved, assigned to the correct choice, and used to generate a current goal. Episodic control should not be conflated with autobiographical memory in its everyday sense; the relevant theoretical issue is how event-specific information influences adaptive control.
Study instruction:
- Create a scenario in which one unusual outcome should change the next decision, then contrast an event-specific response with a response based on the average of many past outcomes.
Key point:
- Particular events can carry information that is lost in slow averaging.
- Event-based control requires accurate links among context, choice, and outcome.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Soltani & Koechlin: Computational models of adaptive behavior and prefrontal cortex
26. Q: How does manipulation differ from simple maintenance in working-memory theories?
A: Show answer
Maintenance keeps information available, whereas manipulation transforms, reorders, combines, or otherwise operates on that information to meet a task requirement. For example, remembering a sequence is maintenance; reversing its order or calculating a new relationship among its elements requires manipulation. The chapter considers manipulation as a separate human PF theory because tasks that require transformation may recruit processes beyond passive retention. However, the presence of a manipulation demand does not automatically identify a unique PF operation: the task may also require attention, rule maintenance, response selection, or coordination with parietal and other cortical regions. A robust explanation must isolate the transformation being performed and show how PF connectivity supports it. The authors’ broader account asks how such operations contribute to generating goals from context and events.
Study instruction:
- Compare a task that asks a participant to retain three items with one that asks the participant to reorder them; list the extra cognitive operations.
Key point:
- Manipulation transforms maintained information.
- Task difficulty alone cannot establish which PF computation is responsible.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Baddeley: Working Memory—Theories, Models, and Controversies
27. Q: What role does attentional selection play in theories of human prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
Attentional selection concerns prioritizing particular information, objects, or actions for processing because they are relevant to the current goal. The chapter includes attentional selection among the human-subject theories it reviews. This approach helps explain why PF activity may bias processing toward goal-relevant inputs and away from distractions. The authors’ account places special emphasis on learned, goal-directed attention in caudal PF cortex, whose visual inputs and projections to oculomotor structures can support both overt attention through eye movements and covert attention without a gaze shift. Attentional selection is therefore not merely a generic increase in concentration; it involves selecting targets based on learned value and current behavioural goals. A full explanation should distinguish stimulus-driven attention from attention guided by learned goals and should identify the relevant cortical and subcortical pathways.
Study instruction:
- Compare a salient but irrelevant stimulus with a less salient learned target and explain how goal-directed attention should alter selection.
Key point:
- PF-linked attention can be guided by learned value and current goals.
- Overt and covert attention are related forms of target selection.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 5: Searching for goals
28. Q: What is attentive retrieval, and how is it different from retrieving information automatically?
A: Show answer
Attentive retrieval refers to retrieving information in a goal-directed way, with attention shaping which memory or representation becomes relevant to the current task. Automatic retrieval may be triggered by a familiar cue or well-learned association without much deliberate selection. Attentive retrieval becomes important when several memories or interpretations are possible and the subject must select the one appropriate to the current context. The chapter lists this approach among theories based on human subjects. It can be related to the authors’ broader proposal because current goals and context determine which information should guide behaviour. Nevertheless, a theory should specify whether the PF cortex initiates retrieval, biases other memory systems, maintains a retrieval cue, or evaluates the retrieved information; these are different mechanistic claims and should not be treated as interchangeable.
Study instruction:
- Construct an example in which the same cue could retrieve different information depending on the task goal, and identify the control requirement.
Key point:
- Retrieval can be selected according to current goals and context.
- A precise theory distinguishes retrieval initiation, biasing, maintenance, and evaluation.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Friedman & Robbins: The role of prefrontal cortex in cognitive control and executive function
29. Q: What are structured event knowledge and the supervisory attentional system, and how do they contribute to the theoretical debate?
A: Show answer
Structured event knowledge accounts emphasize stored representations of how familiar activities and events are organized, including typical sequences, roles, and expected transitions. A supervisory attentional system account emphasizes a control process that intervenes when routine action schemas are insufficient, competing, or inappropriate. Both approaches address how behaviour can remain organized while adapting to novelty or conflict. The chapter reviews them as part of the human theory landscape, but the authors’ central question is whether these accounts explain the evolutionary and connectional specificity of granular PF cortex. Event structures may describe what knowledge is available; supervisory control may describe when routine behaviour needs additional regulation. A comprehensive theory should identify the neural systems that store event knowledge, select among competing schemas, and generate a goal suited to the current context.
Study instruction:
- Analyse a familiar routine interrupted by an unexpected event; distinguish stored event structure from the control needed to revise the action sequence.
Key point:
- Event knowledge and supervisory control describe complementary aspects of adaptive behaviour.
- Their neural implementation and relationship to PF subregions must be specified.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Duncan: The Structure of Cognition—Attentional Episodes in Mind and Brain
30. Q: What do the multiple-demand system, global-workspace accounts, and general-intelligence approaches contribute to theories of prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
These approaches seek to explain why overlapping frontal and parietal regions are recruited across many demanding tasks. The multiple-demand account emphasizes flexible coordination of mental programs and the assembly of task-relevant information into successive attentional episodes. Global-workspace approaches emphasize broad availability of selected information to multiple processing systems. General-intelligence approaches examine the relationship between domain-general control demands and performance across diverse cognitive tasks. The chapter recognizes the value of cross-domain accounts but argues that they should be connected to a more fundamental explanation of how PF specializations evolved and how their connections support specific functions. Shared activation across tasks is evidence of common demands, but it does not prove that every task uses an identical computation or that all relevant activity originates in PF cortex alone.
Study instruction:
- Compare what each framework treats as the central explanatory unit: task coordination, global availability, or common cognitive capacity.
Key point:
- Domain-general frameworks explain commonalities across demanding tasks.
- Shared activation still requires a mechanistic and anatomical interpretation.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Duncan: The multiple-demand system of the primate brain
- Wood & Grafman: Human prefrontal cortex—processing and representational perspectives
31. Q: What is the difference between a processing theory and a representational theory of prefrontal function?
A: Show answer
A processing theory specifies operations performed by the PF cortex, such as selecting, manipulating, monitoring, or coordinating information that may be represented elsewhere. A representational theory specifies the kinds of information or knowledge represented and maintained in PF circuits. Some theories combine both perspectives. Wood and Grafman argue that representational accounts can encourage more precise specification of what is stored and therefore more direct hypothesis testing, while also noting that no single theory reviewed by them explained all available evidence without modification. The distinction is useful for evaluating the chapter’s proposal: it contains claims about what information PF regions use—goals, contexts, outcomes, rules—and about what they do with it—generate, select, and maintain goals. A strong theory should state both the representational content and the operations applied to it.
Study instruction:
- Take one PF theory and write one sentence specifying its representational claim and another specifying its processing claim.
Key point:
- Representational theories specify what information is encoded; processing theories specify what operations are performed.
- Many comprehensive accounts require both components.
Source links:
- Wood & Grafman: Human prefrontal cortex—processing and representational perspectives
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
32. Q: How does the authors’ proposal explain the relationship between region-specific functions and a general-purpose learning system?
A: Show answer
The authors argue that the PF cortex can support a broad learning advantage without every region performing the same operation. Its regions contribute different forms of information and control because their connections differ. Medial PF cortex is linked to internal signals and action/rule choices; orbital PF cortex to sensory information and outcome valuation; caudal PF cortex to learned visual search and attention; dorsal PF cortex to generating goals from recent events and resisting interference; and ventral PF cortex to goals based on visual or auditory context and abstract rules. When considered together, these region-specific functions allow a more general capacity for rapid, context-sensitive learning and goal generation. The generality is therefore an emergent property of an organized network, not evidence that anatomical distinctions are unimportant. The authors’ theory attempts to bridge localized connectivity with cross-domain behavioural advantages.
Study instruction:
- Create a region-by-region matrix showing each area’s information sources, proposed function, and contribution to the general learning advantage.
Key point:
- A general-purpose capacity can depend on differentiated regional components.
- Network-level unity does not imply anatomical uniformity.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 8: Prefrontal cortex as a whole
33. Q: What evidence would challenge the claim that primates have a distinctive advantage in rapid learning from single events?
A: Show answer
The claim would be challenged if non-primate mammals, tested under conditions genuinely comparable to those used with primates, acquired the same conditional mappings, discrimination learning sets, or strategy reversals in as few trials and with similar error rates. The authors emphasize that a valid comparison must control task design, sensory accessibility, response costs, training duration, motivation, and prior learning. A task that allows a rodent to use a simple spatial shortcut or a default response may not test the same cognitive capacity as a monkey task requiring a choice among non-food stimuli followed by a separate reward. Evidence from one unusual species might indicate convergent evolution; repeated findings across a diverse range of mammals would pose a more serious challenge to the proposed primate specialization. The prediction must therefore be evaluated through replicated, carefully matched experiments rather than by isolated claims of success or failure.
Study instruction:
- Design a fair cross-species experiment and list at least five variables that must be matched or controlled before comparing learning rates.
Key point:
- A species comparison is informative only when task demands are comparable.
- Replicated rapid-learning performance across many mammals would weaken the evolutionary claim.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 2: Evolution of the primate prefrontal cortex
34. Q: What proposed experiments could test whether monkeys have a learning capacity that rats do not show under comparable conditions?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes several challenging tests rather than relying only on familiar tasks. One proposal is to train monkeys on “repeat-stay” and “change-shift” strategies, then require them to reverse these into “repeat-shift” and “change-stay” strategies and repeatedly reverse again. Another is to use a cue to switch between the two strategy sets, testing whether animals can learn context-dependent strategy control across short blocks of trials. A further proposal combines conditional visuomotor mappings with object-in-place scene learning: subjects must choose an object based on a background context and then select the action associated with that object, learning new relationships within a restricted trial budget. Comparable versions for rats could use auditory backgrounds and odour choices. The critical outcome is not simply whether an animal eventually succeeds, but whether it learns the integrated relationships quickly and reliably under equivalent conditions.
Study instruction:
- Explain what each proposed task tests, which relationships must be learned, and what pattern of monkey–rat results would support or challenge the theory.
Key point:
- Strategy reversal and task combination test flexible transfer beyond simple associations.
- Trial limits and matched task demands are essential to interpreting species differences.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
35. Q: Why does the chapter warn against interpreting a failure to learn as proof of a species-wide cognitive limitation?
A: Show answer
Failure may result from inadequate training, insufficient trials, poorly chosen stimuli, motivational differences, or task designs that favour one response strategy. The authors illustrate this with a reversed-reward-contingency task: an earlier report concluded that monkeys could not solve the problem, but later work showed that monkeys could learn it when given substantially more experience. The chapter notes that the successful animals required a large number of trials, demonstrating that a negative result after limited training can be misleading. A fair test must distinguish inability from insufficient opportunity, poor task design, or an unintended shortcut. This caution applies especially to comparative neuroscience, where sensory and motor differences between species can alter task difficulty independently of the cognitive operation being investigated.
Study instruction:
- For a hypothetical negative animal-learning result, generate alternative explanations and specify the additional controls or training needed before concluding that a capacity is absent.
Key point:
- A failed task is not automatically evidence of a missing cognitive capacity.
- Training adequacy and task validity must be evaluated before interpreting species differences.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
36. Q: What limitations of the authors’ own theory does Chapter 10 acknowledge?
A: Show answer
The authors acknowledge that evidence is sparse for some PF territories, particularly medial areas 9 and 10 in monkeys and several agranular PF regions. A theory cannot be fully evaluated where relevant experiments have not been conducted. They also recognize that their account must remain open to findings that reveal an important PF result it cannot explain or a PF subdivision it fails to cover. Comparative claims require caution because lineages have evolved separately for tens of millions of years; even homologous regions may differ in connectivity, cellular properties, imaging responses, or lesion effects. The authors also allow that some non-primate species may have evolved similar abilities independently. These qualifications matter because they distinguish the theory’s current explanatory reach from claims that are already conclusively established.
Study instruction:
- Separate limitations caused by missing data, limitations caused by cross-species comparability, and limitations that would count as direct counterevidence.
Key point:
- The authors explicitly acknowledge gaps and possible counterexamples.
- Homology does not guarantee identical function or experimental effects across species.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
37. Q: How does the chapter connect the evolution of granular prefrontal cortex to the origin of human insight?
A: Show answer
The authors propose that the primate PF system first improved the ability to learn from individual events and generate context-appropriate goals, thereby reducing errors in real-world behaviour. In humans, they suggest that re-representation extended this advantage: people can represent their own intentions, represent the intentions of others, and mentally test possible actions before acting. Re-representation can support imitation because an observer can infer not only what another person did but also the goal the action served. It can also support instruction because one person can communicate information about goals and mental states to another. Mental trial-and-error allows people to evaluate possible solutions internally, potentially avoiding errors that would otherwise occur through physical experimentation. The authors present this as an evolutionary extension of earlier primate capacities, not as proof that all insight can be reduced to one cortical mechanism.
Study instruction:
- Explain the proposed progression from event-based learning to goal representation, imitation, instruction, and mental trial-and-error.
Key point:
- Human insight is presented as an elaboration of primate goal-generation and learning capacities.
- Re-representation enables internal simulation and social transmission of goal information.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 9: Human prefrontal cortex
38. Q: What is re-representation, and how does it support mental trial-and-error?
A: Show answer
Re-representation is the capacity to represent information that is already represented—for example, to represent one’s own intention as an object of thought rather than simply acting on it. In the chapter’s account, this makes it possible to inspect and compare possible intentions, simulate the consequences of alternative actions, and revise a plan before carrying it out. Mental trial-and-error is therefore an internal form of testing: a person can explore candidate solutions without incurring the full external costs of each attempt. The authors link this capacity to the human extension of primate PF goal-generation mechanisms. The concept should not be treated as a simple synonym for working memory or imagination; its distinctive claim is that representations can themselves become the content of further representations and evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Describe a problem-solving example in which you mentally represent a possible action, evaluate its likely outcome, and revise the plan before acting.
Key point:
- Re-representation allows thought to operate on represented intentions and goals.
- Mental trial-and-error can reduce the need for costly physical experimentation.
Source links:
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 10: Conclusions
- Passingham & Wise, Chapter 9: Human prefrontal cortex
